• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

A.21. Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages given)
As Small Island Developing States (SIDS) in the Pacific, the Cook Islands, Nauru, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands (RMI), and Tuvalu are at the forefront of climate change. They are increasingly under threat from rising sea levels, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as hurricanes and tropical cyclones, ocean acidification and degeneration, degradation of protective coral reefs, unpredictable and more extreme weather patterns, and loss of biodiversity. The vulnerability to these impacts is exacerbated by the fact that sea level rise in Tuvalu was twice the global average (3.9 ± 0.4 m² y⁻¹) from 1971-2014. The continuity of relative sea level rise, increases in extreme waves, tropical cyclones and storms, and changes to ocean conditions are expected to continue into the future. These climate change impacts increasingly threaten the ecosystems on which Pacific island populations rely for their livelihoods.

The high vulnerability to climate change impacts and climate-related hazards of the five Programme countries arises from their geography, the exposure of their populations and their lack of resilience to shocks and stresses. The majority of the population lives in coastal areas that are vulnerable to flooding and resource loss. Their vulnerability is compounded by socioeconomic factors, particularly the reliance of their small economies on very sensitive sectors such as agriculture, fishing, fisheries and tourism.

The IPCC Special Report on Global Warming of 1.5°C indicates that SIDS are at a higher relative risk of adverse consequences due to global warming of 1.5°C, including increased climate-related risks to health, livelihoods, food security, water supply, human security and economic growth. Furthermore,

The World Risk Index 2018 unequivocally states that Pacific island countries are unable to reduce their disaster risk without external support and notes that disaster risk—the risk that an extreme natural event will lead to disaster—is at its highest in Oceania. All five countries have assets, infrastructure and crops at risk of damage or destruction by disasters. The most vulnerable sectors include agriculture (45%), buildings at risk contributing the majority of this total (86.5%), infrastructure (13.2%) and cash crops (0.3%). Natural hazards that pose a significant threat to Pacific island countries are earthquakes, tropical cyclones. Expected average annual losses (AAL) per capita (USD$1000) for tropical cyclones alone in the last five years is $458 (Nauru), 279 (PNG), 360 (Solomon Islands), 328 (Tonga), and 336 (Tuvalu). Deaths caused by tropical cyclones and other natural disasters are often underestimated. Loss, damage, deaths and disability-affected lives resulting from prolonged drought and other extreme weather events are also underreported.

As the climate changes and climate extremes become more frequent or intense, the Cook Islands, Nauru, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu require reliable, timely and understandable information and early warning on their local climate conditions to enable them to adapt effectively and prepare for disasters that may have long-term effects on climate change impacts. However, the combination of vast ocean areas, large numbers of dispersed islands, limited land mass, low-lying coastal communities and other barriers makes it very challenging for the five Pacific countries to put in place effective early warning systems (EWS) and information and multi-hazard early warning systems (MHEWS).

As the Pacific Island Countries enter their cyclone season, with El Niño or monsoon seasons, the potential for climate change impacts on health is increasing. The UNDRR Asia COVID-19 Brief emphasizes 'the need for countries to focus on a multi-hazard integrated approach to disaster risk reduction and management'. The pandemic has also highlighted accelerated disaster risk reduction across sectors.' The dual challenge of climate change and a global pandemic has increased the importance of early warning systems, including multi-hazard early warning systems. The Global Ocean Observing System (GOOS) asserts that 'Despite its significant impacts on the ocean observing system, the Covid-19 crisis can also be an opportunity for us to better understand how we can use our existing and emerging technologies to provide observations in concurrence with disaster resilience building will be a major focus of the Programme.

The countries' National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHSs) operate at WMO's Basiccategory level. The NMHSs are responsible for providing weather, climate and hydrological services to their countries, but lack capacity for comprehensive climate and oceans observations, impact-based forecasting and early warning systems. Although efforts have been made to improve the quality of climate data collected through the current observation networks, there are still many gaps—particularly in relation to ocean areas and outer islands. Among other challenges, the NMHSs face limited resources, including financial constraints.

The Feasibility Study (Annex 2) provides detail on the staffing levels, qualifications and current capacity, and equipment of the NMHSs.

The Pacific Island Meteorological Strategy 2017–2026 summarised the constraints affecting the NMHSs for Pacific SIDS—poor infrastructure, staffing constraints, limited communication channels to

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 2 OF 167

The barriers preventing effective climate and ocean information services and MHEWS in the five countries include: i) lack of legislative, regulatory and policy frameworks for climate services; ii) inadequate institutional capacity to develop, implement and maintain climate services; iii) limited understanding and use of climate and ocean information within each country; and iv) limited regional cooperation. The Programme will address these barriers by: a) strengthening the national MHEWSs so that they do not reflect the current and potential value of climate services to disaster risk reduction and effective adaptation to climate change in island communities. Lack of resources also impedes NMRIs from demonstrating that they can provide information products.

To address the aforementioned barriers, the proposed Programme will facilitate the development of integrated climate and ocean information services and people-centric MHEWS in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, Samoa and Tuvalu. This will be achieved through key related components – the “Programme Results”:

* **1. Strengthening demand for and use of improved climate and ocean services and products covering coastal and marine areas:** The Programme will increase the capacity for climate analysis, strategic policies and mainstreaming of climate risk knowledge to underpin a sustainable business delivery model for climate services.
* **2. Strengthening observation, monitoring, modelling and prediction of climate and its impacts on ocean areas and islands:** This will transform the five NMRIs from VMMO Basic (Category 1) to Essential (Category 2) level; meet the surface-based standards of the Global Basic Ocean Observing System (GOOS); establish a Climate Information Management System (CIMS), and support impact-Based Forecasting – with a focus on building capacity for climate services.
* **3. Improved community preparedness, response capabilities and resilience to climate risks:** This will establish last-mile delivery of early warnings and early action in island communities through the development of an interactive ICT platform and harmonised data management system; and build response capacities. It will also introduce Forecast-Based Financing to facilitate the shift from traditional reactive responses to pre-event early action.
* **4. Enhancing regional collaboration, information sharing and cooperation for climate services and MHEWS:** This will optimise synergies among the Programme countries; establish an interactive ICT platform and harmonised data management system; institute joint learning and training through WMO and other partners; and support the development of a Pacific Climate Change Adaptation Framework for implementation.
* **The Programme Results will address the five priority areas for action identified in the Pacific Island Meteorological Services Strategy 2017-2026 as follows:** i) Improved weather services and establishment of ocean weather services – Results 1, 2 and 3; ii) Disaster risk reduction – Results 1, 2, and 3); iii) Improved climate and hydrological services – Results 2 and 4; iv) Integrated observing and communication systems for climate services – Results 2 and 4; v) Climate information management system (CIMS) – Results 1 and 4. The Programme proposes to contribute to the establishment of more advanced NMRIs in the five countries, with a focus on improving their capacity as early warning centres as an impact-based forecasting for use by island communities, disaster management actors and climate-sensitive sectors such as subsistence farming, fisheries and tourism. The Programme will also directly support Goal 2 of the Pacific Climate Change Adaptation Framework for Resilient Development in the Pacific and provide enabling environment for Goal 2.
* **The Programme Results are based on the pillars of Global Frameworks for Climate Services (GFCS), a set of four elements: i) Disaster Risk Reduction; ii) Detection; iii) Forecasting; iv) Communication. The Programme is aligned to the four elements of the checklist for Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems (MHEWS) prepared by the partners of the International Network for MHEWS:** i) Disaster Risk Knowledge; ii) Detection;


---

monitoring analysis and forecasting of the hazards and possible consequences; (ii) warning dissemination and communication; and (iv)) Preparedness and response capabilities. Furthermore, the Programme will directly contribute to the attainment of selected targets and indicators of the Paris Agreement on Climate Change, including those related to climate change adaptation and Disaster Risk Reduction.
* The Programme interventions aim to shift the paradigm away from the current “business as usual”, towards a more proactive and coordinated use of insufficient climate data and information, towards a new paradigm in which accurate, timely and actionable climate information is used in climate-resilient policy planning and implementation. This will enable Pacific Island countries to better prepare for and mitigate the impacts of climate-related hazards. The Programme will enable sectors and island communities at the “last mile” in five Programme countries to adapt to increasing climate variability and change by providing them with access to climate information, tools and services that are timely, relevant and actionable.
The Programme will facilitate that (i) Governments implement policies, plans and actions based on climate information; (ii) Climate information is used for decision-making at all levels; and (iii) early warning systems are in place and sustained in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands and Samoa. The Programme will also support the development of a regional system using improved climate information and risk knowledge. This transformation to increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the populations in the five Pacific SIDS to climate change threats is expected to reduce the vulnerability of these countries to increased frequency and intensity of climate-induced disasters, such as tropical cyclones, by 15–30%.
At the request of the National Designated Authorities of the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu, the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) has been mandated to implement this Programme. Each country has also designated a national Executive Unit (EU), who will further sub-cast the responsibilities for the implementation of the Programme in their respective countries.
Reduction implementation will be further supported by key Regional Technical Partners, including the Pacific Regional Environment Programme (SPREP); the Pacific Community (SPC); the Australian Bureau of Meteorology and Climate Services (BOM); the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP); the United States National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA); the University of Hawaii; the World Meteorological Organization (WMO); the Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies (IFRC), APEC Climate Center (APCC); as well as UNEP.
This Programme has been developed as a direct response to requests from the partner countries, the regional technical partners and other stakeholders for support in addressing climate change and its impacts on climate change, as emphasised by Pacific Leaders, most recently at the 50th Pacific Islands Forum in August 2019. The Kainai Declaration calls for “the international community to immediately commit itself to providing the necessary resources and prioritising the development of a better understanding of risk and vulnerability, including through support for modelling and risk mapping capabilities” (Pacific Islands Forum, 2017). This Programme will provide information on climate change-related economic and infrastructure impacts, and capacity building support for evidence-based decision-making and project development. The Pacific Islands Meteorology Strategy 2017–2035 was endorsed by the Pacific Meteorological Council (PMC), which fulfills a crucial convening and coordination role in the region, at its 5th biennial meeting in August 2019.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Climate Change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

Pacific Small Island Developing States (SDS) are among the world's most vulnerable countries to climate change, particularly the many atoll countries whose highest elevation is two or three metres above mean sea level. The region has a high exposure to natural hazards such as cyclones, flooding, drought and sea-level rise; vulnerability as dependent on infrastructure, nutrition, living conditions and economic circumstances; capacity for adaptation as determined by preparedness, prepositioning of early warning measures, access to health care, social and material security; and adaptive capacities with respect to impeding natural resource-based changes in the environment.

This document provides an overview of the region's climate-related risks (including rainfall variability) and resilience. The risks are increasing due to challenges from common syndromes such as ocean acidification and deoxygenation, degradation of sheltering coral reefs, unpredictable and more extreme rainfall, rising sea levels, and increased frequency of tropical cyclones and droughts. These risks are particularly acute in the entire Pacific region. The state of ocean ecosystems is especially critical to the well-being of island populations. Many islands have limited freshwater resources, which are increasingly threatened by saltwater intrusion into freshwater resources and agricultural land, as well as tropical cyclones and severe droughts. They are remote from markets and have small populations and narrow resource bases. Their economic vulnerability is high due to their dependence on tourism and remittances, and they are highly exposed financially and human resources.

The pressures specific to each country are detailed in the Feasibility Study (Annex A).

## Regional climate overview

The tropical climate of the southern Pacific is influenced by three large-scale atmospheric features: the South Pacific Convergence Zone (SPCZ), the Inter Tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ), and the Western Pacific Monsoon (WPM). These features represent expansive bands of large-scale wind convergence and high rainfall, which are driven by differences in temperature between land and sea. They interact with ocean currents, ocean nutrients and other environmental aspects. The interplay between these climate drivers creates a complex system that is highly sensitive to changes in the global climate.

In the South Pacific region (Cook Islands, Niue and Tuvalu), rainfall variability is mostly linked to the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) or the Interdecadal Pacific Oscillation (IPO) and is directly attributable to shifts in the SPCZ. In the Pacific islands north of the equator (Palau and RMI), rainfall variability is linked to ENSO, the WPM and the ITCZ.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 7 OF 167

B


The dominant causes of interannual climate variability in the Pacific is El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO). ENSO is a natural climate cycle with two extreme phases, El Niño and La Niña. The extent and timing of the ENSO event can have significant impacts on weather patterns across the globe. These events are associated with sea levels and coral bleaching. El Niño and La Niña events have distinct impacts on rainfall and can cause large-scale shifts in rainfall patterns due to changes in surface temperatures and winds.

Table 1 provides a summary of the impacts of ENSO for five Programme countries.


| Country | Region | El Niño | Extreme El Niño | La Niña |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Cook Islands | North | Very dry | More frequent and intense droughts | Dry |
| South | Very dry | More frequent and intense droughts | Wet |
| Nauru | Dry | Very dry | More frequent and intense droughts | Wet |
| Palau | Dry | Lower than normal sea level | Higher than normal sea level |
| Marshall Islands | North | Lower than normal sea level | Lower than normal sea level | No consistent impact on rainfall |

*Australian Bureau of Meteorology and CSIRO. 2011. Climate Change in the Pacific: Scientific Assessment and New Research, Volume 1: Synthesis Report.*  
*Australian Bureau of Meteorology and SPC/FT. 2015. Climate in the Pacific: A regional summary of new science and management tools.*

## Table 1. Summary of the impacts of El Niño and La Niña during November to April in each reporting cycle (CO2 emissions)

An extreme El Niño event is defined by unusually high rainfall over the eastern equatorial Pacific Ocean that causes three consecutive months with a positive sea surface temperature anomaly. The extreme El Niño events tend to occur every 10 years, but there have been significant impacts on ecosystems, agriculture, tropical cyclones, droughts, bushfires, floods and other external weather effects.

The frequency of extreme El Niño events is projected to double to one event every 10 years under global warming. The frequency of La Niña events will decrease from two per decade in the past to less than once per decade with global warming. Future El Niño events are expected to be warmer than those experienced in the past, with associated changes in weather patterns.

### State of the climate

#### Temperature
At the regional level, annual mean temperature increased at a rate of 0.14 °C per decade in the period 1951 – 2015, with rates of warming for the same date of December–February, March–May and September–November periods being similar to those observed over the entire year. The rate of warming was highest during El Niño years. Further analysis suggests that at a given location, past extreme El Niño impacts will repeat more frequently with global warming.

Future El Niño events are expected to be warmer than those experienced in the past, associated changes in weather patterns.

The annual number of warm nights and warm days has increased (2.28 % per decade and 2.07 % per decade, respectively) and the number of cold nights and cold days has decreased (1.76 % per decade and 1.70 % per decade, respectively). The WDI increased by 3.97 days per decade and CSD3 decreased by 3.20 days per decade.

#### Table 2: Linear trends by rank-Kendall's rank correlation method in annual extreme temperature indices for the five Programme countries and the period 1951–2015. The trend values for each variable are shaded in pink (having effect) with the largest values shaded most strongly.

Bold italics indicate that trends are significant at the 5% level. Asterisks (*) indicate less certain source. [Source: Pacific Climate Change Data Group]

The trends in annual extreme temperature indices, apart from the diurnal temperature range, all indicate a warming climate. Focusing on the statistically significant trends for the five Programme countries, the most noteworthy changes are:

- Increase in daily maximum temperatures (TXx and/or TXn) in the Cook Islands, Palau and Tuvalu.
  - With the greatest increases (per decade) observed in Cook Islands (0.38°C increase in highest maximum temperature per decade).
  
- Increase in daily minimum temperatures (TNx and/or TNn) in the Cook Islands, Palaus and Marshall Islands, with the greatest increases (per decade) observed in Cook Islands (0.30°C increase in lowest minimum temperature per decade).

- Increase in the number of days greater than or equal to 30°C in the Cook Islands, Palau and Marshall Islands; with the greatest increases observed in the Marshall Islands – 13 days per decade.

- Decrease in the number of cool nights (minimum temperature < 10th percentile) in the Cook Islands and Palaus.
  
- Increase in the number of warm wet days (maximum temperature > 90th percentile) in the Marshall Islands, Cook Islands and Palau; 

- Increase in the number of warm nights (minimum temperature > 90th percentile) in the Marshall Islands, Cook Islands and Palau;

- Increase in the number of cooling degree-days (mean temperature = 18°C) in the Marshall Islands, Cook Islands and Palau; with the largest trends (per decade) being observed in the Marshall Islands (37 days per decade) and Palau (29 days).

Rainfall:
Annual rainfall trend analyses for 1981–2011 (Figure 4) indicate wetter conditions in the regions covering the Southern Cook Islands and Niue. In the North Pacific, it has become wetter west of 165°E (Palau). Northeast of the South-Pacific Convergence Zone and in the central tropical Pacific east of about 180°E it has become drier (RMI and Tuvalu).

Although the vast majority of trends in extreme precipitation indices for the five Programme countries are not statistically significant at the 5% level, the general impression is one of dry climate at these tropical island locations. This is particularly evident on Nauru where several of its indices indicate increasing extreme rainfall consistent with a trend towards a wetter central Pacific.

The only statistically significant trends were observed at Majuro, Marshall Islands, as summarised below:

- Decrease in the number of consecutive dry days, possibly indicating an increase in the occurrence of dry spells.

This indicates that South Tarawa (South Tarawa Creek and South Tarawa Rain gauge) is experiencing less frequent but more intense rainfall; Nauru is experiencing more frequent but less intense rainfall; Palau and Majuro are experiencing generally more frequent but less intense rainfall, while trends are inconsistent.

# Sea level

Sea level varies spatially across the Pacific by up to one meter along the equator; the sea level in the west is about two meters higher than that of the east. Over a year, sea level can vary by more than 20 cm, mainly due to ENSO. During El Niño events, weakened trade winds reduce sea level in the western tropical Pacific and increase sea level in the east. During La Niña, strengthened trade winds cause higher than normal sea levels in the western tropical Pacific and lower than normal sea levels in the eastern tropical Pacific – including the northern Cook Islands, Palau, southern RMI and Tuvalu – are most significantly affected by sea-level rise.

The IPCC Fifth Assessment Report notes that "Rates of sea level rise over broad regions can be several times larger or smaller than the global mean sea level rise for periods of several decades, due to fluctuations in ocean currents. The rate of sea level rise is also highly variable on a local scale and may be three times larger than the global mean, while those for much of the Eastern Pacific are near zero or negative."

The regional distribution of sea-level rise is important because it is the regional or local sea-level change and not just the global mean that determines coastal flooding. The causes of extreme sea level can consist of the effect of surges, high tides and wind waves. From 1993 to 2009, both the altimeter and in-situ data indicate a higher than global average rate of rise in the western Pacific.


---
**Page 19**
---

but, because of the strong influence of ENSO and decadal variability on sea level in the region, this higher rate of rise may not necessarily representative of a longer time span.

Over a longer time period, Church et al. studied the tide gauge records in the tropical Pacific region and found that the average rate of relative sea-level rise (relative to land) – and corrected for glacial isostatic adjustment and other effects – was about 1.7 ± 0.2 mm/year between ~1850–1960. The most recent estimate of the global mean sea-level rise was obtained for Funafuti (Tuvalu) at z ± 1 mm/yr. For Majuro (RMI), the relative reconstructed trend was 2.3 mm/yr.

Sea surface temperature

Sea-surface temperatures in the tropical Pacific have generally warmed since 1950, which has been partly attributed to anthropogenic global warming. However, temperature variations associated with Interdecadal Pacific Oscillation (IPO)/Pacific Decadal Oscillation (PDO) also substantially influence the background trend. The PDO is a long-term oscillation of sea-surface temperatures in the North Pacific Ocean, with temperatures exceeding 29.5 °C having increased by 400 – 600 %.* The extent to which shifts in IPO/PDO indices are predictable on decadal timescales is the subject of ongoing research.

### Ocean acidification

Ocean acidification is measured based on the ocean aragonite saturation state. Aragonite is the most common form of calcium carbonate found in tropical reefs and is used to build marine shells and skeletons – including for coral growth. Coral can only grow when the water temperature, salinity, and pH are within a range that allows it to grow at an aragonite saturation state above 4; below 3, the conditions to grow coral are critically limited.


---
**Page 21**
---

The IPCC Special Report on the Ocean and Cryosphere in a Changing Climate (2019) reported that, since the start of the industrial era (around 1750), oceanic uptake of carbon dioxide has resulted in acidification of the ocean. The pH of ocean surface water has decreased by 0.1 (high confidence), corresponding to a 26% increase in hydrogen ion concentration and a 30% decrease in carbonate ion concentration, globally and regionally.

Based on data collected in the Pacific region as part of the Joint Global Ocean Flux Study / World Ocean Circulation Experiment CO₂ survey, estimates have been made of oceanic aragonite saturation state. In the pre-industrial era, "saturation state values were above 4 throughout most of the sub-tropical and tropical Pacific (including the western Pacific). Since then, there has been a general decline in oceanic aragonite saturation state, with values slightly above 4 only found in the region of the South Equatorial Current and in the west Pacific". Ocean aragonite saturation state has continued to decline, with only the surface waters off Hawaii remaining above this threshold – around Cook Island. Nauru and Tuvalu – with aragonite saturation states at or slightly above values of 4.

### High impact events

#### Tropical cyclones
The tropical cyclone activities are situated in the area of the Pacific regularly subject to tropical storms. Tropical cyclones are the most significant extreme events in much of the Pacific, and often cause heavy rain, strong winds, storm surges and large waves. ENSO plays a significant role in influencing tropical cyclone risk throughout the region. The El Niño phase is associated with an increase in tropical cyclone activity across the entire Pacific, close to Australia, while the region of greatest cyclone occurrence is found further east during the La Niña phase.

There are no significant trends in the overall number of tropical cyclones, or in the intensity of tropical cyclones, in the South Pacific Ocean over the period 1981 – 2007. However, during the El Niño phase, cyclones occurred with greater frequency and intensity than average across the entire region. During La Niña conditions, there was a "slight decrease in the total number of cyclones, with little change in the numbers of the most intense"; however, the record is too short to determine if the decrease is a trend or due to natural variability.

The Pacific Climate Change Science Program (PCCSP) has identified that climate change will increase the possibility of local floods.13

Between 1920 and 2006, the Cook Islands witnessed 148 tropical cyclones "between 1945 and 2013, Palau experienced between 70 and 100, and between 1989 and 2010, 63 tropical cyclones passed within 400 km of the Marshall Islands. The Marshall Islands are less affected by tropical cyclones, but are subject to major storms once every 4-7 years.

The PCCSP has identified that climate change will increase the frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones in the region.13 Major typhoons since 1972, one of the most recent was the 2015 Tropical Cyclone Pam, which reached

### Drought

Drought is a recurrent extreme climate event in the Pacific islands, and there is a perception among Pacific island residents that the frequency and magnitude of drought has increased, particularly in the last couple of decades. The trend analysis shows that the frequency, duration and magnitude for the Pacific islands were greater during 1981 – 2010 than during 1951 – 1980. The trend is non-linear with a strong increase from 1976 to 1983 followed by a decrease until 1994 and then an increase over the period 1951 – 2010. A strong lagged relationship in the year after the El Niño onset was found for locations southwest of the SPCZ and north of the ITCC, which includes all five Programme countries.[6]

Climate-related losses and damage

Damage costs of climate change are significant for SIDS in general and for the Programme countries in particular. The Cook Islands, Niue, Palau and RMI are in the top 20 countries experiencing the highest average annual loss per capita from natural disasters (see Table 1). Cyclones are a major driver of these losses; however, financial losses in the Pacific region is tropical cyclones. However, it should be noted that data on both cyclones and other causes of death and damage are patchy and incomplete. Climate studies show that predicted increases in sea level will have significant impacts on coastal areas, particularly for low-lying islands with high rainfall.

All five Programme countries have assets, infrastructure and crops at risk of being damaged from natural disasters with total replacement costs of over USD$ 6 billion. Buildings at risk contribute the majority of this total (96%). Infrastructure such major ports, airports, power plants, bridges and roads (13.2%) and cash on hand (0.8%) are also at risk. The water supply infrastructure is particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts as infrastructure such as power, telecommunications, sewers and water lines are within 30 m of the high water line and are therefore particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. In Tuvalu, the King Tides in 2013 resulted in flooding of the main island of Funafuti. The capital city of Nukufetau is also at risk from sea level rise. The island of Funafuti is particularly threatened from tropical cyclones and storm surges and it is in Funafuti that most of Tuvalu's infrastructure is located.

Expected average annual losses (AAI) per capita (USD$2015) for tropical cyclones alone in the five Programme countries are estimated at: 537 (Cook Islands), 2122 (Niue), 208 (Palau), 119 (RMI) and 336 (Tuvalu). Some historical data is available on deaths from natural disasters. The first recorded death in the Pacific region was in RMI starting from 1831, although it most likely underestimates deaths and financial losses, especially in early years when there were no formal disaster response systems or insurance schemes.

Destinational Porter are summarised in the table below, with the caveat that the data has significant gaps. For example, few countries quantify or record deaths and subsistence crop losses caused by drought or its long-term effects on food security.

Disruptions to agriculture, tourism and industrial production continued into early 2017. In RMI, the economic impact was estimated at USD$35 million or approximately 4% of GDP for FY 2016. ### Climate change scenarios and impacts

Climate change impacts already observed include variations in air and ocean temperatures, ocean chemistry, rainfall patterns, sea levels, and wave climate, and in particular extremes such as tropical cyclones, droughts, and distinct storm swell events. Over the past several decades, the Pacific region has experienced an increase in the frequency of extreme weather events. Extreme weather events have also increased in frequency and intensity[1] and most climate projection models indicate an increase in the proportion of more intense storms by the late 21st century.[2]

The key projected change impacts affecting the five Programme countries are as follows:

- Increased temperature: The average annual temperature is expected to rise by about 0.5°C, as large as the magnitude of global average warming for all emissions scenarios due to the fact that the oceans have been warming, and are projected to warm into the future, at a lower rate than land areas.

Assessment of climate change impacts on health: The projected increase in temperature is expected to be more pronounced for all tropical Pacific countries. By 2050, warming under the very low emissions scenario is projected to plateau at around +3.0 to +4.0°C above pre-industrial levels[3] and under the very high emissions scenario it could reach +6.0 to +7.0°C.[4] The projected warming ranges from: <0.5 ± 1.0°C for the very low emissions scenario to 2.0 ± 4.0°C for the very high emissions scenario[5]. Excess heat mortality is expected every 20 years in average temperature increases of +3.0°C or more, and will continue to increase approximately in line with average temperatures.[6] The frequency and magnitude of extreme heatwaves, including the frequency and magnitude of extreme high temperatures, will continue to increase through the 21st century[7].

## Impacts to coastal morphology, ecosystems and dependent human communities are detectable today and disproportionately higher risks are expected over the course of the 21st century. Without adequate adaptation measures, there is increasing concern that some island nations may become uninhabitable due to rising sea levels and climate change, including the five small, low-lying island states covered by this Programme.

### Figure 11: Sea-level projections on the A1B (mid-range) scenario in the Pacific island region for 2081 - 2100 relative to 1981 - 2000. Continues into the uncertainty (Source: Australian Bureau and CSIRO, 2015)

#### Increasing sea surface temperature:
In the Pacific region, sea-surface temperatures are projected to increase by 1.4 ± 0.7°C, 2.2 ± 0.8°C and 2.6 ± 0.9°C between 1990 and 2090 for the B1 (low), A1B (medium) and A2 (high) scenarios respectively. This is consistent with the observed warming projected in the south-eastern Pacific. The West Pacific Warm Pool provides the energy to sustain western Pacific convection and tropical atmospheric circulation; and is an important factor in ENSO variability. The warm pool has been expanding over recent decades, increasing in size, with the edge of the pool moving thousands of kilometres to the east over the next few decades.

The key impacts of rising sea-surface temperatures currently being observed and projected to continue include coral bleaching and death, loss of habitat for many marine species, and changes in the timing of periodic life cycle events. Coral death and bleaching threaten marine biodiversity and food security by reducing fish supplies for local communities; and affect the ranges of the pelagic fish from which island nations derive valuable licensing incomes. Increasing sea surface temperature can also exacerbate tropical cyclone intensity.

## Ocean acidification and degeneration

The projected increase in atmospheric CO₂ concentration is expected to exacerbate ocean acidification, which can severely disrupt the health and sustainability of all carbonate organisms in marine ecosystems, especially coral reefs. In its 2019 Special Report on the Ocean and Climate Change, the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) projected that by 2100, the tropical Pacific ocean will experience pH drops of either 0.364–0.482 (RCP2.6) or 0.297–0.291 (RCP8.5) pH units by 2081 – 2100 compared to pre-industrial levels, with a corresponding increase in seawater carbonate ion concentration and lower buffer capacity and upwelling systems. Under the RCP3.5 scenario, the tropical Pacific pH is projected to decrease by 0.15 units from the period 1986 – 2005 into the 2040 – 2060 period, with dramatic changes to aragonite saturation state.

Projected changes in the tropical Pacific Ocean aragonite saturation state were also estimated under the low, medium and high emissions scenarios (B1, A1B and A2, respectively). The results indicated that:

- Magnitude and rate of ocean acidification are proportional to atmospheric CO₂ concentration.
- Aragonite saturation state by 2050 is largely independent of emissions scenarios, which predict similar atmospheric CO₂ concentrations.

Aragonite saturation state from 2051 – 2100 will decline in the entire Pacific Island region, with the greatest changes observed under the highest emissions scenario. The lowest aragonite saturation state values will occur over eastern equatorial Pacific, to the east of 160°W, and the highest values will occur in the South Equatorial Current region, approximately between 5°S and 20°S (Cook Islands and Tuvalu).

## B

### Increase in sea level change-related sea level rise, with population growth and coastal developments increasing the exposure of populations and assets to coastal risk. Essential infrastructure—ports, hospitals, power supplies, water storage, roads, sanitation systems, cyclone shelters—are already vulnerable and are expected to become more so.

#### Drought: An increase in drought is projected at the eastern edge of the SPCZ—including the northern Cook Islands. Projected changes in the average frequency, intensity and duration of drought reflect changes in annual precipitation and temperature. Projections for other variables such as evaporation rates and soil moisture are less certain than for other variables.

#### Country-specific climate projections

Historical data on monthly surface temperature and mean annual precipitation plots are provided below. Values for the projected changes in the annual and seasonal mean climate for the five Programme countries under each RCP scenario are given in Appendix B (Country Study [Annex A]).

Further warming is expected at all countries with very high confidence. The increase in extreme days is projected to be by about the same as average temperature. Under all RCPs, the warming is up to 1.0°C above pre-industrial levels for the five Programme countries by 2030 and up to 2.0°C by 2050 in New Zealand. However, there is a growing difference in warming between each RCP after 2030:

- In the Northern Cook Islands by 2090, a warming of 2.0 – 3.8°C is projected for RCP8.5 (very high emissions scenario) while a warming of 1.7 – 4.2°C is projected for RCP6.5 and a warming of 0.2 – 1.1°C is projected for RCP2.6.
- In Niue by 2090, a warming of 1.7 – 4.2°C is projected for RCP8.5 while a warming of 0.4 – 1.2°C is projected for RCP6.5 and a warming of 0.2 – 0.3°C is projected for RCP2.6.
- In Palau by 2090, a warming of 2.1 – 4.0°C is projected for RCP8.5 while a warming of 0.4 – 1.2°C is projected for RCP6.5 and a warming of 0.2 – 0.3°C is projected for RCP2.6.
- In northern RMI by 2090, a warming of 2.2 – 4.2°C is projected for RCP8.5 while a warming of 0.5 – 1.2°C is projected for RCP6.5 and a warming of 0.3 – 0.7°C is projected for RCP2.6.
- In Tuvalu by 2090, a warming of 2.0 – 4.0°C is projected for RCP8.5 while a warming of 0.4 – 1.3°C is projected for RCP2.6.

In the South Pacific Cook Islands and Niue, mean annual rainfall increased between 1979 and 2006 but this is not projected to continue in the future, which suggests that the recent increase may be due to natural variability rather than as a climate change warming. The year-to-year variability in rainfall remains higher than that from the programme for Cook Islands and Niue. There are no clear signs of climate change on average rainfall nor may it be obvious in the short or medium term due to natural variability.

The influence of topography on the local climate may cause variation between the Northern and Southern Cook Islands.

## Niue
- Annual mean rainfall is projected to increase according to most models, although the model average indicates a slight increase for the high-emission scenario by 2100. There is greater uncertainty about the sign of change in the low-emission scenario, with some models projecting an increase and others a decrease.

## Palau
- Long-term annual rainfall projected almost all models to increase. Most models project increases from November – April rainfall, with little change for November – April rainfall. There will still be wet and dry years and decades due to natural variability, but the long-term average is expected to be wetter.

## RMI
- Long-term annual rainfall is projected by almost all models to increase, with most models projecting increases from November – April rainfall. There will still be wet and dry years and decades due to natural variability, but the long-term average is expected to be wetter.

## Tuvalu
- Annual mean rainfall is projected to increase or decrease according to different CMIP5 models, although there are no clear signs of climate change on average rainfall. The regionally averaged annual rainfall is projected to increase to the north, and a region of little change or slight decrease in the south. Consequently, there is large spread in model projections for both the November – April and May – October seasons.

The potential high impact climate futures for the Programme countries are as follows:

- **Cook Islands**: *Warmer and Much Wetter* with annual mean temperature increases of 14 °C and annual mean rainfall increases of 17 % relative to 1980–1999. *Warmer and Much Drier* with annual mean temperature increases of 0.9 °C and annual mean rainfall decreases of 21 % relative to 1980–1999.
- **Niue**: *Hotter and Much Wetter* with annual mean temperature increases of 1.7 °C and annual mean rainfall increases of 34 % relative to 1980–1999. *Warmer and Much Drier* with annual mean temperature increases of 2.5 °C and annual mean rainfall decreases of 60 % relative to 1980–1999.
- **Palau**: *Warmer and Much Wetter* with annual mean temperature increases of 1.2 °C and annual mean rainfall increases of 13 % relative to 1980–1999. *Warmer and Much Drier* with annual mean temperature increases of 4.5 °C and annual mean rainfall decreases of 67 % relative to 1980–1999.
- **RMI**: *Warmer and Much Wetter* with annual mean temperature increases of 1.3 °C and annual mean rainfall increases of 23 % relative to 1980–1999. *Warmer and Drier* with annual mean temperature increases of 5.7 °C and annual mean rainfall decreases of 76 % relative to 1980–1999.
- **Tuvalu**: *Hotter and Much Wetter* with annual mean temperature increases of 1.8 °C and annual mean rainfall increases of 25 % relative to 1980–1999. *Warmer and Drier* with annual mean temperature increases of 9.9 °C and annual mean rainfall decreases of 93 % relative to 1980–1999.

**Sector-specific impacts of climate change**

Despite their small size, Pacific island nations are faced with the impacts of climate variability and climate change – rising temperatures, changing rainfall frequency and intensity, ocean warming and acidification, and sea-level rise – as outlined in the above sections. Pollution and overuse of natural resources are also major challenges for these countries.

The impacts of climate change on agriculture, food security, water supply, health, and infrastructure are degrading Pacific island ecosystems and compound the impacts of climate change.

This section analyses the sector-specific impacts of climate change on key sectors in the Pacific – agriculture, fisheries, water supply, health, and infrastructure. These sectors have been identified as priority sectors which have also been identified as priority sectors in the Pacific Roadmap for Strengthened Climate Services 2017–2026. Given that there is a lack of specific information on climate change impacts available for some countries, the analysis is presented from a regional perspective. Information specific to the five Programme countries is included, where available. Further analyses are provided in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2).

**Agriculture**

In the five Programme countries, food crops are predominantly grown for subsistence purposes; however, most households are unable to meet their food needs through their own production and rely on markets. Staple foods include root crops such as taro, sweet potato, yam, and banana. Wheat flour and rice are important staples that are almost entirely imported. Cacao, coconut, palm oil and sugar are the main export crops. Traditionally, Pacific island communities grew multiple crops, which contributed to food security by diversifying their diets and reducing reliance on imports. However, increasing production of monoculture export crops has reduced the diversity of local production and decreased resilience in the face of climate change.

Climate change is adversely impacting on agriculture and food security in Pacific island countries in several ways: affecting both the ability to produce food and the ability to import food. Intermittent climate variability has a large influence on agricultural production, and longer-term systemic changes in the Pacific islands’ food production and food security


---
**Page 38**
---

additional limiting factor. For coastal communities, food production is compromised by the effects of erosion, increased contamination of groundwater and estuaries by saltwater intrusion, cyclones and storm surges, heat stress and drought.** B **Sea-level rise is a direct threat to taro cultivation – the main agricultural activity in many Pacific Island countries. The loss of land due to sea-level rise has significant impacts on cultural and religious obligations. At present, since saltwater seeps into or displaces fresh water in their taro paddies, farmers are forced to plant their crops at higher elevations where they have to climb up steep paths and clear people-sleeping slopes to plant. This creates further issues such as erosion, silting of waterways, and the loss of land for the downstream marine population.* In Tuvalu, sea intrusion is enhanced by soil porosity and destroys pulp from coconut trees, which are a major food source. The destruction of fruit tree yields of coconut, banana and breadfruit.

Tropical cyclones can cause significant losses in agricultural production, with the potential to destroy trees and harvests. Cyclone Pam caused severe damage across Vanuatu.* For example, in 2005, Kupukupa (Cook Islands) was completely inundated by wave surges associated with cyclones and strong winds resulting in loss of agricultural land and infrastructure.* In Tonga, high wind speeds are said to have destroyed crops such as bananas, breadfruit, coconuts and mangroves.

Increases in extreme weather events such as droughts and sea level rise lead to the decline of waterlogging and short-term flooding (2003–2009), with devastating effects on agricultural productivity – including grain, horticultural, forestry and livestock products. Key export crops such as pawpaw are also at risk.* In Tonga, the loss of waterlogging has led to a decline in production of taro, while waterlogging and sugar cane is affected by floods. High temperatures affect the formation of sweet potato and yam; and increase the risk of pests and diseases.*

Frequent flooding events and sea level rise are critical infrastructure undermines both subsistence and commercial agriculture. Damage to equipment for processing and storing food can threaten the effective supply of food; and damages to roads, rails and vehicles can disrupt the supply of goods to markets, which in turn undermines food security.

The impact of future climate change on agriculture has serious implications for food security in the five Pacific Programme countries. Increasing food imports will be required to meet basic caloric needs and the price of imported foods is likely to increase, particularly for low-income households.* Food insecurity will increase unless supplementary sources of income can be found.*179

Energy

Pacific island countries are heavily dependent on fossil fuels to meet local energy demands; and the cost of importing renewables the highest in the world – much as 200-300% compared to other regions.* predominantly due to high fuel transportation costs. Consequently, Pacific SIDS are highly vulnerable to oil price shocks and thus energy insecurity.

The development and use of energy resources are highly important to development in the Pacific. Fossil fuel dependence impedes adaptation capacity; increasing demand for foreign exchange for imports – forces spending on imports may constitute over 50% of total export earnings. Developing self-sufficiency in energy production using renewable energy sources can enable import savings to be redirected to adaptation investments and

## Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and Greenhouse Gas Emissions

Furthermore, although greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from SIDS are minute on the global scale, reducing GHG emissions in the Pacific is important for two reasons: i) Every GHG reduction counts; and ii) Reducing emissions demonstrates feasibility to larger emitters and increases the credibility and momentum of climate action.

In 2012, the Cook Islands, Niue and Tuvalu set the target of 100% renewable energy by 2020. The Nuse Strategic Energy Road Map 2015-2035 subsequently revised the target as 80% renewable energy generation by 2030.

### Energy Production

Energy production – including the efficiency of production – is highly sensitive to meteorological and climate conditions, and the intermittence of renewable energy systems in particular must take into account local climatic conditions during both their design and operation. For example, information on solar radiation and wind field characteristics are critical for determining the optimal location of photovoltaic (PV) or wind turbine catchment domains needed for hydropower systems. Therefore, partnerships and stakeholder engagement between MINHs and the energy sector to apply weather and climate information are critical to developing efficient and effective energy systems.

## Health

Climate change has significant and diverse impacts on human health: i) primary or direct effects – e.g., injuries and deaths caused by extreme weather events such as cyclones; ii) secondary or indirect effects – e.g., the increased incidence of vector-borne diseases, potential changes in food production patterns and limited access to clean water; iii) delayed effects – e.g., disruptions to health and social services.

Pacific island countries are some of the most vulnerable in the world to the health impacts of climate change. These include changes in temperature and precipitation patterns, associated health risks, and their limited capacities to manage and adapt in the face of climate risks. The figure below shows the climate change and health impact pathways relevant to the Pacific island countries.

## Water

At the regional level, the Pacific island countries have the lowest access to improved drinking water in the world and the highest prevalence of unsafe water. This is due to a combination of factors including small landmasses between countries and within regions; and regional statistics thus hide the impressive performance of individual countries. Improved drinking water coverage is at least 95% (Cook Islands, Niue, RMI and Tuvalu) but no data was available for other Pacific island countries.

The lack of access to improved sanitation facilities in many Pacific island countries presents significant challenges to improving water, sanitation and hygiene in Pacific island countries. The disparity between urban and rural drinking water and sanitation coverage is particularly evident in the RMI. The Marshall Islands has the largest number of inhabited islands or atolls (24) of five Programme countries, spread across

16 Woodman et al., 2014; Lancet. Climate change and health on the latest IPCC report: a review of the published literature
17 Global Change Institute and the School of Public Health, The University of Queensland, 2019. Water, Sanitation and Hygiene in the Pacific: The need to meet SDG 6


---
**Page 41**
---

an EEC of less than 10 km² and is small rural communities are often too remote – and thus expensive – to be supplied with improved water and sanitation.

In the Pacific island region, water is the primary means through which climate variability, climate change and natural disasters impact human health and wellbeing. Direct impacts on water resources can occur from increased intensity of rainfall and flash floods leading to contamination of water supplies impacting on water quality; reduced safety of groundwater due to slower recharge and saltwater intrusion from reduced freshwater flow; and increased salinity in coastal areas. Indirect impacts include increased pressure on water resources, pressures on ecosystems and biodiversity and subsequent changes (e.g. changes in species abundance and distribution) that can impact human health and wellbeing. These indirect impacts may also result in potential food insecurity; contamination of water resources due to changes to runoff and sedimentation; and sea-level rise.

All Pacific island countries are highly dependent on rainfall as the main freshwater source, with particular vulnerability in the low-lying islands (Nauru, RMI and Tuvalu) that have no significant surface and/or groundwater resources. In the moderate-sized volcanic island of Rarotonga (Cook Islands), water resources are generally adequate to meet demand; however, there is a risk of contamination from poor sanitation practices. In Palau, despite its small resident population, water consumption is relatively high due to a growing tourism industry. Water supply in Palau is primarily dependent on rainfall and groundwater. However, the lack of regular rainfall as few irrigation systems are in place. In Tuvalu, groundwater must be used for washing clothes and bathing; however, there is no potable water available for drinking or cooking.

The population growth is increasing demand for already constrained water resources, thus exacerbating the issues.

More intense ENSO events will impact on water supply and island economies. The El Niño event in 1997–1998 was particularly severe, with a prolonged dry season lasting from April to August – a four-month period – and led the Government of RMI to declare the entire archipelago a disaster area. Droughts are especially damaging in the atolls lacking sufficient rainwater harvesting and storage capacity, as is the case in Kiribati where water supply is already limited by low rainfall (10–25 mm per year) and there is no water already in limited supply, a very serious issue. The strong La Niña event that followed in 1998 – 2000 led to acute shortages in many Pacific island countries – with partial shutdown of the tourism industry required in some extreme cases.

**Fisheries and Aquaculture**

The Pacific island region is heavily dependent on oceanic and coastal fisheries for food security, livelihoods, representation and economic development. Fish intake is high and is estimated to account for 50–90% of people’s protein intake in rural areas, and 40–60 % in urban areas. Most of the fish eaten by rural communities comes from oceanic sources (e.g., tuna), while most of that consumed in urban areas comes from farming or fishing to GPP ranges from 1.5% (Cook Islands) to 29.5% (Marshall Islands), as estimated by SPC in 2016.

Climate change is expected to have significant impacts on coastal and oceanic habitats, the fish and invertibrates that they support, and consequently, the productivity of fisheries and aquaculture. Increasing


---
**Page 42**
---

temporal and spatial variability in fish abundance may result from degradation of reefs and mangroves, and changing water turbidity, salinity and temperature. In particular, decreased productivity of demersal fish and invertebrates—which constitute the greatest percentage of total catch in Palau, RMI and Tuvalu—and a more pronounced effect on small pelagic species such as tuna, may have significant impacts on food security resources by Pacific island communities and economies. Invertibrates such as pearl oysters and shrimp, which are important sources of income for many Pacific island countries, also face increased mortality.

Increasing variability in fish abundance will differentially affect nutrition and incomes across the five Programme countries. In particular, coastal fisheries may not meet the food security needs of Nauru and Tuvalu due to limited transport infrastructure and access to markets.

The Western and Central Pacific Ocean has the largest tuna fishery in the world.147 In 2017, fishing license fees to tuna operators accounted for 58% of total government revenue. The tuna fishery is also vulnerable to severe ENSO events due to climate change impacts on sea surface temperature and catch in the EEZs of the Pacific island countries, with resultant impacts on revenue earned from access fees paid by distant water fishing nations.148 Climate change will likely result in increased variability in fish abundance, which may reduce catches per unit net productivity, increase variability in the catches and so decrease catch per unit effort with subsequent increases in costs to harvest a given quantity of fish. This will place pressure on the most valuable species of bigeye and yellowfin in an attempt to offset increasing costs.149

Furthermore, climate change-related impacts in storm damages may adversely affect fisheries development or infrastructure. For example, in 2016, Cyclone Winston caused damage to fishing boats and processing facilities. For example, in 2004, damages caused by Cyclone Heta caused subsistence fishing in Nue to cease for several weeks.150

Reef ecosystems

The tropical Pacific region is home to around a quarter of the world’s reefs, with RMI among the top ten richest coral reef countries in the world. Coral reefs are one of the main attractions of the Pacific region's tourism industry. Pacific reefs sustain fisheries and marine ecosystems and act as a natural protection against coastal inundation and erosion.

Coral reefs are highly vulnerable to climate variability, increases in sea surface temperature result in coral bleaching, which occurs when water temperatures rise 1.8 – 3.6°C above the warmest normal summer temperature for that location. Coral bleaching is caused by increased stress on corals from high SSTs and can be prolonged and intense coral bleaching lead to coral death. The projected changes in severe coral bleaching risk are shown in Figure 2.10. These projections are detailed in (Figure 2.10). Over time, there is a decrease in the time between two periods of high risk and increase in the duration of elevated risk as sea surface temperature (SST) increases. When bleaching occurs, coral reef recovery is dependent on the SST returning to normal levels within three months or less. Recovery from bleaching can take once the frequency of severe bleaching events rises above more than once every five years.150 The projections indicate that the Palau EEZ and the highest coral bleaching risk of the five countries at SST rise.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 167

## Warning: Rising sea surface temperatures increase the incidence of toxic algae that grows on Pacific reefs.

In Nauru, as in the rest of the South Pacific, ciguatera poisoning is associated with coral bleaching and rates of infection were reported to increase in the Nauru in the years following Cyclone Heta in 2004. Furthermore, increasing ocean acidification has been linked to a decline in calcification rates of corals and other organisms that build calcium carbonate skeletons.

### Mass coral bleaching has already occurred in the Pacific and more frequent and severe episodes of bleaching are expected as global temperatures rise.

In 1982–1983, 1997-1998, 2002-2003, and 2014 – 2017, the world experienced its longest, most widespread and most severe global bleaching event on record. **[1]** In some areas of the Marshall Islands, up to 100% of the coral died due to repeated bleaching events in 2016-2017. The percentage of corals that were lost or damaged by bleaching in this area was in 2009 around 80%, of what would have been in the absence of thermal stress; and that increasing temperature has reduced Pacific coral areas to 55% in 2000 and 20% in 2016 – from 88% in the base year (1974). By 2100, coral reefs will be lost or severely damaged by bleaching events in all but a few locations. This will have impacts on tourism, fisheries and coral mining industries, and loss of areas of significance to maintaining marine biodiversity.

Furthermore, coral reefs are threatened by unsustainable development practices. In Tuvalu, sand, calcareous algae and shells, and coral are essential to the underlying structure of its islands. Urban development, habitat conversion, dredging and extraction of coralline aggregates have led to the destruction of coral reef systems. **[2]**

## Mangroves

Mangroves act as a natural defence against coastal erosion, and filter sediment to maintain the health of reefs and fish stocks. In Palau, communities are reliant on mangroves for food, medicine, building materials and firewood. Mangrove forests also provide habitat for many species of birds, reptiles, mammals and fish. Climate events can negatively impact on mangrove ecosystems. For example, periods of intense precipitation and drought affect the growth and distribution of mangroves, which will be exacerbated by high temperatures.

## Tourism

Tuvalu is a major economic driver in the Pacific region, particularly in the Cook Islands and Palau, where it accounts for a majority of GDP. **[3]** In Cook Islands, tourism is estimated to account for around 82% of GDP. **[4]** In Palau, the service sector – including direct tourism contributions – accounts for more than 80% of GDP and contributes significantly to employment in the country. **[5]**

In 2017, Tuvalu was ranked as one of the top 30 most sustainable destinations for sustainable tourism and launched its Responsible Tourism policy in September 2017. The Government of Tuvalu has identified tourism as a key sector for economic growth and development, with the cost of access to the islands **[6]**. In Tuvalu, tourism is proposed to have the highest growth potential of any


---
**Page 44**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 167 OF 167

Industry-level modeling recommendations for development of the tourism sector are stated in its National Tourism Development Strategy 2015-2019.

The interrelationship between climate and tourism in the Pacific is complex and multi-faceted—climate is both a driver of tourism demand, as well as an important factor, as well as a changing factor that poses significant threats to both the industry and to tourists. Climate change can impact the tourism industry "by changing the attractiveness of the climate of tourist destinations, by reducing the value of attractions at destinations, and by altering the availability of environmental resources that serve as tourist attractions in Pacific island countries. Widespread resource degradation and loss of biodiversity will also reduce the overall quality of life for residents and decrease their attractiveness." A study commissioned by the Asian Development Bank predicted that global warming will lessen the Pacific island region as a tourism attraction, and reduce tourism revenues by around 30% for the Pacific region as a whole, under all climate scenarios.

Interconnectivity between the COVID-19 pandemic and climate change

As of 12 June 2020, there were 312 reported cases and seven deaths attributed to COVID-19 across the Pacific island region. None of the five Programme countries have yet been affected; however, a State of Emergency was declared in Papua New Guinea on 8 April 2020. Given that many Pacific islands are vulnerable to enter their cyclone, drought, heatwaves or monsoon seasons, the potential for an "unprecedented double disaster" is increasing.

COVID-19 represents a significant challenge for the Pacific island region, on account of several risk factors:

* Limitations in quality health services due to lack of infrastructure, equipment and qualified personnel;
* Lack of laboratory equipment needed to analyse tests on sites, which impedes the identification of cases;

## Baseline climate information services

As their climate becomes more chaotic and unpredictable,178 the impact of these changes in the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu is magnified by a lack of data and therefore of information, and by uncertainty about future conditions. This makes them highly vulnerable to climate-related hazards, and whose advice is often sought by other Pacific SIDS.

Each of the five countries has a WMO Category (Basic level) National Meteorological and Hydrological Service (NMHS), which provides local weather forecasts and seasonal (3 or 9 month) climate projections of probable rainfall and temperature. These services are based on data from observations generated by the Fiji Meteorological Service in its role as the Regional Specialised Meteorological Centre, and Pacific Climate Change Services.

The NMHSs in all five countries are currently able to generate basic climate observations, and conduct data management and interaction with users of their national data and the forecasts that they receive. However, the quality and quantity of these services is limited by the availability and reliability of good data from terrestrial and sea surface observation points over a wide geographical area.

Lack of data from their vast ocean areas and widely dispersed outer islands, changes in climate patterns, and the increasing frequency of extreme weather events make it difficult for Pacific SIDS to put in place comprehensive and sustained climate information and multi-hazard early warning systems (MHEWS).179 In addition, many of these countries have limited data capacity. Some have sufficient climate services staff able to work directly with climate-sensitive sectors, agencies or communities issues common to all NMHSs of Pacific SIDS identified in the Pacific Island Meteorological Strategy 2017–2026180 that are not present in the five small countries of this Programme differ from those of larger Pacific island nations.

### • Weather Services: Many NMHSs in the region operate with poor infrastructure and with staffing constraints. Communication to communities and other user groups requires strengthening, in addition to training for staff on climate services and public awareness.

This applies particularly to very small SIDS, whose forecasts are generated using low-resolution data from too few observation points.

Communication to communities will benefit from several Programme activities, including the development of accessible language and visual presentations, and the improvement of communication technology and coverage. (Results 2 and 3).

### • Financial Services: The Programme will support securing long-term financing of climate services, will strengthen the capacity of NMHSs to negotiate more secure base appropriation as the value of their services is demonstrated through this Programme. (Result 4).

## B

### Climate Services:
- As a qualified collectors and analysers of national and local climate data, NMHSs have a thorough understanding of climate processes and change and of the practical implications for their countries.
- They are well placed to provide advice on climate-related planning, aid coordination, disaster preparedness and risk reduction, and to international advocacy and negotiations.
- Climate services are increasingly being included in the allocation of funding for climate change data collection and analysis and adaptation planning.

This applies to the five Programme countries and is a major focus of the Programme. The development of climate services will be the priority agenda item for monitoring, early warning, prediction, disaster risk reduction strategies, dissemination and communication of advice on extreme events, and response actions are essential to reducing the impacts of climate change.
- Improved or new legislation, where requested, and strategic plans that formalise roles, responsibilities and funding mechanisms for NMHSs as well as disaster management organisations and public broadcasters, will be developed through Result 1.

### Ocean Services:
In many Pacific Island countries, there is no official purveyor of ocean information, and enquiries are often directed to the NMHS. The Pacific Islands Climate Change Technology has identified ocean services as a long-term goal.
- NMHSs report high demand for ocean climate information services. In the absence of observation equipment, data collection and analysis, and forecasting, the lack of ocean climate information has implications for climate and weather prediction both locally and globally and limits the ability of Pacific SIDS to provide reliable and timely early warnings. The Programme will establish end-to-end ocean services in the five countries through Result 1.

### Hydrology:
To support the needs of NMHSs for drought and flood forecasting, priority needs include data management and sharing, technical support, downsampling modelling on water resource uses, and integrating climate science in water resources planning.
- The Programme will focus its efforts in improving data sharing and management at national level through the existing regional GEF-funded "Inform" project. Lessons learned from previous projects will inform approaches to drought forecasting and management.

Regional Specialised Meteorological Centres (RSMC) are responsible for the distribution of official information, advisories and warnings for one or more specific weather types such as severe weather, tropical cyclones or hurricanes. The World Meteorological Organization (WMO) coordinates these centres through its Regional Office for the Western Pacific (RWO-WP) in Manila, Philippines. The WMO's World Weather Watch. In the Pacific, the Fiji Meteorological Service (FMS) operates as a RSMC and is responsible for the distribution of official information, advisories and warnings to all countries from the east coast of Australia to French Polynesia and beyond. However, due to existing arrangements between Fiji and other centres (Wellington, NZMet and Brisbane, BM) in the Southwest Pacific, Fiji's primary area of responsibility extends further north into Papua New Guinea and Solomon Islands. The Tropical Cyclone Centre names and tracks all tropical cyclones in this area and issues regular tropical cyclone and severe weather bulletins for these countries. FMS also provides a daily tropical cyclone forecast service to Cook Islands, Niue and Tuvalu. FMS has been providing this service free of charge since its inception as an RSMC in 1995. For the two northern Pacific countries, as part of NOAA, Guam National Weather Service (NWS) is responsible for issuing tropical cyclone warnings and forecasts for the Federated States of Micronesia, which includes Palau and RMI. All five of the countries' NMSs then disseminate the forecasts and provide their own warnings. The Joint Typhoon Warning Center (JTWC) provides a daily tropical cyclone forecast service to all five countries in the western Pacific region, including Japan, South Korea, and Indian Ocean for all branches of the U.S. Department of Defence and other U.S. government agencies.

The JTWC uses NASA satellite data such as the Tropical Rainfall Measuring Mission's (TRMM) Microwave Imager (TMI), the precipitation radar aboard the National Polar-orbiting Operational Environmental Satellite System (NPOESS) Preparatory Project (PJP) satellite, the CloudSat cloud radar and the Moderate Resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer (MODIS) onboard one of NASA's Aqua and Terra satellites. FJi RSMC uses data from Japan's Himawari satellite. Himawari-8 and 9 are geostationary satellites that provide continuous coverage over the western Pacific region.

The GFCS categorises MMS climate services as Basic to Advanced (Figure 14). The climate services in the five participating countries can be categorised mostly as Basic (Category 1). The NMSs in the Programme countries have a long history of providing basic climate services, including weather forecasting and climate monitoring. The proposed Programme interventions will enable them to develop capacity for more advanced climate services, such as seasonal forecasts and establish themselves as Essential (Category 2) services. Further details regarding the current status of climate services provided by NMSs in each of the five countries are provided in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2).

## Types of climate products and services by category of national climate service provider™

While the states of Nauru in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands and Tuvalu have many competencies and are committed to providing climate and weather services, they are constrained by the limits of the human, financial and technical resources available to them. These factors limit the use of climate information for decision-making at national level in these countries. In contrast, some small island developing states (SIDS) in the Pacific Island communities, such as Cook Islands, Palau, RMI, and Tuvalu, are particularly vulnerable due to a lack of infrastructure, limited access to markets, and their geographical remoteness compared with other SIDS with remote outer islands. These countries have a number of outlying islands with weak communication and transport networks. Consequently, these communities often lack access to and understanding of actionable climate information that can help them respond timely responses or enhancing their resilience.

## Disaster risk management in the Pacific

In 2016, the Pacific Islands Forum Leaders endorsed the Framework for Resilient Development in the Pacific: An Integrated Approach to Address Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management (FRDP). The Pacific Resilience Programme (PRP) is a multi-year initiative that will support the implementation of disaster risk management communities and other partners (e.g. government representatives, relevant sectors and private investors) with technical assistance and capacity building in order to implement the FRDP at national level across the Pacific region. The FRDP provides high level voluntary strategic guidance on how to enhance resilience to climate change and disasters based on three inter-related Goals: i) Strengthened integrated adaptation and risk management; ii) Improved access to information, knowledge and services for decision-making; iii) Enhanced capacity of communities and institutions to manage disaster risks.

The Programme will adopt a regional integrated approach to directly address Goals 1 and 3 of the Framework, and will create an enabling environment to support Goal 2. At the national level, disaster risk management (DRM) is organised as shown in the table below:

| Country | Institutional arrangements for DRM | National policy / framework/ plan for DRM |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cook Islands | Ministry of Environment | Climate Change Strategy |
| Palau | Ministry of Environment | Climate Change Strategy |
| RMI | Ministry of Environment | Climate Change Strategy |
| Tuvalu | Ministry of Environment | Climate Change Strategy |

Cook Islands

* **Emergency Management Cook Islands (EMCI)** coordinates all DRM activities and provides policy advice to the National Disaster Risk Management Council (NDRMC), which was chartered in 2013.

Nauru

* **The National Disaster Council (NDC)** is responsible for day-to-day coordination for hazards, with the Police undertaking much of this role. The NDC also coordinates disaster preparedness and awareness raising. In times of disaster, the Chief of Police provides leadership over overall response relief and initial recovery.

Palau

* **The National Emergency Management Office (NEMO)** is responsible for the development and implementation of DRM prepreparedness, response and immediate relief actions. NEMO also acts as a focal point for coordination of DRM activities and stakeholder engagement.
* **The National Disaster Council (NDC)** provides overall direction and coordination responsibilities of the National Emergency Operations Centre (NEOC) and coordinates disaster management and public education programmes in the outer island.

Marshall Islands

* **The National Disaster Committee (NDC)** is the principal body responsible for DRM policy, strategic action and monitoring in the Marshall Islands. The National Disaster Management Office (NDMO), which is the coordination centre at the national level, provides overall direction and coordination of DRM activities on a countrywide basis.
* **Atol Disaster Risk Management Committees** are established to provide local disaster risk management.

Tuvalu

* **The National Disaster Committee (NDC)** is coordinated by the National Strategic Action Plan for Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management (NSAP). The NDC is housed in the Office of the Prime Minister. The NDC is responsible for DRM policy, strategic action and monitoring, disaster risk management programming, policy development and implementation.
* **The National Strategic Action Plan (NSAP)** serves as a bridge between the NDC and outer island communities during periods of normal operations and in times of disasters. It also provides direction to disaster management and public education programmes in the respective islands.

The NSAP is the basis for the National Climate Change Advisory Committee and NDC should merge for the purpose of coordinating and driving implementation of the NSAP.
