# Overview of disaster risk management (DRM) arrangements in the five Programme countries

## Financing for climate services and disaster risk management

### Cook Islands: The estimated climate change spending for the financial period 2016/17 – 2019/20 is NZD 3,584,000. This includes NZD 1,000,000 from the Government of Cook Islands, 6% by development partners and 3% by borrowing (loans). Total climate change finance equates to approximately USD 972,000. The total disaster risk management spending is NZD 200,000 from the Government Reserve Trust Fund in the 2011/12 financial year. The Fund is limited to emergency response and early recovery activities and is not intended for long-term recovery or reconstruction of infrastructure. Total climate change and DRM spending is USD 900,000.

### Niue: There is no national budget allocation for climate change programming in Niue through the GCF Readiness and Preparedness Support Program. SREP6 is acting as the proxy of Niue to notionally fund mainstream climate finance. Disaster risk management is not budgeted for explicitly but Niue does not have a disaster risk management plan. The National Climate Change Strategy (2013) states that Niue has funds for emergency responses.198 The JNAP estimates that the total cost for implementation is NZD 2,117,255 (approximately USD 1.3 million), with NZD 268,037 (approximately USD 160,000) coming from the GCF Readiness and Preparedness Support Program (GCF RPSP), the Pacific Adaptation Fund (PAF) and SREP6.

### Palau: There is no national budget allocation for climate change programming in Palau. This is also reflected in the budget for finance post-disaster needs, resulting in reallocation of funds from development priorities to disaster relief efforts. The Palau Climate Change Policy 2015 estimates that the cost to implement the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) is USD 34 million. The total NAP implementation includes USD 393 million for climate change adaptation measures and USD 40.8 million for disaster risk management. The Action Plan includes establishment of a USD 25 million disaster contingency fund and a USD 1 billion National Disaster Recovery Fund and Insurance Program, neither of which is in place.200

### Marshall Islands: There is no national budget allocation for climate change programming in RM, with the exception of the GCF Readiness and Preparedness Support Program (GCF RPSP). A DRM management workshop for key ministries and development partners was held in collaboration with the NDRF Project. The aim of the workshop was to assist the setup of a national climate finance mechanism to support the Government in managing and leveraging climate change-related funds.

## Lessons from other projects and programs

There are a number of existing initiatives for climate information management in Pacific countries. For example, the Pacific Climate Change Science Programme (PCCSP)²⁹ generated the most detailed scientific assessments and projections of climate change to date for 14 Pacific SIDS. Its 2011 reports and 2015 updates are available online at <https://www.pacificclimate.org>. The PCCSP also developed a Climate Information System, which is streamlining existing environmental data by putting in place a series of data repositories and tools.²⁹ It will be important to ensure that the lessons learned from these initiatives are captured and shared widely.

The Pacific Climate Change Science Programme (PCCSP) was funded by USAID and the Australian Government and is active participants in the second phase of Australia’s Climate and Ocean Support Program in the Pacific (COSPac). Programs such as New Zealand’s National Climate Information and Atmospheric Research (NCIAR) Project, which is USAID’s National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) funded project, are also designed to ensure the long-term complementarity of their inputs.²⁹

The Global Climate Change Alliance: Pacific Small Island States Technical Assistance project is an EU-funded initiative that provides technical assistance to 14 Pacific countries (PCCSP countries) to adapt to climate change that was implemented by the Pacific Community (SPC) from July 2011 – November 2016. The project contributed significantly to the development of a regional adaptation strategy and provided support for national and regional collaboration. The overall assessment was that the project was highly effective and successful in achieving its objectives, with significant outputs and impacts.²⁹

The Pacific Climate Change Science Programme (PCCSP) identified a number of best practices and lessons learned, including: (i) Project incorporated on-ground adaptation projects complemented by climate change mainstreaming and capacity building; (ii) Recruitment of competent national coordinators fosters national ownership and was a key success factor for most projects; (iii)


---
**Page 53**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 48 OF 167

Embedding dedicated staff in other regional organisations (e.g. SREP) fosters regional collaboration and enhances efficiency; iv) Regional and national level training helped build country capacity across common and country-specific needs to support both project delivery and ongoing climate change adaptation, and v) Funding to the Pacific Climate Change Centre (PCCC) has been critical for building a strong foundation of climate information services in the region. The proposal also highlighted that significant challenges to implementing projects within the Pacific region included transport limitations, lack of access to reliable data, limited capacity at national level, and institutional constraints across the government and private sector. SPC identified a general “rule of thumb” to multiply the overall cost and time for outer island projects by two (compared to main islands) when deciding budgets and timelines. The proposal also highlighted that the project was implemented in a phased manner, with emphasis on Programme development and implementation cycle.

The Pacific Climate Prediction Initiative (PCKI-PAC) [8] project, implemented by the APEC Climate Center (APCC) and SREP in 2014-2017, has built a regional mechanism to provide locally tailored seasonal climate prediction information and training on downscaled climate predictions in the 14 Pacific island countries. The PCKI-PAC project was based on the Pacific Climate Prediction Initiative (PCKI), which is a sub-project of the Pacific Climate Watch (PCW) initiative. PCKI-PAC is a web-based dynamic multi-model ensemble (MME) prediction tool optimised for the Pacific region, and it provides climate information services to the Pacific region through the PCW platform.

PCKI-PAC is a series of trainings, application guideline development, and participatory development activities were conducted to ensure the sustainability of the regional mechanism. The ROK-PCI Q5 project was approved for a second phase in 2019. The project will continue to build on the success of the first phase by focusing on improving the forecast tool to address NHMS concerns regarding conflicting seasonal climate models, prediction and forecasting interventions (Result 2) and specialised technical training (Result 4).

The Pacific Islands Climate Information System (PICIS) [9] project “Pacific Strengthening Hydro-Meteorological and Early Warning Services” is being implemented by WMO in seven Pacific island countries, including Cook Islands, Niue and Tokelau. The objectives of the project are: i) Strengthening national meteorological services to provide climate information services for decision-making; ii) Supporting the seven NHMSs that it serves; iii) Enhanced regional and national facilities and capacities to produce seasonal climate forecasts and early warning systems; iv) Improved access to climate information by users, including the seven NHMSs that it serves; v) Enhanced regional and national facilities and capacities to produce seasonal climate forecasts and early warning systems; vi) Improved access to climate information by users, including the seven NHMSs that it serves.

The project is also supporting the development of long-term strategic plans for their NHMSs (planned for Q3 and Q4 2020).

A proposal for Phase II of the CREWS Pacific SIDS Project intends to further strengthen RMSC-Nad, regional climate services in the region. The project will focus on strengthening the capacity of RMSC-Nad to provide early warning systems in the Pacific. Phase II will cover all 14 Pacific island countries. In June 2020, at the 11th Meeting of the CREWS Steering Committee, the allocation of funds for Phase II of the project was approved.

The CREWS SIDS Project is a multi-year initiative that aims to strengthen national meteorological and hydro-meteorological services (NHMSs) in Pacific Small Island Developing States (SIDS). The Programme will implement “Contributions to Value” approaches proposed under the CREWS project, including using the existing NHMSs as a platform for climate information services. The programme will focus on strengthening early warning systems focused on reaching communities that are not currently well connected with the NHMS; promote coherence through cooperation with ongoing projects and initiatives; and promote an active dialogue with beneficiaries to find solutions to their identified EHS-related problems.

**Page 54**

Despite several initiatives having been implemented in the Pacific region, no previous project has so far attempted to address the priorities of the Pacific Island Meteorological Strategy 2017-2028 (1) in a comprehensive manner and address all the barriers to effective communication of climate information consistent with the strategy. The lack of infrastructure for weather stations, the limited capacity of meteorologists and the lack of communication to people at “the last mile”. Valuable lessons have been drawn from completed projects that highlight the importance of addressing these barriers in an integrated way if climate services are to be sustained unless all the barriers are addressed concurrently. For instance, COSPPac Phase 1 enabled NMHSs to provide more frequent and reliable weather forecasts for the region during the 2015-2016 El Niño drought periods. However, without formal agreement on their role in disaster forecasting, and without effective access to communities and the ability to effect behaviour change, the opportunity to help people prepare for climate-related hazards was lost. The COSPPac Phase II programme has piloted to provide capacity development and technical support to local governments in this area. However, the NMHSs were not aware of their role in disaster forecasting and many community members were not fully aware of their role and responsibilities. Thus, a major lesson learnt was that meaningful participation of vulnerable and marginalised communities must be ensured to effectively integrate climate risk management into disaster risk reduction.

Another pilot project worked successfully with communities in several Pacific SIDS on understanding and applying basic climatic science. However, the communities still receive forecasts and warnings in overly technical language which is difficult for them to interpret. The lack of a clear message from the NMHSs demonstrated that engagement with all related disaster management services, agreement on roles and responsibilities, and effective communication are critical if climate services are to be effectively integrated into early warning systems. For example, the proximity of the undersea earthquake meant that many of those woken and alerted had no time to reach higher ground.

A further lesson learnt is that it takes a long time for communities in the Pacific SIDS: It is that it takes years to establish trust in new systems and practices, and information alone, no matter how well presented, does not produce behaviour change. This Programme’s point of difference is that each output reinforces others, addressing all barriers at once. The Programme also aims to build capacity for climate services through permanent behaviour changes from multiple entry points. As demonstrated in the below figure, the Programme is aligned with the priorities set out by the Pacific Island Meteorological Strategy 2017-2028 and addresses its priorities in a comprehensive manner in the five countries while reinforcing the coordinating role of the Pacific Meteorological Council (PMC).

## Problem Statement

The livelihoods and economies of Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, Republic of the Marshall Islands and Tuvalu depend heavily on the ocean and climate-sensitive sectors such as fisheries, tourism and agriculture. Changes in sea level, temperature and rainfall patterns are expected to have a significant impact on the availability of water, agricultural yields and availability of arable land. Fisheries are under threat owing to loss of coral reef, marine biodiversity decline and increased competition from other countries.

Livelihoods, assets and infrastructure are threatened by several climate-related hazards. Current forecasts are not sufficiently reliable and downscaled for use in disaster risk management. As recognised in the Pacific Islands Meteorological Strategy 2017-2026, National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS) underpin economic growth and sustainable development in the region and yet their capacity to provide climate information services is limited by a lack of resources, infrastructure and access to the safety and livelihoods of Pacific Island populations. However, communication of the information is not reliably reaching those who need it.

The Programme will address constraints including gaps in geographical coverage (e.g., of ocean areas and outer islands); lack of capacity to deal with multiple aspects of climate-related risks and hazards; lack of end-to-end

## Without the Programme Scenario

Without the proposed Programme, a "Business as usual" scenario is likely to continue, characterized by the provision of basic and outdated climate data and information. NMHIs will continue to generate basic weather forecasts and useful seasonal rainfall predictions but their information will be based on incomplete data transmitted from remote islands and other sources. The quality of this information will be poor.

The roles of agencies responsible for disaster preparation and warnings will often remain unclear and they will operate reactively in the absence of reliable forecasts. Data critical to adaptation planning will be lost or forgotten, and decision-making processes will be slow and inefficient. Climate data will become less accurate, less and less reliable but will not be able to understand the technically worded information available, or how these relate to their own communities.

NMHIs' traditional development partners will continue their support, addressing some of the obstacles to effective adaptation, but underlying barriers will remain and undermine the sustainability of efforts. Severe humanitarian crises are likely to occur more frequently as extreme events become more common and less predictable. Maladaptation and inadequate disaster responses are easy to result, and the five countries' development progress will be halted or even reversed.

## Preferred solutions

The preferred solution is to implement integrated climate and ocean information services and impact-based MHEWS in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI, and Tuvalu, simultaneously addressing the major barriers to coherent systems. This will address the common vulnerabilities of the island populations, their livelihoods and assets, and the ecosystems on which they depend. The five countries have indicated interest in adopting demand-driven solutions that enable them to generate information and multi-hazard early warning systems at the same time. This can be achieved through:

- Strengthened delivery model for climate information services and MHEWS covering oceans and islands;
- Strengthened observations, monitoring, modelling and prediction of climate and its impacts on ocean areas and islands;
- Improved community preparedness, response capabilities and resilience to climate risks; and
- Enhanced regional knowledge management and cooperation for climate services and MHEWS.

The Programme will ensure that:

- Governments implement policies, plans and (early) actions based on timely and credible climate information and risk analysis; investments in infrastructure will be climate-informed;
- public health agencies will have the capacity to respond effectively to climate-related health risks;
- Integrated climate information and early warning systems are in place in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau,
Republic of the Marshall Islands and Tuvalu and sustained in response to continued demand, in recognition of their practical value; and

**B**

* Communities adopt new climate-resilient livelihood practices by using improved climate information, risk knowledge and early warning systems based on the creation of trust through long-term, consistent demonstration of the efficacy of climate science, so that communities use climate information to protect their livelihoods from adverse effects of climate change.

This transformation to increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the populations in the five Pacific SIDS to climate change threats is expected to reduce the damages and losses incurred through increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. The assumption behind this transformation is that the difference in effective early warning can be delivered and acted upon hours or days earlier than previously, including through Forecast-based Action.

## Barriers

The main barrier to the implementation of integrated and effective climate and ocean information services and MIEWS in the five programme countries include:

* Lack of legislative, regulatory and policy frameworks and delivery models for climate services (e.g. legislation establishing the role and responsibilities of the weather service and ensuring its funding; its relationship with other agencies and the relationship with Disaster Management Authorities and other national partners);
* Inadequate observation networks and limited climatic and ocean-specific information (including the lack of a comprehensive network in geographical coverage of monitoring and observing stations);
* Limited preparedness, understanding and use of climate and ocean information (including limited access to expertise for analysis of climate risks and hazards, climate modelling and hydro-meteorological data; inadequate training on climate services and their application; limited awareness of early warnings and end-users; limited capacity of vulnerable communities and sectors to interpret and use climate information services); and
* Limited knowledge management and training for service users (e.g. in an archipelagic country such as the Cook Islands climate risks are amplified for communities on outer islands by a lack of local climate data, unreliable communication links and a lower level of awareness of both hazards and climate change).

The effect of these barriers is that island communities, policy decision-makers are not being provided with the accurate, timely and actionable climate information required to address the climate change risks and hazards facing them.

Furthermore, it should be noted that as reaffirmed by the UN General Assembly in the 2019 Political declaration of the High-Level Meeting to Review Progress Made in Addressing the Priorities of Small Island Developing States (SIDS), small island developing states remain a special case for sustainable development as they continue to face the combined challenges arising, inter alia, from their vulnerability and exposure to climate change and natural disasters, adverse effects of climate change and natural disasters. The small size, remoteness and insularity of SIDS pose daunting challenges in amongst other transport logistics. For example, domestic inter-island shipping is often unreliable due to weather conditions and the cost of air travel is high, time-consuming and expensive. Similarly, air travel in the Pacific often involves long-haul, multi-leg and expensive flights in the


---
**Page 58**
---

absence of direct flight connectivity.[27] Therefore, the cost of travel, general logistics and transactions in the Pacific region are comparatively higher than in many other parts of the world.

B.2. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

The Programme interventions aim to shift the paradigm away from the current "business as usual", characterised by a lack of climate information and decision support systems, towards a new paradigm in which accurate, timely and actionable climate information is used in policy, planning and response actions, and enables sectors and communities in the five Pacific island countries to adapt to increasing climate variability and change. The following barriers are identified as being key challenges that need to be overcome in the five Pacific SIDS covered by this Programme:

1. Lack of legislative, regulatory and policy frameworks and tools for climate services, at all levels (national, sub-national and local), including the Paris Agreement and its Sustainable Development Goals (SDG) 13 target 13.b, which aims to "Promote mechanisms for raising capacity for effective climate change-related planning and management in least developed countries and small island developing States".

2. Inadequate observations networks and limited access to data, as highlighted by the Paris Agreement Article 7c, which outlines the need for "Strengthening socio-economic knowledge on climate, including research, systematic observation of the climate system and early warning systems, in a manner that informs climate services and supports decision-making".

3. Limited capacity underpinning climate information services, as recognised by the Sendai Framework on Disaster Risk Reduction, which highlights in 1.4 that "there is a need to address existing challenges and prepare for future ones by focusing on monitoring, assessing and understanding disaster risk, including through the development of early warning systems...". and in 17g, that "Disaster risk reduction requires a multi-hazard approach and inclusive risk-informed decision-making based on the open exchange and dissemination of disaggregated data...".

4. Limited regional cooperation mechanisms and training for climate services, as also recognised under the Paris Agreement Article 7a, which states: "The Parties shall...for the purposes of good practices, experiences and lessons learned, including, as appropriate, as these relate to science, planning, policies and implementation in relation to adaptation actions".

To address these barriers, the proposed Programme will establish a regional climate services and early warning system covering ocean and islands. This will be achieved through the following four inter-related Programme Results, which also aim at creating enabling conditions for scaling up and replicating the programme's outputs and impacts across the five Pacific SIDS covered by this Programme:

Pillars of Global Framework for Climate Services, a UN-wide initiative coordinated by the World Meteorological Organization (WMO):

1. Strengthened delivery model for climate information services and M&EWS covering oceans and islands: Among others, each country will establish a National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) based on the GFCS to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen collaboration among national institutions for enhancing the development and provision of climate services and to facilitate a long term sustainable business delivery model.

2. Strengthened observations, monitoring, modelling and prediction of climate and its effects on ocean areas and islands: Each country will establish an Ocean and Island Climate Observing System (GCOIS), of which the AE is one of the co-sponsors, and facilitate compliance with global

### Improved Early Warning Systems (EWS) and Risk Reduction

3) Improving the capacity of NMSs to provide early warning capabilities and resilience to climate risks: This will be based on priorities for Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems (MHEWS) and Disaster Risk Reduction set in the Paris Agreement, Sendai Framework and SDGs (further elaborated in section B.5.) and will contribute towards achieving the goals of the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The GCF is committed to supporting Risk-informed Early Action Partnership (REAP) on investment in "early warning system infrastructure and institutional capacity building for a 'first mile' community" and "more people are covered by new or improved early warning systems."

4) Enhanced regional knowledge management and cooperation for climate services and MHEWS: This requires strengthening the capacities of NMSs to provide climate information, including MHEWS, as identified in amongst others the Paris Agreement (Article 7.7a), Sendai Framework (IV. 34d) and SDG 17 target 6 [Enhance North-South, South-South and triangular regional and international cooperation on environmental matters by 2020]. Each of the Programme Results comprises a set of Activities and Sub-Activities that have been designed to remove specific barriers to achieving increased resilience to climate change threats. The four Programme Results are:

1) Strengthening the capacity of NMSs to provide early warning services, including MHEWS, and to build climate change resilience through institutionalizing and mainstreaming climate and ocean information into sectoral and community level planning; ensuring climate risk-informed decision making and response in disaster management and development planning; and increasing access to climate and ocean information available to countries; strengthening the capacities of their NMSs and other agencies to analyze and apply this information for improved decision-making, including through the use of new technologies.

2) Furthermore, climate services market assessments and the preparation of national policies for financing climate services are required. The Programme will support the development of a National Climate Services Strategy in each country. This strategy requires specific localized climate and ocean information, which is often not available due to lack of in-situ data or lack of local capacity to provide tailored products. Where climate and ocean information exists, it may be difficult for countries to access this information because of high costs associated with the financing. National climate and ocean services cannot advance in isolation and therefore information sharing through regional cooperation will be required on technology and products, and support for further national capacity development, fostering long-term sustainability.

3) As elaborated in section D.1 and Annex 3 (Economic Analysis report), the transformative impact of the Programme is expected to be felt over a period of approximately five years, with benefits to all segments of the population in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu to climate change threats through:

- Reduced expected losses of lives and economic assets due to the impact of extreme climate-related disasters. Economic damages and losses are expected to be reduced by 15–30%, including through increased resilience of infrastructure (e.g., roads, bridges, buildings) and protection of human settlements and properties from climate-related hazards using accurate, timely and actionable early warning services established by the Programme; and
- An increase in the number of men and females benefiting from the adoption of diversified, climate resilient livelihoods. The Programme aims to improve the resilience of up to 110,739 people (51% male and 49% female). This is the estimated entire population of the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, the

B.3. Project programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

The project objectives are:

1. To support the five Pacific Island countries in achieving sustainable development in the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands (RMI) and Tuvalu through the achievement of three key GEF outcome levels: i) Increased generation and use of climate information in decision making; ii) Improved capacity for early warning systems; iii) Enhanced climate change risk reduction processes.

The populations of the five programme countries have extensive traditional knowledge concerning adaptation to climate variability, which complements modern approaches and decision-making informed by science. This is essential for their adaptation to long-term changes. The Programme recognises the complementarity between traditional and scientific knowledge in addressing climate change impacts. It will support communities to update up-to-date climate science and risk knowledge. That science must be based on more data than is currently available, and that it should be used as a tool to inform decision making. This will ensure that evidence-based actions can afford. The Programme will affect a paradigm shift to science-informed evidence-based climate adaptation, disaster risk reduction and people-centred multi-hazard early warning system. This will be achieved by building the capacity of communities to access and use climate information, integrating it into populations and mainstreamed into community planning and decision-making processes, so that people at "the last mile" clearly understand climate change and have the capacity to prepare for and respond to its impacts.

The Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V2.0 | Page 88 of 167

B


The Programme will assist five Pacific island countries to address their common vulnerabilities and increase the resilience of their livelihoods and assets to climate change impacts and multiple climate-related hazards. The countries will integrate the use of climate and disaster risk information across and within sectors, and coordinate with other development partners in the region to ensure that the climate services are integrated into activities supported by a regional hub. The end-to-end Programme will have measurable impacts on the reduction of vulnerability to climate change. The Programme is described in detail in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2) and is summarised below.

Result 1: Strengthened delivery model for climate information services and NMHSs covering oceans and islands

The effective delivery of user-tailored climate services is essential for the provision of climate information to assist with decision-making and climate-resilient sustainable development.171 Pacific NMHS advice can significantly reduce climate risks if the advice has a recognised formal role in disaster preparation, warnings and response, as well as in other sectors such as agriculture, water resources management, forestry, fisheries and other climate-sensitive sectors. While Pacific NMHSs have competencies in monitoring and analysing climate data, they often lack the capacity to provide tailored climate information services and technical resources.172 Furthermore, all Programme countries lack clarification and/or formalisation of their NMHS's mandate and its relationship with National Disaster Management Authorities, other national institutions and international partners. In addition, while many NMHSs have a clear understanding of the key functions of their NRHIs in relation to weather services, none has an Act that adequately covers the roles of its NRHI in climate-related services.173 The only exception is Tuvalu, where the National Disaster Management Authority has the exception of Tuvalu, none of the Programme countries allocates for climate change programming in the national budget and any disaster funds are limited to immediate relief efforts rather than pre-emptive disaster risk reduction.

Therefore, Programme Result 1 will assist each country to establish a coherent National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen collaboration among national institutions for enhanced climate information services. The NFCS will ensure that climate information is available in formats that are relevant and accessible to users of the climate information and disaster risk knowledge into the decision-making of climate-sensitive sectors. Continuing research and development efforts on climate information and climate change adaptation strategies will serve the practical needs of end users. This will lead to increased understanding of and use of NMHS's information and advice, and reduced losses from climate variability and extreme events. Moreover, NMHS staff will gain a better understanding of how climate scientists-to-scientists achieve practical outcomes and ensure that essential climate information reaches "the last mile".

Long-term sustainability of climate services and disaster risk management will be achieved through strengthening the capacity of national stakeholders to implement the mandates of the NFCSs, the development of opportunities for private sector engagement and investment based on detailed market assessments, and the development of financial mechanisms to support the implementation of the Programme. NMHSs will also need to adapt their mandated services in order to mitigate weather-, climate- , and water-related risks. This will facilitate national climate funding, including through cost-recovery mechanisms from sectors, where feasible, beyond the Programme's lifespan, based on the value chain captured in the figure below.

Activity 1.1 - Strengthen institutional and policy frameworks and delivery models for climate services

Under this Activity, the Programme will establish comprehensive institutional and policy frameworks and delivery models for strengthened climate services, including the development of a National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) in each country to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen the use of climate information.

The frameworks will be supported by effective coordination mechanisms to mainstream climate risk knowledge into the decision making of climate-sensitive sectors. Moreover, each country will conduct a climate services market assessment to identify gaps and opportunities for strengthening climate services. In addition, engagement with the development of national budgets will enable justification of the value of climate services, strengthen existing funding for disaster relief and contribute to the identification of long-term sources of funds.

Sub-activity 1.1.1: Develop National Frameworks for Climate Services

## B

will put into practice the five pillars of the Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS) in the five countries as follows:

- **User Interface Platform (UIP):** a structured means for users and NMHSs as climate information providers to interact.
- **Climate Services Information System:** the mechanism through which information about climate (past, present and future) is routinely collected, stored and processed by the five NMHSs to generate products and services that inform often complex decision-making across a wide range of climate-sensitive activities.
- **Observations and Monitoring:** to be implemented under Result 2 of this Programme.
- **Research, Modeling and Prediction:** to be implemented under Result 2 of this Programme with a focus on climate modeling and prediction of its impacts on ocean areas and islands.
- **Capacity Development:** to be implemented across the four Programme Result areas (as described in section C.3).

In a series of consultative workshops with government agencies (including representatives of key climatemanagement sectors such as agriculture and food security, health, disaster risk management, energy, infrastructure, transportation, water resources, etc.), NMHSs will be invited to participate in the development of climate services co-development through to use at the local level). NMHSs will communicate what information they can generate and how it could be used. The government agencies will provide feedback on which climate information would be most useful for them, what additional information would be useful to receive, and how they would like to work with the NMHS. This will help the NMHSs understand their users' needs and agree on a set of priority climate services that can meet those needs. The NMHSs will then develop these services in collaboration with their users and other stakeholders, taking into account climate change impacts. The outcomes of the workshops will be used to establish the NCFCS and to develop a suite of sector-specific climate information products. These decisions will be informed by the results of the workshops and will reflect the needs of the users, as well as lessons learned and learnings. The process will be repeated in the second, third and fourth years with the private sector, NGOs and community representatives respectively. The NCFCS will therefore serve the following functions in the five countries:

- A platform for institutional coordination, collaboration and co-production amongst relevant technical departments at national level, sub-national levels, NMHSs, and technical experts to develop and deliver user-oriented climate services.
- Support the development of a legal framework by articulating collaboration at the national level to generate and disseminate climate services. This will be done in consultation with sub-national levels, NMHSs, and technical experts who will develop national meteorological strategies and legislation to clarify and formalize the mandate of their institutions.
- An opportunity to bridge the gap between available climate services and user needs at national, subnational and local levels through the User Interface Platform, which will continuously identify user needs for climate information products and services. The UIP will provide direct access by users to the relevant sectors, and obtain feedbacks from users on climate products and services.
- A vehicle for scientific coordination to monitor the state of the climate at the national level and disseminate this information in a timely manner for policymaker actions founded on scientific evidence through the Climate Services Information System.
- A functional chain for linking climate knowledge with action on the ground so as to maximize the application of climate information and products, including the identification and removal of bottlenecks for improved delivery of climate services.

- An opportunity for enhancing the contribution of climate science to the development of national adaptation plans, disaster risk reduction and management, Sustainable Development Goals and national development policies by enhancing the integration of climate information and products into decision-making processes.
- Also essential is the negotiation and development of institutional structures that will formalise the role of NMRIs in the provision of services to climate-sensitive sectors and in emergencies. The Programme will support each NDMI in developing a National Meteorology Strategy (NMS) with the involvement of relevant stakeholders, organisations, public broadcasters, public works departments, public health authorities) on roles and responsibilities.
- To support its NCFs, RMI plans to develop a national Meteorology Act for presentation to Parliament with the help of WMO. The Palau and RMI Weather Services Offices (WSOs) will each develop a National Meteorological Strategy (NMS). The NMS will be developed in line with the World Meteorological Organization’s (WMO) Meteorological Strategy and the Roadmap for Strengthened Climate Services into operation. RMI will document the process by which the WSO will put its proposed Meteorology Act into operation.
- Subactivity 1.2: Conduct a market assessment to explore viable opportunities for climate information services in sectors and business segments.
- This sub-activity will support the Programme countries to utilise a value chain approach for mobilising private sector investment in service delivery. The Programme will conduct a detailed market assessment in each country, which will assess these areas:
    - Involved actors in climate services – including providers, intermediaries and users;
    - Regulatory environment – including identification of policy incentives to unlock barriers to private sector investment in climate information and early warning systems;
    - Supply and demand analysis – including identification of sector and business needs for climate services (for example, level of information, scales and access required);
    - Private sector engagement – including identification of private sector sponsors’ interest in the generation, transmission and dissemination of climate information; and
    - Business models – including analysis of viable business models that are successful in other countries.
- Based on the above analysis, the Programme will support the five countries to identify opportunities to develop value-added climate products and services; and potential for public-private partnerships and private sector investment. This sub-activity will also inform the development of a national policy for financing climate services in Sub-activity 1.1.4.
- Subactivity 1.1.3 Mainstream climate risk knowledge into government, public and community sectors through a value chain approach of linking climate knowledge to action at the local level. This sub-activity will support the five countries to establish Pacific Islands Climate Outlook Forums (PICOF) in each country for the dry season and after the regional Pacific Islands Climate Outlook Forum (PICOF). These forums will provide a platform for dialogue between climate service providers, disaster risk management actors and all relevant stakeholders at the national level. The PICOFs will also be used to mainstream climate knowledge into government at the local level. This will facilitate users to better understand the content and uncertainties in climate forecasts and projections, and how these can be applied to inform decision-making processes at the local level.
- Each NCF will be preceded by stakeholder consultations to ensure that the PICOFs are relevant and useful for stakeholders. The PICOFs will also provide a platform for dialogue between climate service providers and disaster risk management actors and all relevant stakeholders at the national level. This will facilitate service providers to better understand and coordinate how climate and disaster risk information is used, and thus develop tailored climate products for each sector.

## B

### Workshops Sub-activity in Sub-Activity 1.1.1: As each group – public and private sector organizations, community groups – takes part in the workshops, the relevance and practical applications of the Climate Outlook Forums will be better understood and acted upon by its members.

Before the workshops are held, a series of meetings will take place with the National Climate Sector Action and Communication Plans for key sectors. The plans will address sector-specific needs for information services relating to both disaster risk reduction and management, and effective climate change adaptation; and will outline a process to test the best way forward in each country.

A National Climate Change Information Services Training Programme will be developed and delivered to facilitate uptake of climate information services by key service providers.

Furthermore, each country will undertake specific climate risk knowledge mainstreaming activities based on their needs. These include: (i) conducting vulnerability assessments and incremental climate change costing to determine the most cost-effective adaptation measures; (ii) developing a national climate change action plan in line with the NDCs and the National Climate Sector Action Plans; (iii) engaging the Nauru’s Parliamentarians on the climate outlook and use of its data to inform climate-resilience planning; and (iv) conducting crop climate change risk assessments to inform agricultural crops planning in Tuvalu.

The national climate change information services will be integrated into existing disaster risk management systems, and ensure that climate change is included as a key element in disaster risk information on the agendas of sector agencies. Their responsibilities include regular liaison with ministerial and departmental staff, making relevant data discoverable by agencies and their development partners for planning purposes; ensuring that climate change considerations are reflected in all relevant policy documents and evaluation processes. This will also contribute to generating and accessing essential data and information for disaster risk management and climate change adaptation at local level.

### Sub-activity 1.1.4: Develop national policies for financing climate services

This sub-activity will provide the foundation for the establishment of a financially sustainable business model for climate services in the five Programme countries. Based on the NHCs established under Sub-Activity 1.1.1, this sub-activity will develop and implement national financial mechanisms (NFM) to finance climate services, which will facilitate that the five NHBs have the means to sustain and ensure the ongoing operation of their mandated services in order to mitigate weather-, climate-, and water-related risks.

Currently, there is no dedicated funding for climate services in any of the five Programme countries. However, RMF organized a workshop in May 2019 to explore the setup of a national climate finance mechanism, in collaboration with the NDC Partnership. The workshop resulted in agreement on a “Climate Finance Mechanism” (CFM) as part of the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP). A Climate Finance subgroup in RMF is responsible for ensuring that development of the national policy is aligned with and facilitates climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction activities.

In 2015, Tuvalu established a “Tuvalu Survival Fund” (TSF) – was established in 2015. The TSF is intended to support both immediate disaster response efforts and longer-term responses for future climate change impacts and natural disasters. A phased approach will be taken by RMF and the NHBs to ensure that the resources of the external Fund are used to ensure that Tuvalu’s national climate services are adequately and sustainably financed.

Given the specific circumstances of the five Programme countries relating to their small, remote, small economies, remoteness, and vulnerability to natural disasters, a set of country-specific financial policies will be carefully developed with the support of the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) to ensure their tailoring to the requirements of PACS. In line with World Bank guidance,123 the financial policies will cover the following elements:

123 WMO. 2015. Climate Change and Disaster Survival Fund Act 2015

Opportunities for greater cooperation between the public and private sectors and academia given that many economic sectors increasingly depend on meteorological information for safe and efficient operations.

Opportunities for win-win situations that fulfill the public sector responsibility to help the economically disadvantaged while meeting the needs of enterprises for climate services. To this end, the Program will ensure partner governments are made aware of the economic value of climate information in, for instance, reducing the costs associated with extreme weather events such as floods and droughts; water to outer islands in drought, and reducing the costs of recovery from cyclone damage.

Opportunities to coordinate and/or integrate financing for climate services and disaster risk management to strengthen national capacities. This will include opportunities for co-financing of preparedness activities, which are often limited to ad-hoc donor funding. This would facilitate a more efficient and streamlined approach to implementing overlapping actions for climate change adaptation and disaster risk management.

Identification of the elements for a sustainable financial model for NMHSs based on the climate services value chain. The Program will support NMHSs in developing business cases that demonstrate how they can protect society from the adverse effects of severe weather (a public good typically supported by governments, for which predictable national budget allocations need to be ensured) but also in providing specialized services and products that are not provided by government agencies. This would provide an opportunity for cost-recovery from governmental and non-governmental sources beyond the Program.

Potential to establish National Climate Funds (NCFs) as mechanisms that support countries to manage their engagement with climate finance by facilitating the collection, blending, coordination, and of accounting of funds from a variety of sources. The establishment of NCFs in developing countries using these funds could have the following goals: i) collect sources of funds and direct them toward climate change activities that provide national priorities; ii) blend finance from public, private, multilateral and bilateral sources to support climate change activities; iii) ensure that climate change is integrated into country-wide climate change activities to ensure that climate change priorities are effectively implemented. The establishment of NCFs could be done in a variety of ways, including through partnerships for the development of strategic plans for climate finance, ii) provide financial control; iii) manage performance measurement, including monitoring and reporting on activities and results, and iv) provide technical assistance to support climate change management.

Potential for continued support from the Synergistic Observations Financing Facility (SOFF) as part of the African Hydrological Development, which was launched in December 2019 by 12 international organizations including UNEP. The SOFF is envisaged to ensure provision of basic systematic observations as a global public good by providing equitable, predictable, sustainable, and performance-based financing for the Global Climate Observing System (GCOS) observing systems. The SOFF will use observational data as per the Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) standard adopted by the WMO Congress in 2019. The SOFF is also envisaged to provide financial support for capacity building and defining the obligation for countries to implement a minimal set of surface-based observations for which international exchange of observational data will be mandatory. Further details are provided under Activity 2.1.

Result 2: Strengthened observations, monitoring, modelling and prediction of climate and its impacts on ocean areas and islands

Despite its critical significance in driving the global climate system and generating severe weather events, the oceanic and sub-oceanic regions remain a data-sparse region. The current sparse observational network in the Pacific Islands (PIs), Nauru, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu is insufficient to adequately provide the climate and ocean information required at national, sectoral and community levels. Several observation sites for ground and upper-air observations are no longer operational due to equipment obsolescence; lack of technical and financial capacity for observations and maintenance; the ongoing need to fund infrastructure and equipment; and the fact that many PIs use rudimentary observing programs and limited communications infrastructure. Maintenance in many cases is not a priority, and in-situ measurements is essential.

Programmes will enable the five countries to expand their observation networks with robust and well-supported equipment, collecting data from critical sites such as distant inhabited outer islands and from ocean areas. Information derived from systematic upper-air observations in the Pacific region, using meteorological coastal stations, can provide a more complete picture of the climate system, including its variability and extremes, systems, as well as other weather and climate-related services.240 The Programme will enhance the capacity to collect and disseminate data on oceanic and sub-oceanic regions through the use of the WMO Global Basic Observing Network (GBOON), which represents a new approach in which basic surface-based observations are designed, defined and monitored at the global level241 and emphasises the need for accurate and reliable measurements. The GBOON will provide a more complete picture of the climate system; observations will further enhance extreme weather monitoring and early warning systems, and support improved understanding of oceanic and sub-oceanic regions. This is particularly important in the Western and Central Pacific which not only benefit the host countries but also the entire global forecasting capability.240 One of the key objectives of the GBOON is to provide a more complete picture of the climate system; this is a priority for Pacific Islands, in response to user demand for ocean climate information, particularly relating to fishing conditions and safety.241 The ocean is a fundamental climate regulator and is the major influence on the climate of the region. It also has significant impacts on the economies of the PI countries, render most modern equipment and consumables infallible to obtain.

This Result will support implementation of the Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS) as outlined in the GFCS for Cook Islands, Nauru, Palau, RMI, Tuvalu, Vanuatu and Tonga. This is fully in line with the Pacific Roadmap for Strengthened Climate Services 2017–2026, which proposes several regional and national actions within the GFCS pillars as shown in the below figure.

**Activity 2.1 – Enhance infrastructure and technical support for observations and monitoring**

The Programme will strengthen infrastructural and in-country technical capacity to enhance observations and monitoring networks in all five countries. Amongst others, this will be achieved through the installation of new and replacement equipment, training on data management and analysis, and ensuring that the countries’ climate and weather observations are compliant with WMO Global Basic Observing Network (GBOON) standards; training and support for observations, monitoring and maintenance; and establishment of robust quality assurance systems.

The Global Basic Observing Network (GBOON) concept was approved at the 18th World Meteorological Congress in June 2019 in order to ensure a robust supply of real-time observational data from the entire global observing system. The GBOON is designed to provide high-quality, reliable and timely observations that are essential for the quality of forecasts and climate analyses, which in turn are essential for public services that help save lives and livelihoods. It also provides a framework for defining the minimum set of observations and observational requirements for global NWP as defined by WMO technical experts, and specify in quantitative terms the accuracy and precision required to meet these requirements. The GBOON is based on a series of principles which are described in the GBOON document. These principles are based on which measurement techniques to use. Based on the GBOON concept adopted by the World Meteorological Congress, the detailed GBOON regulatory material is currently under development and will be submitted to the Executive Secretary for approval at the 19th WMO Congress in June 2021. The draft regulatory material is thus yet to be approved, for the purpose of this Programme, the draft regulatory material that served as a basis for the GBOON concept was used. The GBOON will provide a framework for defining observations-related activities, as discussed with the five countries and the AE and agreed upon with WMO; **[2]** will help ensure that the activities proposed represent suitable, feasible and optimal solutions for the national


---
**Page 73**
---

basic observing networks and their contributions to the global public good provided by the NEIP systems regardless of any potential further refinements of the GBN requirements.

As a priority of the Alliance for Hydrological Development in 2020, the Systematic Observing Framework Facility (SOF) was established. The SOF is designed to provide a framework that will enable massive improvements over current country-based financing of basic observations and the value of these observations as a global public good. It will provide long-term financial and technical assistance to developing countries - prioritising Africa, South Asia, and Latin America - in order to establish and maintain GBN compliant observing networks using a fragmented and time-bound modeling, model to achieve and maintain GBN compliance and data sharing at the global level. This will enable early forecasts of, early warnings and climate information products which are essential for effective climate action.

With the objective to advance the concept and design of the SOF, the GCF approved funding in 2019 for a Working Group (WG) to develop a conceptual framework for the SOF. The WG was established as part of the SOF. Group 2 is working on implementing case studies, including proposing concrete gap mitigation activities based on existing investment opportunities in selected countries. In depth country case studies are being undertaken to identify and prioritize gaps in GBN compliance and data sharing at the global level. This will provide cost estimates and estimates of the resulting number of stations to be newly established, rehabilitated or upgraded. The case study approach is also expected to provide a more detailed cost estimate for establishing and maintaining GBN compliance in the developing world.

The five Programme countries were selected as initial case studies for the GBN country gap analyses on a representative sample basis. Based on the preliminary dataset and discussion with WMO, the following outcomes are highlighted:

* **GBN obligations should take into account the unique circumstances of SIDS with small land masses and limited resources (for example, the land area of Cook Islands (240 km²) constitutes just 0.12 % of its EEZ (1,947,760 km²).**

* **The observations from SIDS are of high value for the global observing network – the SOF Working Group estimates that the GBN observations data in the south-west Pacific are estimated to account for 17% of global forecast improvements.**

* **The Programme will ensure that all inhabited islands / atolls in the five countries will have an observing station.**

* **GBN compliance in Pacific SIDS will be achieved through a mix of surface-based Automatic Weather Stations (AWS), marine observations (e.g. through equipment that the Programme will install under Activity 2.1) and satellite observations (e.g. from the GCF-supported constellation of satellites – and selected upper air observations). Together, these will ensure GBN coverage of land surface and ocean areas in the five countries through a significant increase in density of observation points.**

As case studies are being developed, the Systematic Observing Framework Facility is demonstrating for GBN as a global public good, the five Programme countries are in a leading position to demonstrate the value of investing in GBN to protect local communities and benefit local populations, but also as a critical investment in climate resilience and adaptation. The SOF Working Group estimates that 90% of the funding support from the GCF is expected to be available to continue maintaining GBN standards in the five Programme countries.

Sub-objective: Enhance national observations and monitoring networks on GBN standards and establish QMS

The Programme countries will extend the geographical coverage of their climate and weather observations through enhancement of infrastructure and local technical capacity in alignment with draft GBN provisions for surface-based observations. Each NMHS has committed to securing sound Operations and Maintenance (O&M) for its expanded hydrometeorological networks during and for up to 20 years after the end of the Programme

### Activity 1.2 – Strengthen ocean and climate modelling and impact-based forecasting

Under this Activity, the Programme will establish end-to-end ocean services and strengthened climate information and early warning systems in all five countries in recognition of the fact that Pacific island countries are among the most vulnerable to climate change impacts. The Cook Islands has a coastline of approximately an average factor of 300 to 1, and which are rich in biodiversity and natural resources. The Programme will develop a new ocean modelling framework, along with web-based portals for climate and ocean data. Further, it will establish end-to-end ocean services that provide access to the latest information on climate services and products relevant to climate-sensitive sectors from government agencies to community level users.

# Sub-activity 1.1: Establish ocean information services

This sub-activity will develop end-to-end ocean services in all five Programme countries through the establishment of ocean observations and monitoring, strengthened ocean modelling and prediction, and tailored decision support tools. The programme will provide a comprehensive suite of ocean observation products that will enable the NMHSs to engage with the marine sector within their respective countries and provide information vital for safe and effective industry operation. The Programme will facilitate that NMHSs have access to relevant ocean observations and forecast products in near real-time, including satellite-based ocean colour imagery accompanied by targeted training and capacity building. It is essential that guidance is provided to NMHSs and representatives on how to access and interpret ocean and climate data, which can then inform relevant decision-making.

The following interventions will contribute to the achievement of WMO standards for marine and oceanographic services:

- Establish ocean observations and monitoring through the installation and maintenance of surface wave and environmental buoys for in-situ measurements of a wide range of physical and environmental oceanic variables (e.g. real-time temperature, salinity, dissolved oxygen, waves, pH, etc.); multi-polarisation microwave scatterometer (MWS) and high-resolution (HR) radar; to provide real-time 2D current and wave data (Nixus); precision sea surface temperature (SST), wind speed and direction, air-sea fluxes, and ocean colour imagery (AquaPAM); breaking-zone current profiler and wave directional system (Palau) and unmanned deep ocean observing vehicle (Cook Islands); installation of automatic weather stations (AWSs) on four inter-island shipping routes; deployment of a multi-sensor buoy to measure surface temperature and wave height (Cook Islands); and routine deployment of an unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) for in-situ measurements of wind speed, direction, air-sea fluxes, and SST (Palau). This will also enhance capacity for remote sensing derived coastal observations such as water quality mapping (e.g. turbidity, chlorophyll, coloured dissolved oxygen meter etc.), marine habitat mapping and abundance estimation (e.g. coral reef health), and the development of a real-time ocean colour index to calibrate satellite-derived ocean observations to improve understanding of local impacts and establish prior monitoring capabilities.

- Strengthen ocean modelling and prediction to improve local ocean and coastal impact monitoring programmes and enhance capacity for risk-based planning and impact-based early warning systems. With the support of regional partners, NMHSs will be able to develop a comprehensive suite of static and dynamic downscaling of localised ocean information and multivariate forecast modelling based on satellite-derived data. The programme will also provide training in the use of these tools to develop an ocean modelling framework utilising a three-step methodology: (i) analysis of long-term large-scale ocean and atmospheric data to map spatial and temporal variabilities, and identify annual, monthly and daily patterns; (ii) development of a suite of models for short- and long-term prediction of surface wave, circulation and inundation models at country, island and lagoon scales; and (iii) establishment of a long-term monitoring system to validate the model outputs against in-situ ocean and coastal impact data. The framework will enable NMHS staff to assess and predict short and long-term lagoon health-related hazards and risks, and be tailored to specific sites to identify large-scale environmental changes.

The Programme will establish and develop in-house capacity and capabilities for ocean modelling and prediction through the development of a suite of models that can be run at different time scales (days/weeks to seasonal scale) through technical training and workshops, including the use of satellite


---
**Page 78**

(e.g., COPERNICUS) and HF radar. Engagement of the Copernicus Marine Service will support lagoon health monitoring through a combination of remote sensing, in-situ environmental sampling and dynamic modeling. In Palau, the Programme will build capacity for ocean modelling through the Pacific Islands Climate Change Adaptation Model (PICAM) project. PICAM will provide real-time wave forecast models; and (i) long-term future climate scenarios to ascertain the most at-risk coastlines and advise on adaptation measures; (ii) a suite of tools to support decision-making in coastal management services to empower ocean users and stakeholders in the U.S.-affiliated Pacific Islands, including Palau and Nauru. The Programme will also develop a regional forecast system for the Western Pacific region, including development of wave run-up forecast tools that are already being used by RMI to predict high sea levels and wave inundation. In RMI, the Programme will focus on ocean modelling and prediction systems, as well as developing a coastal flood warning system to support decision-making. ###### To deliver a free Sentinel-2 derived lagoon health monitoring product. In Palau and RMI, the Programme will develop web-based national “dashboards” including (where available) real-time observations, simulations and forecasts for key ocean parameters / factors to be calibrated using in-situ data (from buoys and data loggers). The dashboards will integrate data from a range of sources, including existing data from and be integrated into a regional dashboard, which will support the development of the WMO Regional Ocean Observing System (ROOS) for the Western Pacific region. The Programme will also develop web-based national “dashboards” including (where available) real-time observations, simulations and forecasts for key ocean parameters / factors to be calibrated using in-situ data (from buoys and data loggers). The dashboards will integrate data from a range of sources, including existing data from and be integrated into a regional dashboard, which will support the development of the WMO Regional Ocean Observing System (ROOS) for the Western Pacific region. The Programme will also develop web-based national “dashboards” including (where available) real-time observations, simulations and forecasts for key ocean parameters / factors to be calibrated using in-situ data (from buoys and data loggers). The dashboards will integrate data from a range of sources, including existing data from and be integrated into a regional dashboard, which will support the development of the WMO Regional Ocean Observing System (ROOS) for the Western Pacific region.

####### As many ongoing facilities and programmes for ocean information services and early warning systems. The dashboards will also integrate data from the global tide gauge network (sea level), drifting floats and observing system arrays (SSA) and moorings (providing real-time measurements of temperature, salinity, current speed and direction). In Cook Islands, the Programme will develop a suite of ocean observation and monitoring data into actionable information via decision-making tools tailored to fully cover the needs of forecasters.

######## Information, will be held at the annual Pacific Islands Climate Outlook Forum. This will also provide a mechanism for wide exposure of the interventions.


Sub-activity 2.Z.2. Enhance climate information and impact-based forecasting


---
**Page 79**
---

The Green Climate Fund proposal document outlines several initiatives to enhance NMHS capacity for tailored climate services and products relevant to climate-sensitive sectors from government agencies at the community level.

### Enhance forecasting-in-country capacity and systems:
- The five countries do not currently undertake forecasting in-country but use forecasts provided by other national meteorological organisations such as the Regional Specialised Meteorological Centre – a section of the Fijii Meteorological Service – and the Pacific Climate Change Science Programme (PCCSP) of the Australian Bureau of Meteorology and the National Oceanic Administration (NOAA).
- To enhance in-house forecasting capacity, the use of data and information for preparing the tropical cyclone season and for climate extremes such as droughts and floods.
- The Pacific Climate Change Science Programme will conduct training on forecasting using high-quality climate model output from the Australian Bureau of Meteorology and the National Oceanic Administration (NOAA).
- To improve the ability of NMHS to utilise all of the already existing high-quality climate information available to them, the programme will provide training in the use of these data.
- The programme aims to develop a suite of products that can be used by NMHS to make their forecasts more reliable and accurate. This will be achieved through creating value-added products such as graphics, maps, and reports to explain climate forecasts and climate model information (for example, Niu and Tovarai will develop user-friendly forecast information for the Solomon Islands, Vanuatu, Fiji, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, Tonga, Kiribati, Tuvalu, Niue, and COPERNICUS) through developing skills in image analysis and assimilation of observations into climate models.
- The programme will also provide training on the use of ocean prediction services. Global ocean products are often too coarse to provide reliable information in Pacific Island countries. Under this Programme, high-resolution ocean forecast systems will be developed for NMHS to generate more accurate and reliable ocean forecasts and associated ocean prediction products. The new ocean forecast system will be based on meta-models providing a lightweight approach that can be used by NMHS.
- A lightweight ocean forecasting system will be established in each country with the forecast system hosted by the NMHS, thereby increasing country ownership of the Programme's outputs.

### Introduce impact-based forecasting:
- Forecasting that considers the vulnerability of people, livelihoods and assets as well as consideration of the hydrometeorological hazard – to support NMHS staff in building their capacity to understand the relationships between the spatial and temporal variations in observed climate hazards and the impacts on human health and wellbeing.
- The programme will work with Disaster Management Officers and other partner agencies and user communities. Building on the ocean forecast system, the programme will develop a suite of products that can be used by NMHS to generate more accurate and reliable ocean forecasts and associated ocean prediction products.

### Establish climate science and analysis capacity:
- To support the enhancement of in-country forecasting systems, NMHS staff will build a strong scientific and methodological foundation for NMHS through training and mentoring attachments covering climatology and oceanography. Early-career scientists will be supported by the Pacific Climate Change Science Programme through the provision of customised Young Scientist Support Program (supported by APCC), including highly-in-depth training for 2–3 months exploring various climate prediction and analysis techniques such as downscaling, precipitation bias correction, and seasonal forecasting.

**Page 80**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 76 OF 167

## B

### Facilitate access to climate data and information through the development of tailored products and services for climate-sensitive sectors.

This will include the Climate Risk Early Warning System (CREWS) and Early Action Rainfall Watch (EAR Watch) in Niue, Palau and Tuvalu; and hydrological early warning systems in Cook Islands. The Department is in the context of developing data and information products that will be useful to those sectors in Tuvalu, Niue and Palau. These will include climate risk assessments, vulnerability assessments, and adaptation strategies. The CREWS will be one of the much-expanded climate information services and will focus on science climate data and analysis for new functions. The climate experts will also be responsible for sector and inter-ministry coordination and the development of these products.

### Introduce customised climate information and early warning generation through enhancing existing climate prediction systems to generate local station-based temperature outlooks, provide monthly rainfall forecasts, and develop a seasonal climate forecast system. This will include information and variables upon request – and based on sector needs identified in stakeholder workshops (Sub-activity 2.3.1). The Department is also in the context of developing a Climate Information Management System generated by the existing climate data management system (CDMS). NMHS staff in Niue and Tuvalu will be supported to undertake real-time analysis of climatic and other environmental variables, develop customised climate information products, and provide early warning services. This will enhance capacity for communication of up-to-date climate risk information to key stakeholders and facilitate risk-informed early action. The Department is also in the context of developing a Climate Information Management System using existing data management software. The availability of a decade-long reliable dataset from a specific location will make it possible to discover long-term trends and to make projections of future changes.

#### Act 2.3 – Harmonise climate data and information management

Under this Activity, the Programme will integrate climate data and information into decision-making and planning in climate-sensitive sectors. This will include harmonisation of climate data and information strategies in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau and Tuvalu. Sub-activity 2.3.1 Establish and implement national climate data and information strategies.

In collaboration with the Pacific Climate Change Services (PCCS) each country will develop a National Data and Information Strategic Action Plan for improved climate-related data management, governance and access. The plan will include an assessment of existing climate data and information systems, including sex- and age-disaggregated, where possible and/or relevant. Building on the GEF-funded “inform” project, a decision support tool will be developed to assist countries in identifying gaps in their climate data and information relevant to climate change into previously established Environmental Data Portals. This will be implemented in close cooperation with PCCS. The Department will also develop a national portal for climate data and information that can analyse and publicise the existence and value of these portals’ contents, and the Programme will build capacity for countries to use the data, with technical support from SPREEP. Two immediate benefits will be greater access to climate data and information in the Pacific region and improved tracking and reporting of progress against the UNFCCC and other climate-related multi-lateral environmental agreements. The Department is also in the context of developing a Climate Information Management System (CIMS) for Niue, Palau and Tuvalu.

The Programme will support countries to develop national data and information strategies that are aligned with their NDCs and other relevant international commitments. This will include the development of climate risk assessments, vulnerability assessments, adaptation strategies, and sector-specific early warning systems (for some sectors and countries directly through this Program’s inputs), the potential will be created for overlay of datasets such as climate and disease incidence in such a way that risks can be predicted.

**Page 81**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE OF 167 B

National Fisheries Offices (NSOs) are fully engaged with and leading the implementation of the "Inform" project through: i) setting up national data management and governance networks; ii) facilitating the collection and management of national environmental databases and reporting tools; iii) design and use of cloud-based national environmental databases and reporting tools; iv) development of national environmental database templates. Lessons learnt from the "Inform" project will be utilised when working with NSOs and other data custodians as part of the Programme.

## Result 3: Improved community preparedness, response capabilities and resilience to climate change

Vast ocean areas and widely dispersed islands mean that isolated communities are under considerable threat from climate-related hazards and increasingly frequent extreme climate events. Making climate information available in a timely manner is critical for these communities to improve their preparedness and response capabilities, representing a significant challenge. Therefore, this Result will focus on strengthening the capacity of communities to access and use climate information to support sustainable disaster risk management actions, and building local capacity to improve community preparedness and disaster reduction for multi-hazard risks.

Programme Result 3 contributes to the attainment of the overall Goal of the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction: "The substantial reduction of disaster risk and losses in lives, livelihoods and health and in the economic, social and environmental assets of communities at all levels". In particular, the Programme will contribute to the Framework's Global Target (g) "Substantially increase the availability of and access to multi-hazard early warning systems and disaster risk information and services".

The Programme will also contribute to the outcomes of the 2019 UN Climate Action Summit, in particular targets 3 and 4 of the Risk-informed Early Action Partnership (REAP), on investment in "early warning system infrastructure", and on increasing the number of people active in "last-mile communities" and more people are covered by new or improved early warning systems.

Furthermore, its Result will introduce Forecast-based Financing (FbF) in the five countries as an innovative mechanism whereby early actions at community and government level are pre-planned based on credible forecasts, and are funded and implemented before a disaster strikes to minimize losses and damages caused by climate-related hazards and reduce the need for humanitarian assistance in their aftermath.
