particularly focus on strengthening last-mile communication systems to ensure that people and communities in remote locations receive warnings in advance of impending hazard events. NMHSs will be supported to develop a range of communications products tailored to end-users at the community level. National Disaster Management Authorities (NDMAs) and other disaster management agencies, as well as community-based organizations will play a key role in enhancing preparedness and response capabilities.

### Sub-activity 3.1.1: Strengthen EWS organisational and decision-making processes

This sub-activity will ensure the effective and coordinated delivery of early warning services through strengthening NMHSs' organisational structures, including the development of inter-agency agreements with civil society organisations and other key actors. In conjunction with the establishment of National Frameworks for Climate Services (NFCSS) in all Pacific Island Countries and Territories (PICTs), this sub-activity will be included and included in standard operating procedures, such as the Early Action Protocols to be developed in Sub-activity 3.3.2. Warning communication strategies will be developed to ensure coordination between NMHSs and other key actors at national level, including NDMAs, disaster management agencies, local governments, and women's groups. The communication strategies will include the development of community feedback mechanisms for continuous improvement of early warning services through engagement with communities networks. At the start of Programme implementation, an in-country deep dive study on gender and community stakeholders will be conducted to facilitate that the design of EWS organisational and decision-making processes are inclusive and that women's voices are heard, ensuring that concerns and capabilities of different gender groups.

### Sub-activity 3.1.2: Strengthen communication systems to reach the last-mile

This sub-activity will enhance connectivity and communication systems to facilitate that climate information and early warning services can be delivered effectively in all PICTs, including small island states and remote islands populations). This will include agreement on a glossary of climate and weather terms in local languages, and the routine use of the terms in television weather reports, which will enhance understanding of climate change by communities. NMHSs will also participate in the Pacific Islands Climate Change Adaptation Network (PICCAN) and the international Hydrographic Organization's Safety of Life at Sea (SOLAS) convention.

- Develop last-mile communication strategies based on understanding of last-mile connectivity (which population groups can be reached by different communication channels) and tailored to the differential needs of communities in urban, peri-urban, rural, small island states, and remote islands populations).
- Upgrade last-mile communications infrastructure through equipment maintenance and upgrades; establishment of backup systems and processes; and the development of additional communication channels for reaching communities (e.g., mobile phones, radio, television, internet, newspapers, and websites). NMHSs will install village "compacts" posts with pointers to known locations, and signposts to show people which way to go in case of an emergency. This will be done through the development of a standard protocol for climate and early warning information.
- Enhance communication channels and early warning systems through the development of multiple-channel communication strategies (e.g., mobile phones, radio, television, internet, newspapers, and websites applications). For example, Nue and Palau will each develop an ocean and climate mobile application to provide real-time updates on weather conditions, sea level rise, storm surge, tsunamis, and ocean climate conditions. NMHS websites will also be upgraded with a user-friendly interface to enable easy access to climate and ocean information.
- Nue will increase their reach into communities with additional radio and newspaper coverage. In RMI, development of a web-based dashboard linked


---
**Page 84**
---

To further improve accessibility and understanding of RM’s ocean data and early warnings as they relate to coral reefs:

- Develop and implement localised mobile phone information communication systems with early warning capabilities for coastal communities.
- Through mobile-to-mobile communication networks, this system will provide predicted risks and alerts utilising geostationary satellite nowcast and local/regional forecast information. The system could also integrate information on locations of first-aid stations and nearby medical facilities, as well as the location of shelters and other emergency services.
- The system can be designed to handle potential and existing threats such as tropical cyclones, tsunamis, storm surges, heavy rainfall, flash flooding, torrential rainfall, coastal flooding etc. to long-term disasters (e.g. droughts). The possibility to integrate a two-way communication capability with the mobile phone information provider would allow for continued enhancements to the system based on user feedback.

- Identify opportunities to utilise private sector resources to disseminate warnings, such as mobile-cellular, television, radio and social media.
- This sub-activity will be tailored approaches to improve the content and communication of NMHS forecasts to ensure the effective dissemination of impact-based early warnings and enable communities to take advantage of the information. The system will also provide early warning messages for island communities in the event of severe weather as a result of, amongst others, the VMO Severe Weather Forecasting and DRR Dissemination System (VMSWFD) which covers Cook Islands, Niue and Tokelau participated.
- This sub-activity will be achieved through the following activities:
  - Improve warning messages to provide clear guidance for triggering response actions. The messages should be tailored to specific audiences and communities and co-design and co-produce information products, drawing on their specialised knowledge of their environment to create warnings that are actionable and effective.
  - Ensure early warning messages are disseminated through channels of which authorities issue the warnings and trust their messengers: engagement with island communities and other stakeholders to improve community awareness of which authorities issue warnings, build trust between stakeholders and improve acceptance of early warning messages.
  - Include different risks and needs of subpopulations in early warnings: the particular climate information needs of the elderly, people living with disabilities and geographically or socially vulnerable groups should be considered. It is important that warnings are gender responsive. For example, the Disability Coordination Office at the Ministry of Culture and Community Development will lead on the development of a disability-inclusive communication on disability-inclusive programming for early warning. Additionally, the delivery of disability-inclusive climate change and disaster preparedness activities and educational products will be expanded. This will include production of an educational video using a Sign Language Interpreter or other accessible formats.

### References
1) This will be delivered in the APEC Climate Center in collaboration with the Early Warning University, utilising a proven method that is currently being used by the National Disaster Management Office of the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology, Asian Disaster Preparedness Centre (ADPC) and the Phuket Viharn National Authority

**Page 85**
---

### Activity 3.2 – Enhance preparedness and response capabilities

Under this Activity, the Programme will engage with communities at the last mile to enhance preparedness and response actions at the local level. The proposed interventions will focus on knowledge and capacity building to support community-based disaster risk reduction and disaster risk management plans, community-level vulnerability assessments, and awareness of climate hazards and risks.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.1 Enhance disaster preparedness and response measures

This sub-activity will use community-based approaches to enhance risk ownership at the local level and help establish collaborative community networks for coordinated action for preparedness and response to climate-induced disasters and extreme weather events.

Community-based disaster preparedness plans include co-developing of community-based sustainable climate adaptation plans for climate resilience, using integrated scientific information and knowledge on climate hazards and risks. These will be developed in partnership with the Ministry of Natural Resources, Environment and Tourism (Bureau of Agriculture), who will ensure that knowledge gained is contextualised to the context of key sectors and how it relates to livelihood security.

#### Capacity building for disaster response

Women’s training programmes in Palau to enhance capacity to prepare for and respond to climate disasters and emergencies. The activities will be delivered in partnership with the Ministry of Natural Resources, Environment and Tourism (Bureau of Agriculture), who will ensure that knowledge gained is contextualised to the context of key sectors and how it relates to livelihood security.

#### Assessment of community-level communications and response actions

To test and optimise the effectiveness of early warning dissemination processes, preparedness and response actions. For example, Palau will conduct periodic disaster warning drills previously developed by local communities.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.2 Conduct public awareness and education campaigns on climate hazards and their potential impacts on lives and livelihoods of local populations

The Programme will implement a targeted public awareness and education campaign to enhance community knowledge and understanding of climate hazards and the potential impacts on lives and livelihoods of local populations.

Communities will be educated on how warnings will be disseminated, which sources are reliable and how to respond. The Programme will ensure the use of targeted approaches to improve awareness of climate hazards


---
**Page 86**
---

and risks specific to the needs of vulnerable groups (e.g., women, children, older people and people with disabilities). The interventions will include the delivery of school and community-based activities for climate change awareness, and training and capacity building workshops on climate hazards and disaster risk reduction. Notably, the project will also support the development of a national early warning system (EWS) through initiatives, such as climate change awareness days. Talanoa sessions on climate-related stories with elders, and knowledge sharing events with youth are planned to ensure that the information is relevant to local contexts and that it is relevant to local livelihoods and promotes awareness of how the information can be used to enhance climate resilience. The project will also support the development of a national EWS through training workshops in both its capitals and outer islands to enhance knowledge and understanding of climate hazards and potential impacts on lives and livelihoods. The workshops will build the capacity of participants to apply the knowledge gained from the training, including how to use early warning information to make informed decisions about alternative livelihoods. In the Cook Islands, the Women's committee (Au Vaie Kumiti) will work with island communities to develop a climate change adaptation plan and to enhance their ability to access early warning services by using technology to access early warning information and to contribute to local scale vulnerability assessments. The Programme will also support Au Vaie Kumiti to expand its work with young farmers, which strengthens community resilience.

Sub-activity 3.2.3. Integrate traditional knowledge into early warning services

The populations of the participating countries have extensive traditional knowledge concerning adaptation to climate variability. Application of this knowledge to decision-making informed by science will enhance adaptive capacity and reduce vulnerability. The project will support communities in developing a framework for decision making on traditional climate knowledge (TK) when they make decisions, or at best on TK in combination with official information. This will be done through the development of a national TK policy that will provide guidance on long term benefit of recognising TK that will be broadcated advice will be accepted and acted upon so that communities are better prepared to respond to climate extremes, leading to reduced socio-economic disruption and loss of life. The importance of traditional knowledge in climate change adaptation has been highlighted also in the IPCC Special Report on Global Warming of 1.5°C, which noted the cultural resilience of indigenous peoples and local communities as a key factor for reducing vulnerability.

Therefore, this sub-activity will incorporate TK into NMHS products to significantly improve forecast communication while expanding the spatial and temporal relevance of the forecasts and increasing community preparedness. The project will support communities in developing a framework for decision making on traditional climate knowledge (TK) when they make decisions, or at best on TK in combination with official information. This will be done through the development of a national TK policy that will provide guidance on long term benefit of recognising TK that will be broadcated advice will be accepted and acted upon so that communities are better prepared to respond to climate extremes, leading to reduced socio-economic disruption and loss of life. The importance of traditional knowledge in climate change adaptation has been highlighted also in the IPCC Special Report on Global Warming of 1.5°C, which noted the cultural resilience of indigenous peoples and local communities as a key factor for reducing vulnerability.

Support for TK under this Programme builds upon previous work undertaken through the Climate and Ocean Services (COS) project, which developed climate information products and their TK interventions. Under COSPAC, the Niu Meteorological Service (NMS) worked with community elders to collect and digitise TK indicators in a TK database that was used by the NMS to develop seasonal calendars and climate outlooks for communities documented under the COSPAC project. Beyond this stage, the Programme will develop seasonal calendars and climate outlooks for communities in other countries, notably Palau and Samoa, with support from Vagata Nue. Moreover, the Programme will upscale the TK work to other countries – notably Palau and Samoa - using the methodology that proved successful in COSPAC: Niu, Palau and RMI will recruit a local TK consultant who will collect TK data and develop seasonal calendars and climate outlooks for communities through workshops; engagement with relevant stakeholders; provision of community education and awareness activities; and collection and archiving of climate TK information. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 83 OF 167

## Activity 3.3 – Establish Forecast-based Financing (FbF)

Under this Activity, the Programme will establish Forecast-based Financing (FbF) in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu as an innovative mechanism whereby early actions are pre-planned based on credible forecasts of climate-related hazards and reduce the need for humanitarian assistance in their aftermath. The combination of FbF with other activities under Sub-activity 2.3 will be a key element in the overall strategy of improving disaster preparedness and enabling more efficient management of government budgets to promote the shift from traditional post-disaster response to pre-event early action, as shown in the figure below.

### Figure 27: Forecast-based financing conceptual framework

Sub-activity 3.3.1 Develop FbF Roadmaps defining thresholds and triggers
This sub-activity will develop an FbF Roadmap for each Programme country, which will identify forecasts (magnitude, probability and lead time) that can trigger humanitarian actions.

| ACTION | PREPAREDNESS | EARLY ACTION | DISASTER RECOVERY |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Forecast Trigger** | Preparedness | Early Action | Disaster Recovery |
| **FUNDING** | Grants | Emergency Fund (Action early) | Emergency Fund (Recovery) |
| **PROCESS** | Early Action development | Activation of FbF Roadmap | External funding for EFP development |
| **QA** | Scientific Committee | Validation Committee | Data sharing and learning | Operations |


---
**Page 88**
---

The first part of the Roadmap will begin with a scoping study that will cover feasible hazards to target for FbF, forecasting capability, and the institutional landscape in each of the five countries. For FbF to be a sustainable and effective mechanism, Early Action Protocols (EAPs) need to be embedded in national institutions. The Roadmap will work with key stakeholders in each country to identify these actors, who are national and/or sub-national actors (government and civil society) in each country and enter into a dialogue with them on the design of EAPs.

For the countries with a positive landscape for institutional participation in Forecast-based Financing, the second comprehensive phase of the Roadmap will be developed. This phase will consist of collaborative consultations with key institutions to delineate three key elements that would enable country-led design of an FbF mechanism:

- Possible early actions that can be triggered by existing forecasts, which aim to avoid losses and damages in an extreme event materializes.
- A timeline of the available early warning information derived from forecasts that can trigger the early actions.
- A potential structure of county-level technical working groups and institutional ownership for FbF, including funding mechanisms when necessary. *A lead national agency* in which to embed EAPs will be identified.

The FbF Roadmaps will provide a set of 10 recommendations per country for critical next steps to move forward with Forecast-based Financing. These next steps will be focused on filling capacity gaps that enable the design and implementation of FbF, including the development of early warning systems, and the design and testing process for EAPs. The Roadmaps will include the following components:

- **Stakeholder Identification** – The Programme will identify the key stakeholders to be involved in the development and implementation of FbF in each country, including international, national, regional and local actors and lead agencies.
- **Risk Assessment** – The Assessment will include individual analysis of risk factors, key hazards, past impact, exposure and vulnerability. Based on the analysis, a list of early warning systems to be identified will be provided along with an overview of different types of early actions that could be taken to mitigate risks for the identified stakeholders, in different sectors (agriculture, health, etc.).
- **Impact-based Forecasting (Triggers)** – The Trigger analysis will provide an overview of all available forecast products and their potential use as triggers for early warning systems. This analysis will also offer suggestions on impact-based forecasting products or the potential for such products to be developed in each country based on the identified hazards, exposure and vulnerability information. Close collaboration with the executing agencies of Result 2 will be required.
- **Resourceourcing** – The Assessment will identify various options for accessing funding or increasing existing resources for early warning systems and early actions. These options include an overview of potential national level funds for anticipatory actions, Disaster Risk Financing options, and humanitarian funding options.

The sub-activity will build on the scoping study commissioned by the Australian Red Cross and Red Cross Red Crescent Climate Centre (RCRCCC) to identify the main considerations of adapting FbF to the Pacific with a focus on three larger Pacific island countries (Fiji, Papua New Guinea and Solomon Islands), to adapt it to the


---
**Page 89**
---

context and capacities of the much smaller five Programme countries. In developing the FbF Roadmaps, the following key findings from the Red Cross scoping study will be taken into account:

* Forecast skill and possible warning lead times differ considerably from other countries, primarily because they are located in a region with complex topography, multiple river basins, high El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) sensitivity, and each country experiences a broad spectrum of extreme events.
* Forecast skill and timelines differ considerably for different hazards.
* Lack of historical hydrological data and modeling capability throughout the region.
* Some of the most difficult disturbances in the Pacific happen on much longer timescales than FbF typically operates under.
* Many small island nations have capacity constraints that limit possible early actions.
* Consideration of thresholds and potentially creating quality towards other forms of early warning are pervasive in the region.
* The extent to which the populations are dispersed and remote has strong implications for FbF design and implementation.

Sub-activity 3.2.1. Build capacity for FbF

In this sub-activity, up to five of the ten “next steps” that were identified for each country in their Roadmap will be developed and executed. Steps that fall within technical capability building, research or technical advice will be prioritized. Depending on the national context and the findings of the scoping study, these activities could include the following:

* Scientific collaboration with national or regional forecasters to carry out a forecast verification analysis or historical review to support the development of triggers.
* Technical support to build capacity for climate change adaptation and change mindsets:
    * Specific links between the FbF and EWEA narrative with the principles and activities embedded in other outputs of the Programme;
    * Technical support in finding ways to connect with existing regional systems, mechanisms and/or products that could provide underwriting or buy-in of FbF.
* Table-top exercise to discuss a historical extreme event and what could have been done by different actors to prevent impacts;
* Round-table discussion on financing mechanisms for critical early actions that could be part of an FbF mechanism.

Sub-activity 3.3.3. Support development of Early Action Protocols (EAPs)

In this sub-activity, the IFRC Climate Centre will support the countries that have full Roadmaps and identified appropriate national institutions to spearhead FbF (sub-activity 3.3.1) to develop Early Action Protocols (EAPs) through a participatory process. The EAPs will be developed in collaboration with the relevant national EAP focal points, who are responsible for coordinating the development of the EAP and ensuring its implementation from country to country, depending on the results of the Roadmap and the capacity building activities. Each country will develop an EAP that is tailored to their specific context and needs, including a set of life-saving actions by one actor or a more complex document with a greater variety of actors and forecast analyses.

To what should be listed in the EAP, each country lead level focal point will convene a technical working group, engaging stakeholders at all levels – including community representatives, disaster risk reduction committees, civil society organisations, local and national government departments, NGOs and private sector actors. The


---
**Page 90**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 167 OF 167

#### B

Climate change provides technical guidance to the lead agency on the process and provide quality assurance but delivery of the EAP will rest with the lead agency.

The determination of forecast-based actions will be based on: (i) precipitation, temperature time and space variability; (ii) water availability; (iii) energy, material, human and technology resources required; and (iv) access issues in providing assistance. Following identification of the most suitable forecast-based actions, the EAPs will be developed by the lead national agency.

The EAPs will include a detailed implementation plan based on the forecast and trigger information; and assign responsibilities to specific stakeholders for implementation of each action. In the case of more complex EAPs, they can also include a proposal for a Financial Mechanism, which will outline what contributions are required from the lead agency and other stakeholders (e.g., international donors) towards the cost of implementing forecast-based actions) and how they will be accessed by specific stakeholders. This sub-activity will collaborate with the “EAPs Development” sub-activity (1.4.1) to explore situations when funds for early action could be linked to government budgets. Depending on local capacity, simulations can be held to carry out a test of the actions of the EAP.

**Result 4: Enhanced regional knowledge management and cooperation for climate services**

Programme Result 8 will optimise synergies among the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands and Tuvalu. Benefits of the multi-country approach include: i) Facilitation of strategic partnerships; ii) Strengthening of institutional capacity in the region (e.g., through training, knowledge sharing); iii) Increased awareness of the Programme, leading to increased likelihood of replication of community interventions across the region; and iv) South-South cooperation for enhancing the multiples aspects of the Programme. To this end, the project will organize joint learning events with other regional partners, software and tools common to all five countries; and foster networking and mentoring among the five NMHSs. The project will also support the development of a Regional Climate Information System (RCIS) using the Weather Prediction Network (WPN), Instrumentation and Observation and Climate Services – reported by Pacific NMHSs to WMO(10) and in a study conducted by the Disaster Resilience for Pacific SIDS (RESIPAC project).


---
**Page 91**
---

In general, Pacific NMSs are aiming to improve the quality of their services and increase the number of products in existing service areas, rather than increase the provision of broader types of services. Figure 23 indicates that there is a notable demand for professionalisation of the climate, forecasting and marine services (dark cyan), whereas improved observations capacity is a priority for technical staff with 2-year or lesser qualifications (light cyan).

The Result takes advantage of the already established GEF-funded "Inform" project, implemented by UNEP and executed by SPREP, which is helping 14 Pacific Island countries to manage and use environmental data sustainably. The programme will continue to build on this work through the development of a national data validation and the interactive ICT platform into both the national processes in the five countries as well as the operation of the secretariat in multiple focal areas. Building on the platform and processes that the "Inform" project has established, the programme will also seek to increase the capacity for climate-related information communication at the wider stakeholder group and thereby increase use and demand at both the national and secretariat levels. Ultimately, this will lead to greater ownership of data management by Pacific Island countries.

The costs associated with the programme are being absorbed by the SPREP Core budget and country funding, as formally committed by SPREP. These are anticipated to be fully covered through targeted national support in several Pacific Island countries for data management, use and sharing; and the Programme will continue to give this national commitment and ownership over its implementation period.

Activity 4.1 - Enhance regional data, knowledge management and cooperation

Under this activity, the programme aims to enhance data management and cooperation among the five countries to improve data and knowledge management, including establishment of an interactive ICT platform and regional data centre. The organisation of joint learning, mentoring and training events through existing WMO

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE B OF 167

#### Sub-activity 4.1.1 Establish interactive ICT platform

This sub-activity will establish an interactive ICT platform, which will serve as a data analytic centre for the member countries to share climate-related data, information, experiences, case studies and other forms of knowledge from the five Programme countries in standardised, comparable formats most useful for end-users. End-users include national meteorological services (NMSs), National Climate Change Units (NCCUs) in Fiji, Kiribati, Nauru, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands and Tuvalu). The countries will make as much as possible of their climate-related data publicly available through their national data portals and the regional Pacific Climate Change Network (PCCN) portal. The platform will provide a common framework for NMSs and other ministries to find and harvest useful datasets and information on their countries' environment and to publicise these datasets and information in a way that is most useful for end-users. This will help to streamline workflows to share data seamlessly between sectors. The Programme will add a repository for climate data and bring management and coordination into its schedule of capacity building activities. Improved data sharing and access will also be supported by the establishment of an electronic link with existing data sources while hosting and sharing their own on a common platform. Enhancement in data management capability can be achieved through:

- Addition of a new category for climate data and information to each national portal;
- Training and engagement of national climate data consultants (See Sub-activity 2.3.1);
- Support for countries to prepare their national reporting for climate-related agreements (e.g. NDCs, VNRs). The platform will provide a common framework for NMSs and other ministries in very small countries, but with access to good quality data, it provides an opportunity to evaluate policy and assess progress on adaptation. A two-way flow of information from NMHSs to and from relevant sectors will be supported by the platform. This will help to improve the use of climate-related information and raise the profile of NMHS in other sector thereby supporting demand and fostering sustainability;
- Participation of data consultants in regional forums to enhance the use of data in national planning;
- Establishment of electronic links with existing data sources and back-up in the regional portal; and
- Management of the ICT platform for the five Programme countries through support for ICT interventions across the five countries, including application of ICT in NMHSs operations and upgrading of hardware and software infrastructure to ensure that they are able to use Internet of Things (IoT) infrastructure for climate services.

#### Sub-activity 4.1.2 Organise learning, mentoring and training

This sub-activity will comprise training, mentoring and advisory services for local consultants and staff in NMHSs in Cook Islands, Fiji, Kiribati, Nauru, Palau, the Republic of Marshall Islands and Tuvalu on strengthening climate information services; strengthening observations, monitoring, modelling and prediction; strengthening of marine weather and ocean services; establishing MWEWs at national and community levels; and building community resilience to climate change. The training will be provided by regional centres (e.g. China Meteorological Administration (CMA) Training Centre™ through coordination by the Chinese Academy of Sciences) and local NMHSs.

- Joint learning events for exchanging knowledge and sharing experiences and lessons learned in strengthening climate information services and MWEWs in these five countries. This will have a major focus on

## B

### On the development and implementation of National Frameworks for Climate Services (NFCs) through all Results and related activities of this Programme.

- **Targeted training of NMHS staff** (e.g., meteorologists, ICT administrators, statisticians) in key areas that are essential to the effective delivery of climate services. This will be achieved through existing training centres in the WMO network, the University of the South Pacific (USP), the International Centre for Theoretical Physics (ICTP) and others. Training will be delivered through a combination of on-site training at these institutions and e-learning.
  - **Forecasting and Numerical Weather Prediction (NWP)** by suitably qualified providers such as WMO regional training centres and other WMO-approved meteorological organisations. This could include training in the analysis of weather, short-term climate monitoring and prediction in disaster prevention and mitigation.
  - **Observation and Instrumentation**, including Operation and Maintenance (O&M) of equipment for long-term sustainability.
- **Innovative and cost-saving technologies** for observation, modelling and prediction with special focus on the application of ICT. Hence, these events will also be critical for regular reviewing options in terms of new technologies and applications, such as wireless communications and Internet of Things (IoT) infrastructure for climate services and disaster management.
- **Principles of satellite sensing and use** of meteorological satellite images in weather analysis and forecasting.
  - **Demonstration and training on the operation and maintenance of weather radar systems – initialized by WMO and USP under the new WMO Regional Training Centre (WRC) at the University of the South Pacific (USP), and continued with Met Service in cooperation with USP under the new WMO Regional Training Center**. The demonstrations and training will build capacity of the NMHSs for the provision of improved and more accurate weather radar services, including: improving the quality of radar data; determining the rainfall intensity; which is important for determining the potential for extreme events such as flash floods and landslides; validating satellite imagery in real-time and in more timely fashion; and validating Numerical Weather Prediction (NWP) forecasts.
  - **Enhancing institutional effectiveness** of NMHSs through Quality Management Systems (QMS). Weather services will be provided with a quality management system that is internationally recognized, which will ensure the delivery of high-quality services to users.
- **Enhancing NMHS services** through impact-based forecasting and Forecast-and-Basing Financing.
  - **Enhancing climate services in NMHSs**, including options for ensuring long-term financing and cost-recovery such as private sector investment, public-private partnerships and the application of innovative financial mechanisms.
  - **Use of alerts, information exchange and coordination** in the first phase after major sudden-onset disasters, including through the Global Disaster Alert and Coordination System (GDACS).

Furthermore, the Programme will provide mentoring and technical advisory services to NMHSs in the five countries through capacity building, training and awareness raising initiatives and materials for a range of stakeholders. The Programme will also build capacity within NMHSs on national delivery of climate services; and provide expert advice to the Programme on key climate services and best practices, including gender-responsive implementation. In order to enhance synergies and avoid creating parallel initiatives, the Programme will work closely with the WMO Global Climate Services Initiative (GCSI).

according to the terms of the Funded Activity Agreement and the Accreditation Master Agreement, UNEP will also assume a limited role as Executing Entity, as described in the relevant section below.

UNEP brings more than 20 years' experience working on climate change and is an established GCF Accredited Entity. UNEP's work focuses on strengthening national capacity to address climate change through natural science and economics and is tied to the environmental and development concerns of countries. Based on its core science-based mandate, one of UNEP's seven sub-programs is entirely dedicated to keeping the Regional Climate Centre (RCC) in place.

Through its Science Division, UNEP has longstanding expertise in environmental and climate change information management and early warning systems. For example, with GEF and EC funding it is currently supporting the Pacific Islands Meteorology Programme (PIM) in the implementation of early warning systems and using them for reporting progress on SDGs and MEAs. Its Science Division manages the Global Climate Observing System (GCOS), which provides a framework for international cooperation to facilitate regional environmental information networks and the world adaptation science program (formerly PROVIA). Through its work on early warning and foresight, UNEP enables stakeholders to respond to the latest evidence of climate change impacts. It also has extensive experience in implementing sub-regional and sub-national level including a Sub-Regional Office for the Pacific co-located with the Secretary of the Pacific Regional Environment Programme (SPREP).

Under its 7th sub-programme, "Keeping the Global Environment under Review", UNEP also produces a major Global Environmental Outlook (GEO) report every four years, assessing the states, trends and outlooks of the environment at global level. The findings from this assessment are used to inform policy makers and the contents are later used to inform the latter. The Regional and Global reports would greatly benefit from climatic information provided by the PCCP. UNEP will also provide support for the development of climate products from the proposed GCF Programme. UNEP and SPREP are respectively implementing and executing a "GEF-funded cross-cutting capacity development project—the 'Inform' project—which is putting in place national early warning systems, including those that would be used to inform the Pacific Climate Change Programme. This Programme will partner with Inform and, more generally, build on existing climate information inputs to provide a strong evidence base for policy making.

The Pacific Meteorological Council (PMC) has defined issues and priorities for the region in the Pacific Islands Meteorology Strategy 2017–2026, endorsed by the Council members and Pacific government ministers responsible for meteorology. The strategy is aligned with the GCF Programme and the resources to be leveraged for this Programme. It is an influential body and may advise the PMU and the Programme partners on coordination, technological and political issues if required. Programmatic annual reports will be provided as required by the GCF Programme and the PMU will provide a progress report at the 5th biennial meeting of the Programme at their 5th biennial meeting in July 2018.

Programme Management Unit (PMU)

The proposed programme will set up a central Programme Management Unit (PMU) hosted by UNEP's Sub-regional Office for the Pacific, which is co-located with SPREP and the new Pacific Climate Change Center (PCC) in Apia, Samoa. The PMU will provide management and support to the national implementation of the GCF Programme in the region. It will also serve as a focal point for coordination between the various partners involved in Programme execution, in line with their obligations under the respective legal instruments and will coordinate to ensure that reports are received. The PMU will consolidate at half yearly progress reports from the various partners and submit these to the GCF Secretariat. The PMU will also provide support to the Technical Partners and submit these to the AE. The PMU will provide guidance and source expertise as needed on issues related to the implementation of the Programme, including in relation to the engagement with other development partners working with the Programme countries to ensure that activities in related fields are complementary, and to seek opportunities for collaboration. The PMU will also provide secretariat services to the Programme Steering Committee.


---
**Page 97**
---

The PRU will be staffed by three full-time staff administered by UNEP: a Programme Coordinator (PC), a Fund Management, Monitoring and Procurement Officer (FMMPO) and a National Finance and Administrative Assistant (NFAA). Both the FMMPO and the NFAA will report directly to the PC. All staff will be recruited in line with UNEP’s recruitment policy. The PC will be responsible for the day-to-day management of the Programme. This role will include an overall responsibility for:

i. Coordinating the Programme Steering Committee (PSC);
ii. Managing the Programme in line with the budget and workplans, and in accordance with GCF and UNEP policies;
iii. Being responsible for financial management and disbursements;
iv. Coordinating national EEs and Regional Technical Partners to manage the Programme effectively;
v. Conducting regular monitoring of progress against targets and reporting to the AE; and
vi. Ensuring exchange of information and knowledge across the five countries.

To achieve the targets of the proposed Programme the PM will, inter alia:

i. Acquire on-the-ground information to inform UNEP progress reports;
ii. Engage with relevant stakeholders;
iii. Arrange for field visits and meetings;
iv. Provide technical support, including measures to address challenges to Programme implementation;
v. Participate in training activities; and
vi. Facilitate knowledge sharing activities.

Additionally, the PC will liaise with members of the PSC and PMU, technical experts, government staff, civil society and stakeholders involved to coordinate the implementation of the proposed Programme’s Activities. For this purpose, the PC will establish a Project Management Office (PMO) in each country, including a Desk Partner (DPD), within which the PMU will be co-located in Apia, Samoa. In cooperation with the PNDP, the PC will coordinate expert advice to the Programme on key climate information services and best practices and will provide technical support for the development of relevant tools, engineering studies and projects of meteorological and other project-related equipment.

A range of equipment (i.e., AWS, AWOS, weather radars, etc.) and communication infrastructure upgrades will be procured in each country. The procurement process will follow UNEP procedures in respective countries, based on the priorities identified in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2). In addition to undertaking direct procurement as part of UNEP’s fee income, the PMU will also provide support to material EEs and technical partners on procurement in line with the provisions of the respective Public Contracting Agreements (PCAs). The PMU will ensure compliance with the UN rules and regulations related to procurement.

Executing Entities (EES)

The national Executing Entity designated by the Government in each country will be respectively accountable for the effective implementation, coordination at a national level, and for the efficient and effective use of resources. Each EE is an existing established national agency with financial management responsibilities and expertise. Each is also responsible for ensuring that the Programme is implemented in accordance with UNEP’s policies and procedures, regional and international demands, contributing to the effective coordination of nationally and internationally funded climate change projects. The EEs will meet stringent reporting and fiduciary standards of multilateral funding agencies, developed country partners and the GCF.

The responsibilities of the national EEs are to coordinate the execution of the Programme at a national level. They are accountable to the AE for Programme execution and the effective and efficient use of resources.

Therefore, UNEP shall enter into an appropriate agreement (Project Cooperation Agreement) with national EEs for the execution of the Programme. The Project Cooperation Agreements (PCAs) will establish clear roles and responsibilities of the respective parties for the delivery of the proposed activities, and the schedule and conditions under which they are to be carried out. PCAs shall also include provisions on dispute resolution for arbitrations and termination of contract. The PCAs will include specific obligations for the national EEs on Programme implementation, including financial management, reporting requirements, monitoring, evaluation, and liability terms. The EEs will be required to comply with UNEP rules, policies and procedures on procurement.

Each Executing Entity will be responsible for establishing national programme implementation in a relevant part of the country. This will include the development of a National Programme Implementation Plan (NPIP) for each provider and Regional Technical Partners (see later section) in each country. Thus, the EEs will provide technical assistance, guidance and will facilitate cooperation among the implementing organizations. It will also convene the NPIPs quarterly to review progress on implementation and will be responsible for submitting quarterly reports on progress and expenditure. It will be accountable to the central PMU and the National Programme Management Committee (NP MC) in each country.

UNEP will also undertake Limited Executing Entity functions through its Sub-regional Office for the Pacific, including in the context of Programme Result 4 focusing on regional knowledge management and cooperation. The Sub-regional Office will provide technical assistance to national EEs in the region and will contract international consultants and Technical Partners to undertake relevant activities as appropriate.

### Regional Technical Partners

The EEs will draw on the expertise from a broad coalition of Technical Partners, thereby ensuring coherence and complementarity. Technical Partners will include SPC, SREP, NOAA, UH (including PacificAOOS and other relevant regional oceanographic services), and other national and international partners and internationally recognised professional agencies with many years' experience of partnership with Pacific countries. Based on the priorities of countries, some of the partners will lead or provide support for the implementation of specific sub-activities. The EEs will ensure that all partners are aware of their mandates and comparative advantages. Although Technical Partners will work at national level, the agencies will be required to develop a common understanding of the Pacific region's oceanographic procedures and policies – i.e. to deliver their agreed body of work in each country through consultation and coordination with the national EEs. Regional Technical Partners will report to the PMU and Programme Steering Committee.

In order to ensure the collaboration is documented between parties, UNEP will sign a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) between all EEs, Regional Technical Partners supporting national execution, and UNEP to formalise their respective roles in the implementation of the programme.

## B

### SPREP (The Pacific Regional Environment Programme)

Technical organization based in the Secretariat of the Pacific Community with five developed country members, responsible for sustainable development of natural resources and ecosystems, environmental management, and social and physical planning. It is a member of the Pacific Virtual Regional Climate Centre, Pacific Partnership on Marine Protected Areas (PPMP) and the Pacific Adaptation to Climate Change Network (PACAN). SPREP delivers the annual Regional Climate Outlook Forum (RCOF), which includes an On-line Climate Outlook Forum with 12 National Meteorological Services. It maintains Knowledge databases for Pacific NMRSs, manages the Inform project on climate change adaptation, and provides environment related data, information services, technical assistance to member contributions and project funding.

### NOAA (US National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration)

US Government meteorological service whose mandate is: to understand and predict changes in climate, weather, oceans, and coasts. It shares knowledge and information with others; develops and disseminates forecasts and marine ecosystems and resources. It includes the NMRSs of 14 Pacific Island countries and associated states including Palau, Federated States of Micronesia, Marshall Islands, and Kiribati in the Pacific region. Member of SPC and SPREP Agency of the US Department of Commerce.

### UH (University of Hawaii)

The University of Hawaii is a public co-educational college and university

New Zealand National Institute of Water and Atmospheric Research

IFRC assists the Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies in their efforts to reduce the impact of climate change on vulnerable people. It also provides programs for capacity building and reducing climate risks in the region.


## Table 12: Regional Technical Partner agencies, mandates and role in programme sub-activities

Programmes Steering Committee

The Programme Steering Committee (PSC) will be established comprising the five NDAs and a UNEP representative. It will meet at least once per year and co-chaired by the NDAs (in rotation) and UNEP.

The PSC will provide high-level oversight and guidance to achieve Programme objectives. The PSC is responsible for approving the Programme budget, setting priorities and monitoring strategic direction and policy guidance to the Programme team and other stakeholders. Among other responsibilities, it reviews progress against the Programme’s annual work plan and prepares an annual report as prepared by the PMU and national EEs. The committee also provides recommendations on Programme approaches and participates in discussing general strategies and opportunities for Programme planning and implementation.


| Table 12: Regional Technical Partner agencies, mandates and role in programme sub-activities |


---
**Page 106**
---

The functions of the Steering Committee are:

i. Providing overall guidance for Programme execution to the PMU, especially on cross-cutting issues which require consensus from various stakeholders involved in the Programme;

ii. Ensuring that recommended policy and institutional strengthening undertaken under the Programme is consistent with national priorities;

iii. Ensuring full cooperation of various regional and national stakeholders under their jurisdictions to provide access and support to the Programme team in carrying out their tasks;

iv. Representing the interests of NDA members and other stakeholders in their countries derived from a regular formal dialogue between NDAs and national focal points, and

v. Reviewing and monitoring progress in Programme execution.

The members of the Steering Committee will be the five NDAs and the UNEP Representative. Observers will include:

i. The Programme Coordinator;

ii. The five National EEs;

iii. Representatives from the Regional Technical Partner agencies involved in Programme implementation—SPEEP, SPC, NIIWA, BIM, IJH, NOA, IFRC, APCC and others as appropriate;

iv. Additional representatives for Programme Implementation, in particular the NiWiDi, National Disaster Management Authorities and community-based organisations with experience in disaster risk management,

v. Representatives of civil society;

vi. Representatives of women’s organisations; and

vii. Representatives of the private sector.

Secretariat services will be provided by the Programme Management Unit (PMU). The minutes of the Annual Meeting will be provided to the AE by the Programme Coordinator.

The Programme Steering Committee will also be used as a vehicle for enhancing South-South cooperation among the five NDAs.

National Coordination Committees

A National Coordination Committee (NCC) will be established in each of the five countries. The NCCs will include key stakeholders from the national government (in particular, the Disaster and National Disaster Management Authorities), civil society organisations, development partners (including UN agencies such as USAID, DFAT, Tavala), community representatives, private sector representatives, civil society and NGOs involved in early warning systems and disaster risk management, and other relevant stakeholders. The NCC will be a decision-making body within the national-level programme governance structure and will also play a key role in the Grievance Redress Mechanism. It consists of agency or divisional managers and decision-makers who provide leadership on issues related to the Programme. The NCC will include representatives from all relevant stakeholders including community-based organisations with experience in disaster risk management. Among other functions, it will be responsible for providing input into the Programme’s annual report for submission to the PMU and PSC. The Committee also provides recommendations to the Steering Committee regarding how best to involve participants in discussing general strategies and opportunities for Programme planning and implementation.

The functions of the Committee will include:

# National Service Providers

National service providers will be subcontracted by the national EEs. These include the National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHSs), National Disaster Management Authorities, the government climate change agencies and environment ministries, sectoral agencies such as health, agriculture and fisheries, civil society organisations, national campuses of the University of the South Pacific (USP) (present in Cook Islands, Niue, Samoa, Solomon Islands, Tonga, Tuvalu and Vanuatu), and community-based organisations and communities. These organisations will implement activities as described in the country profiles in the Feasibility Study.

For example:

- **Emergency Management Cook Islands** (the national disaster management authority) will use GIS mobile apps technology to undertake household disaster risk surveys in the outer islands, storing the data in a portal for analysis.
  
- The **Girls' and Boys' Brigades in Niue** will conduct disaster preparation training and collect traditional climate knowledge from elders. The Broadcasting Corporation of Niue will acquire practical software to use in weather forecasting and emergency preparedness.

- The **Palau Red Cross Society and the Center for Women's Empowerment Belau**, in cooperation with seven government and non-government agencies, will conduct training for women’s organisations on community and individual needs and resources before, during, and after a disaster or single incident emergency.
  
- The Marshall Islands Red Cross (MIRC) will implement early warning response measures through the establishment of Early Warning Teams in outer-islands. The Marshall Islands Community Health Volunteers will purchase community-based coral health kit cards for distribution to rural communities and will train community members on how to use them. The MIRC will also establish a weather response team at remote locations to easily identify signs of the early onset of coral reef bleaching.
  
- The Red Cross Society in Tuvalu and WMO staff will conduct consultations with each island community, collecting disaggregated information from different vulnerable groups to identify risks and priorities for climate change adaptation. This will be used by the MHRCS to enable Tuvalu’s MHMS to produce social media and video communication products for stakeholders and communities.

The Government of the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands (RMI) and Tuvalu are seeking a GCF grant to strengthen their resilience to climate change impacts and enable their vulnerable populations to adapt to increasing climate variability. The proposed development of robust climate and ocean information services will provide critical early warning systems for extreme weather events, including those of immediate warnings and long-range seasonal forecasts and will provide essential data and in-country technical capacity building to help Pacific Island countries better understand the risks associated with climate change impacts. Addressing climate change threats will require considerable adaptation measures at a cost that far exceeds many of the countries’ financial capacities, with Pacific islands experiencing significant volatility in government revenues due to their reliance on external aid and remittances. The proposed project identifies development needs that, due to budget constraints, are prioritised at the expense of longer term climate change adaptation strategies. This project will provide a platform for long-term climate change adaptation activities, addressing obvious and highly visible needs, and rarely take adequate account of climate change in planning their inputs. External finance is therefore essential for the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu to address these challenges.

Pacific island countries have repeatedly emphasised their priority need for access to climate finance.20 This is particularly evident in the Suva Declaration, which also expressed deep concern for populations that adaptation through increased resilience will be most difficult. The Suva Declaration called on developed countries to provide resources from other development priorities to meet adaptation costs.203 There is limited scope for the five Pacific Island countries to access climate finance from international sources such as concessional loans from International Financial Institutions, loans from bilateral donors, domestic borrowing and bank overdrafts. In 2016, the Cook Islands’ total central government share of GDP rose from 23.8% in 2009 to 46% in 2016. The Marshall Islands’ share was at a high of 57%. Tuvalu’s share is 50%, and Palau’s share is 40%.

Moreover, Tuvalu is classified as a Least Developed Country (LDC). Direct financial assistance from developed countries to Pacific Island States through concessional instruments are not considered feasible for the Pacific SIDS in this Programme. Although there is a clear need for climate information, there is currently no real market demand for climate information services. Climate-sensitive sectors have been identified by the five Pacific Island countries as priority areas of focus for the proposed programme. The Programme is expected to generate interest in using climate information products and multi-hazard early warning systems (MHEWS) in the five Pacific Island countries. These are usually regarded as a public good and are expected to be provided as an outcome of budget appropriation for NMHSs. Although Result 1 of this Programme is designed to facilitate the long-term sustainability of climate sensitive sectors, it will not directly generate revenue from these sectors. The programme will therefore need financial policies to ensure that the five NMHSs have the means to sustain and ensure the ongoing operation of their services.

their mandate services, this will only facilitate national climate funding (including through limited cost-recovery mechanisms from sectors) beyond the Programme’s lifespan.

Donor and bilateral support are also currently not available for strengthening regional efforts. The GCF is best positioned to provide such support in a manner that complements or complements or complements or complements or do so particularly at a multi-country scale. However, strengthening and extending the observation network will provide a foundation of high-quality data from a greatly expanded geographical area, including from ocean surface temperature and sea level rise monitoring, which is critical for supporting climate change adaptation activities and avoiding maladaptation, not only in the five Programme countries but across the region.

The Programme includes dedicated activities for integrating climate information services into national policies plans and budgets. It will also engage with the private sector with a view to creating an enabling environment to leverage private sector investment in climate resilience and adaptation. The costs of implementing these activities including the operation and maintenance of equipment and infrastructure, will be leveraged by the Programme.

The programme is closely aligned with regional priorities, particularly as articulated in the Pacific Islands Meteorological Strategy 2017-2020 and the Pacific Roadmap for Strengthened Climate Services 2017-2026 (see Table in section B.3.), as well as national government plans for strengthening their hydrometeorological services, including the Cook Islands’ National Climate Change Strategy 2015–2020 and the Samoa National Climate Change Strategy 2014–2020. The programme also aligns with the priorities identified in several GCF Readiness programs. For example, the Cook Islands has been selected as a pilot country for the GCF’s Water and Climate Resilience project, which aims to develop national climate change strategies and has now agreed a comprehensive Programme with the GCF; which will be aligned with the Cook Islands’ National Development Strategy and national development strategies and regional and international agreements to which his five countries are parties.

The programme will contribute to long-term social, economic and environmental benefits from avoided losses and damages associated with extreme weather events and climate change. The urgent need to reverse the cycle of decline in ocean health is emphasised in the proclamation of the UN Decade for Ocean Science for Sustainable Development (2021–2030). The Decade will provide a unifying framework to strengthen international cooperation on research, capacity building, data sharing and access to marine resources; and enable countries to achieve all of their ocean-related Agenda 2030 priorities. The programme is fully aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), the Paris Agreement, the Sendai Framework and the SAMOA Pathway as follows:

i. SDG 13 on urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts and related target 1.3: “To strengthen resilience and adaptive capacity to climate-related hazards and natural disasters in all countries”, which is a focus of this programme.

ii. The Paris Agreement, which in Article 7, Subparagraph (c) calls for “strengthening scientific knowledge on climate, including research, systematic observation of the climate system and early warning systems, in a manner relevant to developing countries”.

iii. The Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030, which in paragraph 33(i) stresses that it is important “To invest in, develop, maintain and strengthen people-centred multi-hazard, multidisciplinary early warning systems, including through the use of information and communication technologies, social mechanisms and hazard-monitoring telecommunications systems; develop such

systems through a participatory process; tailor them to the needs of users, including social and cultural requirements, in particular gender; promote the application of simple and low-cost early warning systems and facilities; and broaden release channels for national disaster early warning information.”


The SAKAI Pathways project, funded by the Global Environment Facility (GEF), will be implemented from 2014. This Programme will address the highlighted needs for increased accessibility to technical assistance for early warning systems, risk assessment and data, observation equipment and disaster risk management tools, including through the development of a regional early warning system at sub-regional, national and global cooperation; improved baseline monitoring of island systems and to address the need for capacity building in climate change adaptation. The Programme will raise awareness and share environmental data in an effort to increase resilience to the impacts of climate change. This is consistent with the goals set out by the Pacific Islands Forum Leaders’ Declaration by the UN General Assembly in October 2013. Paragraph 31 (i): special topics: [T]he United Nations Framework Programme to further strengthen support to small island developing States, including through dedicated projects.


Furthermore, the Programme directly responds to the 2015 Paris Agreement on Climate Change and the 2019 Kaisai II Declaration for Urgent Climate Action Now. The Programme will immediately increase support and assistance for Pacific-led science-based initiatives intended to improve our understanding of risk and vulnerability, including through support for modelling and risk mapping capabilities. This will be achieved by supporting the development of a regional early warning system; building economic and infrastructure impacts, and capacity building support for evidence-based decision-making and policy implementation.


#### B & E: Environment and sustainability (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)


This Programme is designed to address key barriers to delivering sustainable climate services and impact-based MHEWS for climate resilience in the Cook Islands, Nauru, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu. The Programme will contribute to the implementation of the Pacific Climate Change Strategy (PCCS) and the Pacific MHEWS and key policy strategies, plans and budgets, which will provide the foundation for update of climate information in decision-making across national, sub-national and community levels in the five Pacific island countries. This will be achieved by supporting the development of a regional early warning system; building knowledge management and learning, which will also enhance country ownership and responsibility for change adaptation and resilience.


The following are key elements of the exit strategy that will ensure sustainability beyond the Programme’s lifespan:


- The Programme is demand-led: It responds to long-standing requests from in-country users of climate information services. It will be implemented through close collaboration with relevant Government Authorities and other agencies who will implement the Programme, and their engagement in adjusting its implementation as required. The Programme will also ensure that the sustainability of the climate information services will be sustained by fine-tuning products over the term of the Programme to ensure that the information being provided is useful and actionable by sectors and communities. For example, more relevant and timely climate information will be used to better inform funding for the Government’s activities on forecast-based Financing (FbF) under Activity 3.3.


- The Programme is aligned to established regional coordination and cooperation mechanisms such as the Pacific Islands Climate Change (PICC) in which all five countries of Nauru, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu play an active role. It will enhance such mechanisms through joint learning events and knowledge

B

sharing such as the establishment of a regional hub and participation of the countries in Regional Climate Outlook Forums. The Programme Coordinator will give presentations on the Programme at SREP and SPC Council meetings, biennial PCOM meetings, and regional meetings of Pacific Heads of Environment and Meteorology.

The Programme is based on national and regional priorities and was endorsed by the PAC, NMIs and associated stakeholders. It builds upon existing climate services in the region through the Pacific Islands Meteorological Strategy (PIMS) and the Roadmap for Climate Services 2017 – 2025, with the PICS taking a key role. The strong country and regional ownership are important pre-conditions for long-term sustainability.

* The Programme is based on a robust knowledge management approach that will facilitate replicability and sustainability in the long term. Programme Result 4 is entirely dedicated to regional knowledge management and will be implemented through a series of workshops, training sessions and lessons not only within the Programme but also with other relevant initiatives and stakeholders. This will be led by the Pacific Islands Climate Services (PICS) and supported by the Pacific Island Partnership (PIIP), with which the Programme Management Unit will be co-located in Apia, Samoa. At the regional level, PICS will work closely with the Secretariat of the Pacific Community (SPC) and Environment Ministers, Ministers responsible for Met services (often the same) and Climate Change Ministers. The permanent stakeholder engagement mechanisms to be established as part of the National Frameworks for Climate Services (NFCS) will also play a key role in this.

* Programme Result 1 will play a key role in sharing knowledge and lessons learned at the national level.

The Programme will strengthen existing NMIs and disaster management mechanisms in the five countries, with particular emphasis on building capacity of the NMIs to provide climate services and capacities and be informed by their local knowledge. Sustainability will be supported by the establishment of institutional coordination mechanisms that will ensure effective governance, coordination and management of national climate services. The Frameworks will institutionalize feedback and review mechanisms in the climate service delivery process, with a focus on user needs and demand for climate information as user demands change. This will contribute to long-term sustainability through ensuring that NMIs products are relevant and useful to users and stakeholders.

The five NMIs have formally committed to sustaining some of the critical functions to be introduced by the Programme (climatology, oceanography and Operations & Maintenance) after its implementation period.

* The Programme will adopt a broad multi-stakeholder approach and bring on board different ministries agencies and other relevant institutions. It will also engage with private sector entities as well as both producers and users of climate information and early warning. National Climate Outlook Forums will be a key mechanism for the dissemination of climate services, including the interpretation of the Programme’s outputs and outcomes. Through capacity development activities, awareness raising and a climate knowledge management component, the capacity of NMIs to provide climate services is enhanced. The holistic approach being taken in each of the needs of the Pacific will be more conducive to sustainability than a sole focus on the provision of technical infrastructure, as many producers are already in place.

* The focus of Programme Result 1 is on establishing a new sustainable business delivery model for the five NMIs. Amongst others, the Programme will establish National Frameworks for Climate Services (NFCS) that will provide a common framework and set of guidelines for the establishment of national institutions for enhanced use of climate information and provision of climate services. The NFCS is based on the Common Framework for Climate Services which is the principal international standard of climate services for adaptation to climate change and sustainable development. The NFCS will be supported by stakeholder coordination mechanisms – including National Climate Outlook Forums

### The Programme has developed a comprehensive plan for Operations and Maintenance (O&M), which details how specific O&M costs and needs will be addressed and budgeted for both during and post implementation. This plan is currently being reviewed by the Programme Implementation Manager, who will ensure that O&M costs are included in annual national budget documents.

The costs will be provided as detailed, disaggregated line items at an appropriate point in the financial year to allow for adequate planning of resources required. For Palau and RMI; the US NOAA will also support part of the O&M. In addition, given the importance of data sharing and access, the Programme will continue to provide training on data security and privacy issues including fees utilising the data portal network. Furthermore, the Programme will provide targeted training in the use of the GSON initiative, which is a strong predictor of sustainability due to its potential for upskilling the workforce rapidly, cheaply and exponentially by developing local educators.

The Programme will also utilise a training of trainers (ToT) approach, which is a strong predictor of sustainability due to its potential for upskilling the workforce rapidly, cheaply and exponentially by developing local educators. ToT will ensure that the capacity building activities are sustainable in the long term.

Moreover, through the Global Basic Observing Network (GSON) initiative; it has been recognized widely as an important tool for improving climate resilience. The GSON initiative is a global public good that is to be financed in a sustainable and predictable manner. The GSON initiative will be established under the umbrella of the Alliance for Sustainable Observations (ASO), which is a multi-stakeholder partnership (of which Palau and RMI are members). The SOFF will be launched in 2021.

The five Programme countries have been confirmed as pilot countries for the GSON initiative. This will allow them to demonstrate the value of investing in GSON both to protect local communities and benefit local populations, while also contributing to global climate resilience by providing data that can be used globally. After the Programme implementation period; support from the SOFF is expected to be available to the counties to continue maintaining GSON standards as needed.

The WMO Commission for Climate and Water (CCW) has endorsed the GSON initiative by defining the obligations for countries to implement a minimal set of surface-based observations for which national responsibility can be established. The Programme will generate additional climate information datasets of significant value to the five countries and their development partners. Fisheries, tourism, disaster management and transport industries are all vulnerable to climate change impacts such as sea level rise, increased storm frequency and intensity, coastal erosion, land subsidence, water stress, food security, maladaptation and the creation of stranded assets. The observed value of improved datasets and regular reporting of these outcomes through Programme communications via public and social media will reinforce the importance of the GSON initiative to national decision-makers and other key stakeholders functions.

Furthermore; the importance to the international scientific community of data covering a large geographical and currently data-sparse area of the Pacific Ocean and its value to global observing

## C FINANCIAL INFORMATION

### C.1 Total Financing

As reaffirmed by the UN General Assembly in the 2019 Political declaration of the High-Level Meeting to Review Progress on the Implementation of the Priorities of Small Island Developing States through the implementation of the SDGS Accelerated Modalities of Action (SAMOA) Pathway, SIDS remain a special case for sustainable development as they confront unique challenges. These include limited land and water resources, high vulnerability to external shocks in their economies, high costs and the adverse effects of climate change and natural disasters.

The small size, dispersed nature, and low population density of many Pacific Island States make them particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as sea-level rise, increased storm intensity, and more frequent extreme weather events. The cost of domestic inter-island shipping services in many countries of the Pacific region – especially to outer islands - are very high compared with other parts of the world. In addition, the cost of air travel is also very high due to limited flights on the absence of direct flight connectivity.

Therefore, the cost of travel, general logistics and transactions in the Pacific region are comparatively higher than in many other parts of the world.

No. 230. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) will provide financial support for projects that take place under the Programme. GCF grant funds will ultimately strengthen the climate monitoring and observation infrastructure/infrastructure (over 50% of the total project cost). Technical assistance and training. Financial management of the Programmes will be in line with UN rules and regulations.
