### Other financing arrangements and contributions (max. 250 words; approximately 6.5 page)

#### Parallel Financing – Total: ~USD 8 million

The Pacific Community (SPC) is the regional lead in coastal hazard / risk management and climate change adaptation, with a strong focus on early warning systems development. SPC will contribute approximately USD 7 million in Parallel Finance to the Programme through projects that have a direct contribution to the Programme Results.

* **Climate Risk and Early Warning Systems (CREWS) and Coastal Inundation Forecasting Demonstration Project (CIFDP)** – SPC is delivering CREWS and CIFDP, which are climate change adaptation tools tailored to Pacific island geomorphology and resources available to the region. The project has been implemented in Fiji, Papua New Guinea, and Tuvalu. SPC received first prize at the 2019 Flood Early Warning Systems (FEWS) conference in Melbourne, Australia for this innovative work on coastal inundation forecasting. Lessons learned will be directly used in the UNEP GCF Programme.

Project budget: ~USD 800,000

* **Tuvalu Coastal Bathymetry Project (TCP)** – SPC is supporting the collection of Lader bathymetry and topography data for the nine islands of Tuvalu. The information produced and capacity transferred under this project will provide a baseline for enhancing the delivery of the UNEP GCF Programme.

Project budget (for relevant components): ~USD 500,000


---
**Page 119**
---

# Pacific Resilience Project Phase I (PrPf-1) – SPC is leading a demonstration activity aiming to develop an innovative early warning system for Tropical Cyclone and Tsunami-driven inundation tailored to Pacific Island Countries (PICs), with the support of the NMHMs. The project focuses on Tonga and Samoa. The main deliverables are: i) Development of a prototype; ii) Validation of the prototype; iii) Successful delivery of the SPC-led activities under the UNEP GCF Programme.

Project budget – USD 1 million

# Pacific Resilience Project Phase II (PrPf-2) – SPC is leading an integrated coastal risk management programme for Majuro, RMI. This project aims to: i) Develop a comprehensive coastal inundation hazard assessment; ii) Risk assessment to inform coastal development decisions; and iii) Strengthening of the existing coastal monitoring system in Majuro. The project will be implemented by SPC undertaken in collaboration with the University of Canterbury, aims at developing a risk-informed decision-making framework for coastal management in Majuro. The project is also co-funding integrating the hazard and risk knowledge generated into a seasonal climate forecast service (SCFS) for RMI, especially on the tailoring of the coastal hazard framework for Pacific islands. This will ensure that the SPC-led activities are readily available to ensure the successful delivery of SPC-led activities under the UNEP GCF Programme.

Project commitment – USD 1 million

# Pacific Catastrophe Risk Assessment and Financing Initiative (PCRCAF) – SPC is leading a regional effort to increase risk management capacity in the Pacific region. The project aims to: i) Develop regional standards for asset and impact data collection; ii) Identify and document existing national vulnerability functions to convert hazard into risk; and iv) Develop regional risk assessment guidelines for various relevant scales (country, sub-national, local). The developed tools and risk knowledge developed will provide a critical baseline to convert historical loss data into risk-based products.

Project budget – USD 3.5 million

Additional Parallel Financing will be provided as follows:

* The GEF-funded Inform project – implemented by UNEP and executed by SREP – will contribute Parallel Financing to Activities 2, 3 & 4 with an estimated amount of USD 172,503.
* APEC Climate Center (APCC) will contribute additional Parallel Financing estimated as USD 1,052,392.

The International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies (IFRC) has committed a contribution of ~USD 112,750 to the Programme through the regional technical position under IFRC. 30% of the person's time will be solely dedicated to providing

## C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 page)

### C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building activities?
* Yes
* No

The Programme incorporates a significant capacity building approach through its design to “bring about transformation that is generated and sustained over time from within”[38] for increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the five Pacific countries populations to climate change threats.

All four Programmes will focus on capacity building activities. Result 1 focuses on capacity building at the institutional level of the NMHS and other government entities through the National Frameworks for Climate Services (NFCS) and the development of a national climate service strategy. Results 2, 3 and 4 are focused on institutional and individual levels (e.g. of NMHS staff) for enhanced observations, modeling and forecasting.

The Programme recognises the difficulty of recruiting qualified personnel in very small island countries, particularly in areas such as meteorology and oceanography where there is a limited pool of expertise within each country’s existing human resource base. Result 3 includes a range of capacity building efforts at the institutional level to build on the NMHSs’ existing climate service delivery capabilities and enhance their capacities. Under this Result, NMHS and National Disaster Management Authorities (NDMAs) will also strengthen their awareness and outreach capacities for community training and engagement. Result 4 is envisaged as a long-term capacity building effort that will focus on institutional strengthening and will focus on regional learning and knowledge management to support the objectives of the Programme and will be implemented through the UNDP.

The Programme also includes funding for mainly observation technologies under Result 2: SPREC, SPC, BoM, NiWA, NOAA and APCC will support the NMHSs in building in-house capacity to monitor use and maintenance of these systems. The NMHSs will also have access to training materials and content incidentally to other ongoing technology transfer activities (data management software, use of software in generating forecasts, etc.) and capacity development through its work on climate forecasting capacity and application.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section shows the performance of the project programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

D.1 Expected Performance (approximately 1 page)

The Programme will directly contribute to increasing climate-resilient sustainable development in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu through the achievement of the following Fund-level impact, as stated in the Green Climate Fund Document Framework (Draft):

A.10 – Increasing resilience for the most vulnerable people, communities, and regions.

The Programme intends to establish integrated climate and oceans information services and impact-based medium early warning systems (MIEWS) to ensure that the entire population of the five programme countries has access to accurate, timely and actionable climate information. The proposed interventions aim to reduce disaster risk in the Pacific Islands region by increasing the capacity of communities living within the coastal zone of the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands (RMI) and Tuvalu.105 The whole population of the five SIDS is at risk from climate-related hazards such as cyclones, storm surges, coastal erosion, flooding, droughts, sea level rise, and tsunamis. These countries are also vulnerable to extreme weather zones (estimated at 90–100% for the free countries106) and communities in remote island locations. Women, children, older people, and persons with disabilities are particularly vulnerable to the adverse impacts of climate change. The Programme includes targeted interventions tailored to the specific needs of these groups.

The World Risk Index 2018 states that disaster risk – the risk that an extreme natural event will lead to disaster – is at its highest in Oceania.107 The main contributor to deaths and financial losses in the Pacific region is tropical cyclones.108 As stated in the 2015 UNDRR Global Assessment Report,109 the concentration of people living within coastal zones (estimated at 63–74%) and on small islands (estimated at 80–85%) is high; cyclone shelters, fishing boats, inter-island ferries and often food gardens along the coasts – in Pacific island countries.110 The World Risk Index also states that the five programme countries are among those with the highest concentrations of risk. All five programme countries have assets, infrastructure and crops at risk from climate-related hazards such as cyclones, storm surges, coastal erosion, flooding, droughts, sea level rise, and tsunamis.111 The total number of buildings in these countries is 205,643 with buildings at risk contributing the majority of this total (86.5%). Infrastructure (13.2%) and cash crops (0.4%) are also at risk.112 The current average annual losses (AAP) per capita (USD$2019) are 537 (Cook Islands); 2122 (Niue); 865 (Palau); 1,000 (RMI); and 4,000 (Tuvalu).113

The Programme aims to reduce the expected losses of lives and economic assets due to the impact of climate hazards and extreme climate-related disasters and enhance adaptive capacity at national, sub-national and community level. The Programme is expected to achieve an overall reduction in damages and

### A0.6 Increased generation and use of climate information in decision-making

The Programme will directly support the increased generation and use of climate and oceans information in decision-making across all sectors and at all levels, including national, provincial, district, and community levels.

The Programme will substantially expand the geographical coverage of climate observations in the five Pacific countries through the introduction of new climate observation technologies (e.g., weather stations), including the introduction of innovative and cost-saving technologies (e.g. a localised mobile climate information system) to improve the quality and quantity of climate data, especially for remote islands in compliance with GBON; and using weather radar, buoys and ocean drones to collect high-resolution, long-range oceanographic data across the extensive marine Exclusive Economic Zones (EEZ). For example, the Programme will support the establishment of a national network of weather stations for agencies, national climate data portals supported by SPREP, the Pacific Ocean Portal and other relevant platforms.

The Programme will develop tailored information products co-designed and co-produced with communities, climate-sensitive sectors and other key stakeholders. End-users will be supported through targeted training and use of new technologies to improve their ability to access and interpret climate and ocean information for decision-making. The Programme will support mutually beneficial partnership between NMHSs and climate information users so that NMHSs understand how sectors and communities want to use information and utilise it in a way that is relevant, useful and actionable; and sectors and communities can better use the information provided by NMHSs.

The Programme will serve as the key vehicles for facilitating the use of climate information in different sectors, such as agriculture, fisheries, water resources management, disaster risk reduction, etc. The Programme will develop tailored products to communities at “the last mile” through targeted outreach activities, which will promote the use of climate information by the most vulnerable end-users such as women, children and disabled people.

### A0.7 Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks

The Programme will strengthen the adaptive capacity and reduce exposure to climate risks posed by extreme climatic events in the five Pacific countries through the implementation of early warning systems (EWS) based on thorough assessment of the existing situation and gaps in the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu.


---
**Page 126**
---

This will support the development or enhancement of MHEWS for communities at “the last mile” to build community response capabilities to secure their lives, assets and livelihoods to climate-related hazards. The Programme interventions will incorporate various activities ranging from vulnerability assessment at community level, to strengthening early warning systems, to generating climate information products that generate climate data from remote islands to support local-scale forecasting and monitoring of extreme events in the Pacific region. These activities will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach, thereby creating opportunities for introducing wireless communications and Internet of Things (IoT) technology, and install highly valuable low-tech information points in outer islands, such as signboards and compass posts. The Programme will also support the development of climate risk management tools at the community level in five Pacific island countries, thus enhancing their capacity to make improved decisions regarding adaptation to climate hazards and disaster reduction.

### A.8 Strengthening awareness of climate change risks and reduction measures

The Programme is expected to significantly increase awareness of climate hazards and disaster risk reduction measures across the populations of the five Pacific island countries. At national, sub-national and community levels, this will be achieved through a multi-stakeholder approach that will mainstream processes and mainstream climate awareness into policy, planning and decision-making. The Programme will also support the development of climate change adaptation strategies at the national level, National Climate Outlook Forums, tailored information products, sector-specific training and workshops.

Furthermore, the Programme will work with communities to co-design disaster drills and improve communication between communities and local authorities on how to respond to extreme weather events and weather themes, which will be integrated into localised mobile climate information communication systems. The Programme will also support the development of a national early warning system for tropical cyclones and discussion about the way in which NMHS forecasts are generated; as local communities come to understand the physical processes that inform a forecast and how they influence the phenomena that they use to make a weather forecast, this will facilitate acceptance of NMHS warnings, and advice will save lives, crops and other assets.

### D.2 Paradigm shift potential (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The major paradigm shift the Programme intends to achieve is towards the use of accurate, timely and accessible climate information in policy, planning and decision actions, enabling sectors and communities in the five Pacific Island countries to adapt to increasing climate variability and change. Without the proposed Programme, NMHSs will continue to provide forecasts that are based on outdated data and models, as well as insufficient climate data and information. NMHSs will continue to generate basic weather forecasts and useful seasonal range data but their information will be based on aggregated data (particularly for outer islands) and will not reflect the specific conditions of a particular island or community. The role of agencies responsible for disaster preparation and warnings will remain informal and often underused. Data collection, analysis and dissemination systems are also weak in many Pacific Island countries.

Communities will find traditional forecasting systems less and less reliable but will not be able to understand the technical workings behind it available, or how they should use it.


### Project for follow-up

Supporting the five Pacific NMHSs’ achievement of VMO standards will have a transformative impact on managing climate and weather risks in their countries along with a better understanding of long-term climate change. The Programme will also support the development of MHEWS at community level, facilitated through the existing regional cooperation mechanisms such as the Pacific Meteorological Council (PMC) and the Pacific Islands Forum Secretariat (PIFS). The five Pacific Island countries that have established MHEWS on selected islands in Cook Islands, New, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu will have a high potential for replicability in other islands communities. Replication will be facilitated through tailoring the collection of

## Hydro-meteorological data (with a special focus on ocean areas) to address specific climate change threats and tailoring the analysis of data and packaging of information to address the needs of specific end-users at community-level. Programme activities are expected to achieve transformative impacts through innovative approaches in five key areas: improving access to climate information; reducing costs and increasing accuracy of observations; and developing innovative modern communication systems to cater for the diverse needs of all users, including remote island communities. The programme will ensure that services reach all end-users especially, including end-users in remote island locations. Moreover, the Programme will create an enabling environment for a new innovative business model for providing climate information services by NMHSs to other NMHSs and private sector entities through the Climate Outlook Forums, which will contribute to long-term sustainability beyond the duration of the Programme.

### Potential for knowledge and learning

The multi-country approach embedded in this Programme as the multi-country approach will facilitate learning and sharing among the five NMHSs and other entities through the dedicated regional knowledge management interventions in Programme Result 4. The Programme will make hub-to-hub exchanges between the UNFCCC, the World Meteorological Organization (WMO), and the Pacific Climate Change Centre (PCC) in Samoa to bring together NMHS staff and climate experts for training, mentoring, and capacity building. This will enable the five NMHSs to learn from each other’s experiences and from each other's progress in implementing changes. The five countries face many similar challenges and each country has unique strengths that can be shared with others. The Programme is designed to leverage these approaches to the communication of climate science. Furthermore, establishing community-based MHEWS (particularly on remote outer islands) and drawing upon both traditional and modern scientific knowledge will provide a platform for sharing information and knowledge among communities related to climate-related hazards on reliable and timely information and knowledge. The Programme will progress innovative ways of communicating climate change impacts and related changes resulting from these learning processes will, in time, safeguard lives, livelihoods and assets.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

Forcing long-term partnerships between the NMHSs, other government entities, universities, NGOs, civil society organisations, and private sector entities to create an enabling environment for sustainable and impactful climate information services. Furthermore, an enabling environment for long-term sustainability will be created by ensuring that climate change considerations are integrated into national development plans and budgets, which will provide a foundation for uptake of climate information in decision-making and facilitate sustainable service provision in the long term beyond the Programme duration. The National Frameworks for Climate Services (NFCS) will be developed to ensure this.

### Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

The Programme will enable countries to formalise the roles of existing government agencies in addressing key issues related to climate change, including climate services. It will provide a structure for the integration of climate knowledge into the functions of all ministries. It will make the data and information that agencies need to use as they mainstream climate considerations into their functions available through a portal, with in-country assistance provided by NMHSs. The Programme will also ensure that the institutional arrangements, planning and operational processes of Government entities in the five countries will bring systemic change. This will be achieved:

- Revising institutional, policy, planning and regulatory frameworks to optimise coordination between NMHSs and other governmental institutions;
- Securing long-term funding arrangements for NMHSs to provide tailored climate information services on Climate Change Threats.

### Ensuring full compliance of each country with its reporting obligations under the UNFCCC, Paris Agreement and climate-related SDGs targets and indicators.

Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways:
The World Bank will support countries in developing and implementing national climate information products that are transformative in building the long-term resilience of key economic sectors (such as agriculture, fisheries, shipping, tourism and insurance) to climate risks. Delivered through a suite of outreach, learning and knowledge-sharing activities, these climate information products will help governments develop more informed approaches and responses actions to climate risks and prevent maladaptation. This will be transformative in building the long-term resilience of key economic sectors (such as agriculture, fisheries, shipping, tourism and insurance) to climate risks. Furthermore, enhanced climate information will enable resilience planning for critical infrastructure impacted by climate change and other natural hazards. The World Bank will support countries in developing and implementing national climate risk reduction plans that are integrated into sectoral development strategies, land use planning, design, construction and management frameworks. The Programme will therefore make it possible for countries to mainstream climate considerations into their development projects and programmes to maximise the climate resilience of their proposed projects—for example, building a parliament house, planting a seasonal garden or setting up an ocean conditions warning system for mariners.

### 3.1.2 Contribution to SDG 10: Reduced Inequalities
This Programme will contribute to long-term social, economic and environmental benefits from avoided human and economic losses and healthier ecosystems in five of the Pacific region's most vulnerable countries. The World Bank will support countries in developing and implementing national climate risk reduction plans that are integrated into sectoral development strategies, land use planning, design, construction and management frameworks. The Programme will therefore make it possible for countries to mainstream climate considerations into their development projects and programmes to maximise the climate resilience of their proposed projects—for example, building a parliament house, planting a seasonal garden or setting up an ocean conditions warning system for mariners.

Furthermore, the proposed interventions will achieve numerous environmental, social and economic co-benefits. These are summarised below:

**Environmental co-benefits**

The long-term environmental co-benefits of the Programme are expected to be substantial. The increased availability and use of actionable climate information can significantly improve natural resource management, from climate-informed policymaking to land use and avoiding biodiversity loss. The establishment of marine protected areas (MPAs), mangrove restoration, lagoon rehabilitation and shoreline amenities – will facilitate improved management of coastal habitats and inform sustainable fisheries and tourism practices that minimise environmental impacts. In addition, enhancing coral bleaching forecast skill and the development of a regional early warning system for coral bleaching will provide an advance warning of marine life as well as a natural buffer against environmental hazards. For example, sea surface temperature forecasts can be used to predict when coral is likely to bleach or recover from stress by allowing coral to recover before human activity is temporarily suspended.

The generation of impact-based forecasts, decision-support systems (DSS) and alerts related to natural resource management will also provide a more robust basis for adaptation. This will lead to the rapid identification of weather and climate hazards that pose environmental risks and consequently inform the safeguarding of resources and assets at risk from these hazards. The use of DSS and alerts will allow decision-makers to determine crops most likely to succeed, reduces the pressure to convert forests to marginal arable land as well as increased food security. Furthermore, detailed climate information is necessary for comprehensive energy planning in order to reduce the carbon footprint of the office through reliance on fossil-fuel generated electricity. The Cook Islands depends largely on imported diesel for electric generation, and electricity costs are among the highest in the world. This proposal will contribute towards reducing the cost of electricity by increasing the share of renewable energy sources, thereby lowering the production cost of electricity, and the amount of diesel imported. This will also contribute to sustainable development as it will reduce the Cook Islands’ potential renewable energy sources, solar energy rates will high due to sunshine hours.

**Social co-benefits including health impacts**


---
**Page 130**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDS FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 138 OF 167

Several socio-economic benefits will be derived from the Programme’s activities, increasing the livability of islands in the five Programme countries. In particular, Programme Result 3 has a major focus on interventions to strengthen resilience of island communities to climate-related hazards through the implementation of knowledge-based early warning systems (EWS). These EWS are expected to reduce the frequency and severity of losses of lives and damage to assets. Enhanced response capacities among island communities will also have positive impacts in terms of improved disaster risk management, increased preparedness for disasters, improvement in weather forecasting and the prediction of severe weather events (mainly under Result 2) and improved climate adaptation planning.10 These co-benefits help avoid or reduce greater harm, and can otherwise lead to socio-economic related health impacts. Furthermore, this Programme will also lead to economic benefits through increased resilience of island communities, both in terms of livelihood opportunities will both directly and indirectly improve residents socio-economic circumstances through local stream wealth.

### Economic Co-benefits

The Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu face geographic, institutional and systemic challenges that affect the cost and provision of climate information and knowledge services. Considerable capital injection into the countries will stimulate current slow economic growth and enhance both government capacity and weather forecasting systems. The Programme will also contribute to increased resilience by reducing exposure and vulnerability to natural hazards, negating abundant savings in indirect losses. Administration costs for disaster risk management are expected to be reduced as a result of improved preparedness of the sheer number of small islands and low-lying atolls. Economic growth is heavily shaped by geography and climate, with limited opportunities for diversification. The Programme will contribute to increased manpower skills, increase research, further advance legislation, policy and planning frameworks, and build up infrastructure facilities. Moreover, as demonstrated in the Economic Study (Annex 3), without the Programme, the islands would be more vulnerable to natural hazards such as cyclones, droughts, floods, such as flooding and tropical cyclones, will be exacerbated, particularly when considering future impact from climate change.

The Programme is expected to yield significant economic benefits in the five countries both from reduced losses from hydrometeorological hazards, and enhanced productivity of resultant climate risk-informed sectoral planning. The Programme will also contribute to increased resilience by reducing exposure and vulnerability to natural hazards through a multi-hazard risk information and vulnerability assessments into targeted early warning and decision-support systems to enhance the resilience of businesses and household income generating activities. This will be complemented with improved disaster preparedness, response capacity and recovery mechanisms that enable the promised benefits of the early warning systems to be realized.10 In particular, the introduction of Forecast-based Financing (FbF) in the five countries is expected to provide a new mechanism for rapid response actions. This has been shown to minimize the damage and loss caused by climate-related hazards and reduce the need for humanitarian assistance in the aftermath.

Improved disaster risk management planning and preparedness are expected to yield economic gains – for example, due to the use of sector-specific impact-based climate products in health services, fisheries and agriculture. In particular, short-term and seasonal forecasts facilitate businesses to implement dynamic management strategies that can be used to reduce exposure and vulnerability to natural hazards.

Coastal management agencies can use long-term climate projections to develop suitable zoning plans. Thus,

# Gender-responsive development impact

The Programme will promote gender-responsive development in accordance with GCF policies and guidelines, including ensuring that expert advice on gender is used in planning and designing frameworks, policies and legislation and in the delivery of workshops, training exercises and capacity building activities. The Programme will also support countries to achieve their targets for striving for 50/50 participation; using best-practice consultation techniques when co-designing communication methods and information products with communities to ensure the outputs suit end-users, such as women’s groups, youth, and other vulnerable groups. This will help to increase the resilience of disaster responders during and after climate-related disasters. Culturally informed gender expertise is available in the Programme through its Gender Focal Points to introduce key concepts to what can be a gender-blind field, drawing on lessons learnt from related activities.

Annex 8 details the Gender Analysis of the Programme and outlines an Action Plan to ensure that gender-responsive development is mainstreamed into all aspects of the Programme. Gender equality will be included in the Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) framework of the Programme, as detailed in Annex 11.

## Impact of COVID-19 on the Global Observing System - a potential co-benefit of the Programme

A comparison of Switzerland to any of the five Pacific island countries illustrates the need to fundamentally change how we observe climate. The cost of observations for Switzerland is roughly US$20 million per year, while the five Pacific island countries (Cook Islands (Area: 183,000 km²), GDP of USD 524.3 million would amount to ~ USD 17,500; the Nauru (Area: 450,000 km²), GDP of USD 43 million would amount to ~ USD 1096; for Palau (Area: 603,939 km²), GDP of USD 28.7 million would amount to ~ USD 480; for the Federated States of Micronesia (Area: 705,563 km², GDP of USD 1.3 billion) would amount to ~ USD 646; and for Tuvalu (Area: 749.5 km²), GDP of USD 42.5 million would amount to ~ USD 58,000) are all significantly poorer than Switzerland. Given the small size of these countries, a similar share of GCF spending for observations would cover less than half the cost of sensors for each country. The Programme will support countries in developing national policies and strategies to cover the full costs of observations. It is also unrealistic to expect that the countries will be able to sustain the provision of observations over their territories or territorial waters on nations and “aid” resources alone.

Any lack of observations over one area negatively impacts the quality of the forecasts and analysis products generated by the Global Observing System (GOS). The GOS has already lost 90% of its observational parts have already been severely affected due to COVID-19. Aircraft observations have drastically declined, with a loss of around 85% in the Northern Hemisphere and up to 90% in the tropics and Southern Hemispheres, where there are already fewer observations available. The availability of satellite data has also decreased by about 20%. A significant part of this decline is suspected cause is reliance on manned observation stations subject to lockdown. For ocean observations, the main impact has been on voluntary ship observations, which are down around 80% compared to the pre-pandemic period. This is due to a combination of reduced funding for ship operations and the Global Shipborne Drifter Network (GSDN) and the Systematic Observations Financing Facility (SOFF). A transition to automation will be required in order to maintain the quality of observations, with the GOS relying increasingly on dependence on data of opportunity and improve resilience. Through the development of national policies for financing climate services (Sub-activity 1.1.4), the Programme will engage with SOFF to ensure that the five


---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 138 OF 167

## Impact of COVID-19 on lives, livelihoods and assets – a potential co-benefit of implementing Forecast-based Financing (FbF) and integrated disaster risk management approaches

As the Pacific Island countries enter their cyclone, drought, heatwaves or monsoon seasons, the potential for an “unprecedented double dose” of COVID-19 and climate hazards is increasing. The UNDRR Asia Pacific Regional Office (APRO) has identified a number of key areas where FbF can be used to support disaster risk management approach that includes high levels of disaster preparedness and accelerated disaster risk reduction across sectors. The dual challenge of climate change and a global pandemic highlights the need for innovative approaches, such as FbF, to complement existing disaster risk management efforts and enhance multi-hazard early warning systems. Furthermore, when Forecast-based Financing is combined with other responses to a climate shock or resilience or disaster risk reduction activities, the co-benefits are maximised:

* A 2018 return on investment study in Nepal implementing the approach found that USD 22 million can be saved per year by using FbF for early warning and response compared to USD 47.5 million over 20 years. USD 30, 42 and 46 kg of CO₂ emissions can be saved per dollar invested, after deducting the investment cost.
* A 2018 USAID study on Ethiopia, Kenya and Somalia indicates that early response to drought, combined with safety net transfers and resilience-building activities, could over a 15-year period save USD 4.3 billion, or an average of USD 287 million per year.
* A 2019 World Bank report carried out in Indonesia and the Philippines shows that using a forecast-based system would lower the cost of an emergency response by 50 percent and that the net cost of a late humanitarian response is four to seven times higher than multi-year resilience-building.

The above studies exemplify the economic co-benefits of implementing Forecast-based Financing mechanisms alongside other disaster risk management approaches.

### D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

**Pacific Small Island Developing States (SIDS) are among the most vulnerable countries to climate change. The World Risk Index 2018 unequivocally states that disaster risk—the risk that an extreme natural event will cause loss of life or damage to property and infrastructure—has increased in SIDS due to climate-related hazards if the five Pacific SIDS covered by this GCF Programme (Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, Samoa and Tonga).**

* Their geography, which maximises their exposure to ocean changes—many islands are less than a kilometre wide and effectively consist of long coastlines. Land mass accounts for only around 2% of the total area.
* Most people depend on subsistence agriculture and fishing for their livelihoods and their profitable industries, notably tourism, are very climate-sensitive. They are remote from markets and have small populations and narrow resource bases.


---
**Page 133**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 128 OF 167

* These countries are resource-poor, lack resilience to shocks and have small economies with many pressing claims on public funds—a bad year for cyclones can cause damage costing more than a small country's GDP.
* Effective communication of climate forecasts and extreme weather warnings requires expensive but robust technology and human resources, both in short supply.
* In some countries, women have limited access to or interaction with the public domain and their representation is low. Women are often disproportionately affected by disasters, and gender-based violence exacerbates this. The specific needs of old people and people with disabilities are usually limited.
* Data on human development indicators are available. The following aggregate figures on population and GDP disguise the inequality in the three countries with high GDP per capita.

These factors contribute individually and collectively to the very high vulnerability of the populations of the five programme countries. The country profiles in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2) provide more detail.

Financial, economic, social and institutional context

Needs of communities and stakeholders are central to this Programme, which has strong elements on climate information products and outreach for informed adaptation actions, as well as MHEWs at national and sub-national levels. The report highlights the vulnerability of small island states (SIS) in general and those highlighted by SIDS as most vulnerable, which includes SIDS such as Tuvalu experience significantly larger per capita losses, amounting to four years per person since 1980. The report also indicates that the number of people exposed to floods and tropical cyclones is increasing at a rate of about 2% annually. In addition, there are significant links between capital investment or social expenditure. SIDS top all of the regional risk rankings. Their combined average expenditure on climate change adaptation is estimated to be around 10–70% of their total GDP in Europe and Central Asia. Similarly, the AAL in SIDS is equivalent to almost 20% of their total social expenditure, compared only to 1.19% in North America and less than 1% in Europe and Central Asia.


---
**Page 134**
---

At the national level, the five countries' NMHSs have achieved some progress in institutional capacity, technical meteorological and climatological capacity, project delivery, project management and communication skills during the last ten years, with colleague support from neighbouring developed country NMHSs. However, they are still not able to consistently deliver the climate services or any of the national ocean services that their populations need. The five countries have identified a need for strengthening their institutional capacity in a Programme simultaneously addressing the structural obstacles to end-to-end service provision can effect a lasting systemic change. This can only be achieved through strengthening the institutional capacity of existing organisations, if the change is to be sustained and self-perpetuating, as this Programme is designed to achieve.

D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

This Programme is the outcome of a two-year strategic consultation process with the five self-selected partners. The results are reflected in the Roadmap for NMHSs in the review of the Pacific Islands Meteorology Strategy and their interests and priorities are reflected in the current PIMS 2017–2026. The five NMHSs also consider climate change adaptation as a priority area in their national strategies and plans, with the priorities and activities in this Programme address those recorded in the Roadmap by each country and are the starting point for the funding commitment.

All five countries have identified climate information, early warning and/or disaster risk reduction as priority areas in their national strategies and plans:

- The Cook Islands' National Sustainable Development Plan 2016–2020—Te Kaveinga Nu—has as its 3rd goal to "Ensure that all people are protected from the adverse effects of climate change". Their Joint National Action Plan (JNAP) –Are we resilient? has four key Strategic Areas: governance, monitoring, disaster management and risk reduction and climate change adaptation.

- Niue's Climate Strategy 2015–2030 is to "Ensure that all people are protected from the adverse effects of climate change" and calls on developed countries to prevent further harm. The Palau Climate Change Policy Framework addresses climate change adaptation, mitigation and disaster risk management.

- The Marshall Islands' National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy 2014–2035 (NCAAS), National Risk Management 2014–2035 (NJRMP) and its National Climate Change Policy Framework 2011 on Adaptation and Reducing Risks for a Climate Resilient Future also prioritise better data and communication.

- In Tuvalu's Te Kakenga III—National Strategy for Sustainable Development 2016–2020, climate adaptation is the first priority. To Kaniva—the Tuvalu National Climate Change Policy 2012–2021—describes climate change as a major threat to their development.

Details of each country's principal policy documents and strategies relating to climate information services is summarised in its country profile, in the Feasibility Study.


A Consultation was held with all five countries on national strategies, policies and agreements was re-submitted to the Green Climate Fund in late 2018 and agreed as the basis for developing the Funding Proposal. In-country consultations were then undertaken in the five countries with a wide range of national stakeholders including government officials, climate change focal points, meteorological services, disaster management agencies, and civil society organisations. The consultation process was designed to be consistent with the priorities of national and regional plans and strategies, and with past, current and planned activities.

The Summary of Consultations (Annex 7) details engagement with NMHSs, disaster management agencies,


---
**Page 135**
---

government agencies responsible for climate-sensitive sectors, media organisations, civil society organisations, women’s community organisations, UN agencies active in climate adaptation in the Pacific and regional technical organisations.

The countries have identified a range of potential activities suggested in the Concept Note to align with each country's highest priorities and most practicable inputs. There is a lot of commonality among the countries in the kind of interventions they believe are most likely to succeed and to effect lasting change.


From this, the five Pacific countries agreed on a set of priority interventions that would be included in their national adaptation plans. They also agreed on a budgeted Programme of activities with each country that will enable them to build their institutional, human resources and infrastructural capacities to provide high quality, well-understood climate services for decision-making at all levels. The five countries have identified the key components of a functional system.


Each country has indicated its endorsement of the Programme by providing a No Objection Letter and committing to allocate funding and resources to its implementation.

In July 2019, UNEP gave a presentation on the agreed Programme at the Fifth Pacific Meteorological Council Meeting to all PAC members, who strongly endorsed it. Country NMHS Directors also referred and supported the programme.


Consultation with the five NDAs, NMHS Directors and other country representatives has continued throughout the iterative revisions of the Programme proposal, since its first submission to GCF in November 2019.

### D.E. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The Governments of Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands (RMI) and Tuvalu are requesting a GCF grant to enable the efficient and effective delivery of the proposed Programme interventions in their countries. The programme is expected to be delivered through the provision of financial instrument to enhance climate and ocean information services in the five Programme countries during 2019-2023, with the aim of improving the effectiveness and efficiency of the use of public resources for prioritisation of immediate development needs.* This investment will directly address financial, technical capacity and coordination barriers to the effective delivery of climate information and mid-harvest early warning services in the five countries. The proposed programme is expected to be delivered through the GCF Investment, the Programme is expected to develop information products with commercial value that will also contribute to the achievement of the SDGs.


The Cost-Benefit Analysis (CBA) has identified and assessed the costs and benefits of the proposed interventions for each of the five countries and has made inherent trade-offs explicit. The feasibility of the investments was tested by comparing the present cost of the investment with its present economic value at discounted NPV is positive). A 9% discount rate has been applied as recommended by the Asian Development Bank (ADB, 2017). The period of analysis covers 10 years over the lifetime of the proposed components. Sensitivity analysis was conducted to test uncertainty for key parameters and assumptions. The results indicate that all investments are economically viable at discounted NPV are positive. The economic EIRR exceeds the discount rates in each instance making all proposed investments economically viable. Sensitivity analysis has been used to test uncertainty for key parameters and assumptions. The results indicate that all investments are economically viable at discounted cost benefit changes, the EIRR remained above the 9% threshold in all cases, demonstrating that the proposed interventions will be both financially sound and efficient in addition to the delivery of considerable social benefits.


The benefits calculated in the CBA are in the form of avoided economic damages and losses. Avoided economic damages include impacts on infrastructure and physical assets (particularly coastal), crops


---
**Page 136**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDS FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 132 OF 167

Effective climate services and impact-based MHEWS developed by the Programme have the potential to partially reduce the loss of costs and crop loss. Improved hazard information and dissemination, providing better preparedness and longer lead times to evacuate, move content and harvest crops, are key components in reducing the economic losses associated with extreme weather events and disasters arising from the disaster, lasting up to several years. The Programme aims to increase sectoral and community resilience by strengthening climate services and early warning systems, resulting in improved productivity and avoided losses to agriculture, livestock, fisheries, commerce and tourism.

The Programme will build on existing networks, strengths and planned actions in the five Pacific Island countries (FIPs) to provide a comprehensive, integrated, and coherent approach to climate information and complementary manner. The development and implementation of National Frameworks for Climate Services and climate finance policies by the five FIPs host countries will provide the fundamental framework for the Programme’s activities over its 2017-2026 term of the Programme. The benefits resulting from investing in climate services are far greater than the costs, as they can reduce disaster losses by up to 50% and save lives (UNISDR, 2015). Evidence also suggests that the benefits of systematic investment in strengthening the operational global regional and national climate services infrastructure will be realized over a period of time, ranging from one to thirty. The implementation of a holistic and integrated approach to climate information and early warning services delivery that concurrently addresses the root causes and barriers will have long-term efficiency and effectiveness gains. This is particularly important for countries in the Pacific region where investment in conjunction with disaster risk management strategies and investments – such as the community level capacity building, infrastructure development, and insurance schemes – can be more cost-effective and enhance the sustainability of last-mile investments.

Efficiency and effectiveness will be further enhanced through the strengthening national institutions including the Pacific Community (SPC) and the five Pacific Regional Climate Services 2017-2026. The Programme will provide coordinated regional interventions (Programme Result 4) to optimise synergies amongst the five Programme countries. Regional knowledge management and training activities, as well as the development of a common climate services platform that makes more use of data and information, communications and standard operating procedures. The Programme will leverage existing national and international initiatives and best practices in the region for the sharing of best practices for climate and ocean services and disaster risk management are used in the scope of the Programme. These interventions will be aligned with the five countries’ National Climate Change Strategies and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including: i) Innovative and cost-saving technologies for observations, monitoring and prediction; ii) Operations and Maintenance; iii) Enhancing institutional effectiveness through Capacity Building and Training; iv) Strengthening climate services and early warning systems through impact-based forecasting and Forecast-based Financing; v) Options for ensuring long-term financial sustainability of the Programme, including: i) Innovative and cost-saving technologies for observations, monitoring and prediction; ii) Operations and Maintenance; iii) Enhancing institutional effectiveness through Capacity Building and Training; iv) Strengthening climate services and early warning systems through impact-based forecasting and Forecast-based Financing; v) Options for ensuring long-term financial sustainability of the Programme.

Without the Programme, economic losses incurred through increased frequency and intensity of climate-related disasters will continue to rise. The costs associated with these disasters are exacerbated by the negative impacts of potential changes in weather patterns. Climate-resilient sustainable development in the five Pacific Island SIDS is impeded by the shortage of development capital and human resource capacity; a lack of institutional capacity for planning, implementing and monitoring climate change adaptation frameworks; and inadequate infrastructure facilities. Investment in public services is lacking, and technical expertise to manage climate risks is limited. The provision of disaster risk management services is limited and not generally the focus of local governments, who are busy providing basic services and infrastructure. None of the five Programme countries currently conducts systematic, comprehensive economic cost-benefit analysis (CBA) or impact assessment studies on the costs associated with climate


---
**Page 137**
---

related disaster data. This Programme will implement a multi-pronged approach to address these shortcomings.

In lengthy and detailed planning sessions with the individual meteorological offices, partners and wider stakeholders, state-of-the-art technologies and practices have been identified, evaluated, and proposed for application. Partners with an extensive knowledge and understanding, with a strong history in climate related research based in Australia, New Zealand and the Pacific have provided detailed technical terms of reference for the development of the various components of the Programme implementation plan.

Further details can be found in Annex 3: Economic Analysis.

E

Requirements established in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RM and Tuvalu:
- Observation station capabilities improved according to WMO standards e.g., new-upgraded AWS and new-upgraded AWOS stations installed.
- Air-based remote sensing (GNSS/IR) receiver and hydrogen peroxide analyser installed in Cook Islands; 2 new-upgraded AWOS stations installed in Niue, 8 new-upgraded AWS and 1 new-upgraded AWOS station installed in Palau, 3 new-upgraded AWOS stations installed in RM and Tuvalu.
- Low-cost weather radar system established and operational at Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RM and Tuvalu.
- Very Small Aperture Terminal (VSAT) communications system to ensure continuous data exchange with AWS sensors weather installed in Tuvalu.
- Compliance with WMO regulatory guidance issued for the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RM and Tuvalu.

Data and data management enhanced so that historical as well as real-time data can be exchanged between stations. All observations of Essential Climate Variables (ECVs) are exchanged hourly from all stations to one Global Surface Network station in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RM and Tuvalu

## Strategy for data archival processes and systems, including a draft operating manual for ensuring data integrity, retention policy with respect to migration, implemented in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu.

### Best Practice Package Meteorological Forecasting System to support cost-effective and efficient data collection in alignment with the needs of Niue and Tuvalu.

ISO standards are being implemented in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, RMI and Tuvalu.

## 2.2 Strengthen ocean and climate modelling and early warning forecasting
- This Activity will establish end-to-end ocean services and strengthened climate information and early warning systems for the Pacific Island countries in recognition of the fact that Pacific island countries have some of the largest global Exclusive Economic Zones (EEZs) relative to their land area by an average factor of 300.

### Stakeholders focused on improving coral bleaching monitoring and early warning efficacy in RAR region, invited from Cook Islands, Niue, Papua New Guinea, were invited to participate virtually (with a focus on knowledge transfer).

- **Data products and services developed:** Decision making tools including: real-time interfaces with the model output, automated reports, impact-based decision support needs, impact-based vulnerability assessment, univariate forecasting tool, off-shore wind energy potential.
- **Satellite data:** Sentinel-2 satellite-based monitoring tool, and high-resolution ocean models and particle tracking models in Niutao in Cook Islands, and a PaC-DOS multi-component model (including 7-day high resolution wave forecasts, 7-day high resolution output from ocean circulation models; and 14-day wave forecast models) for Palau.
- **Quality control data:** Data made directly available to NAMO and the public is in near real time and integrated into local databases and regional networks including the Pacific Ocean Observing System (POOS), public access, underpinning model application in Cook Islands, Niue, and Tuvalu.
- **National Ocean Portal:** Web portal has been developed that provides relevant climate information for modeling (where available) real-time.

## Knowledge and capacity of NMHS staff in Nauru
- Issuing climate impact-based forecasts and warnings, including the development of tailored climate services for climate-sensitive sectors.
- Climate Risk Early Warning System (CREWS) and Early Action Response Workshops (EAR Watch) workshops conducted with the Ministry of Health and Twvala; customized climate information systems for health and drought management.
- RMF: climate and sector risk assessments harmonized between the NMHS, Nauru Meteorological Service, and Fisheries Department, in the context of existing planning data and information products that are already useful to these sectors in Nauru.
- Enhanced existing hybrid multi-model climate prediction system (PMCS) with local station-based temperature sensors; provide monthly forecast data on a weekly basis with the ability to generate sector-specific tailored climate information products upon request in Cook Islands, Niue, Samoa, and Tuvalu.

## Customized products
- Customized products, reports and advisories for climate-sensitive sectors generated to provide up-to-date climate change risk managers for early action responses in Nauru and Tuvalu.
- Access to reliable observation records on major weather events; climate records held in existing databases compatible with the existing COMS for Tuvalu.

## Harmonised climate data and information management

This Activity will enhance the dissemination and communication of climate-related early warnings based on the enhanced data generated by the National Data Programme. The Programme will particularly focus on strengthening organisational and operational processes in communities to enable people and communities to receive and understand early warning messages, including impending hazard events. Mitigation will be supported through the development of communications products tailored to each community’s needs.

### 3.1 Improve warning dissemination and communication

This Activity will integrate climate data and information into decision-making and emergency response strategies across all sectors through the development and implementation of national climate data and information strategies.

#### 2.5.1 Establish and implement national climate data and information strategies

- National climate data and information strategies established, harmonised and implemented in Cook Islands, Niue, Palau and RMI
- National data, research and information network (e.g., EWS, SMS, etc.) established

## E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements from EEs perspective

The AE will engage a consultant to design a performance monitoring and evaluation framework to track the Programme's progress towards achieving its targets. The Programme Coordinator in the PMU, under the overall guidance of the AE, will be responsible for implementing this framework.

### Monitoring and technical advisory services provided by WMO/SIPEP Pacific Met Centre

The PMU will provide technical assistance to EEs in Palau, Nauru, Niue, Cook Islands, Federated States of Micronesia, Marshall Islands, Tuvalu, Kiribati, Solomon Islands, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, and Tonga.

### Monitoring and evaluation framework

The AE will engage a consultant to design a performance monitoring and evaluation framework to track the Programme's progress towards achieving its targets. The PMU will be responsible for implementing this framework as outlined in the monitoring and evaluation framework. This will be achieved by:

1. Monitoring progress against the mid-term and final targets.
2. Reporting the programme performance to the PSC and PMU based on inputs from national EEs (e.g., baseline, annual performance reports, mid-point and end of Programme).
3. Coordinating with national EEs to ensure that data collection is consistent across all EEs.

### Specific attention will be paid to ensuring gender responsiveness, uptake of climate and early warning information, and environmental and social safeguards (ESS).

In addition to the Programme monitoring and evaluation framework, the PMU will also develop a specific monitoring and evaluation framework for each EE. The PMU will support the five countries in enhancing human resources and expertise, aiming to have fully operational national EEs by 2015.

### Training of EEs

The PMU will then organise training for EEs in data collection and analysis, and on the Programme cycle, particularly on effective monitoring and reporting of activities. All training should take a strengths-based approach with an emphasis on capacity building. The training should be reinforced by follow-up training at least annually to ensure that monitoring activities are collecting meaningful information and that the information is able to be used both for adjusting inputs throughout the programme implementation phase and for informing the development of the next Programme Action Plan (PAP). During the Mid-Term Evaluation and Terminal Evaluation an evaluation consultant will validate a sample of the data collected through these monitoring tools.

### EE feedback

EE feedback should also be sought in order to help implementing partners in their countries to assess the impact of their activities using methods that measure change over time and provide evidence on the effectiveness of the interventions. In particular, for very small countries it should be assumed that all staff and local consultants will need professional development in monitoring and reporting with at least annual follow-up training to reinforce learning.

### EE reports

EEs will submit quarterly progress reports to the PMU and the PMU will consolidate the reports and submit them to UNEP as the AE. In turn, UNEP will submit annual performance reports and semi-annual financial reports to GCF. The detailed reporting timelines are as follows:

Under the PCAA, each EE is to report to UNEP as follows:
a. Progress reports: by 30 July to January to June;

## Footnote
**Footnote:** For indicator A1.2, monitoring of the mid-term and Final Targets will take into account uptake and implementation of recommended climate change best livelihood options and actions to be outlined in the Climate Sector Action Plan and Communication Plans (to be developed under Activity 1.1).


---
**Page 161**
---

b. Annual Performance Reports on or before 1 February;
c. Quarterly financial reports by 15 January, 15 April, 15 July, and 15 October;
d. Annual audited statements by 30 April;

e. Final Annual Performance Report (APR) within 6 months of Programme completion.

UNEP (AE) reports to the GCF:
a. Annual Performance Reports by 1 March;
b. Semi-Annual Financial Statements by 30 March and 30 September;
c. Mid-Term Evaluation report: halfway through Programme;
d. Final APR: within 6 months of Programme completion;

Monitoring will also be undertaken by the AE through supervision visits and field missions to track implementation progress and changes and strategically plan the way forward. The Programmes reporting requirements are described in section B-4. UNEP will not be responsible for managing the Mid-Term Evaluation and will instead engage a third-party independent consultant to carry out the Mid-Term Evaluation, which will provide an assessment of programme performance at the Programme's mid-point. This will be a formative exercise and will cover whether the Programme is on track to achieve its intended outcomes by Programme completion.

The UNEP Executive Director (ED) will be responsible for undertaking the Independent Terminal Evaluation (TE) at the end of Programme implementation, which is a summative evaluation, and will liaise with the UNEP Task Manager to ensure that the TE meets the requirements of the GCF. The TE will use the most recent standard evaluation criteria (e.g., strategic relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, likelihood of impact and sustainability) as well as the Programme's specific objectives, outputs, and results in line with its mission. Each evaluation criterion will be rated using a six-point rating scheme and a weighted average will be determined to provide an overall performance rating for the Programme as a whole. Where there are any differences between the UNEP Task Manager and the Evaluation Office on the criteria or weighting of each determination, it will be resolved by the Evaluation Office when the Terminal Evaluation report is finalised. The draft TE Report will be shared with the UNEP Task Manager and the GCF for review before being submitted to the Evaluation Office. Formal comments on the report will be shared by the Evaluation Office in an open and transparent manner. This evaluation report will be publicly disclosed and will be followed by a response from the UNEP Task Manager.

The costs for results monitoring and performance evaluation are included in the Programme budget.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk assessment and mitigation measures (plus 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Limited interaction between NMRGs and the end-users.

**Mitigation Measures**

The National Frameworks for Climate Services will be established in each country to generate regular liaison between NMRGs and the government agencies responsible for climate sensitive sectors, with dedicated programme-hired local consultants to ensure that climate information is integrated into the functions of each sector. Specific attention will also be given to ensuring that climate change considerations are included in the consideration of climate change into their policy making and planning. Annual National Climate Outlook Forums (NCOFs) will be held at least once a year, with the participation of NMRGs, government agencies, and the monitoring undertaken by departmental liaison officers and the national climate data consultants will also provide feedback.

UNICEF programmes currently oblige NMRGs to work through government ministries and departments and national agencies such as disaster management organisations to deliver their products and information to communities. This Programme enables NMRGs to work directly with NGOs, CSOs, Red Cross Red Crescent Commissions, local governments, and other relevant stakeholders in the delivery of climate services to communities in delivering training and awareness workshops on climate and ocean information products and disseminating them through appropriate channels. The programme will also support the capacity building process and their contributions have informed the identification of activities and Programme methodology.

The Programme will strengthen capacity to enable the NMRGs have resources to work with communities and other stakeholders, including the newly established structured feedback mechanisms. NGOs, CSOs, local government councils, and representatives from other participating local committees will be members of the National Coordination Committee.

This measure will reduce the risk to Low

### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Medium |

**Description**

Countries unable to maintain new and upgraded climate and ocean monitoring equipment.

**Mitigation Measures**

The costs for operation, maintenance and repairs for new equipment have been factored into the Programme budget, and maintenance and calibration programmes have already been agreed with the participating countries. Wherever possible, low maintenance equipment has been considered. The Cook Islands, Palau and RMI all have existing climate monitoring systems that will be upgraded under this programme. The programme has budgeted training programs for observers, programme-hired local consultants, entry level staff and technicians. The Programme will also engage technical partners to support all essential training on equipment, AWIS, calibration etc. Training and calibration manuals/information will also be provided by the


---
**Page 163**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 139 OF 167

Technical partners for some highly technical equipment such as wave buoys, relevant partners will maintain the items over the programme lifetime while building national capacity to take over this function. The value of the data collected from such equipment is likely to generate funding for its maintenance beyond the term of the project. The Global Earth Observation System of Systems (GEOSS) and the Global Atmosphere Watch (GAW) and partners for the Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) is a likely a probable source of ongoing maintenance funding. The SREP will provide funding for the Technical Officer to oversee the Programme’s Operation and Maintenance (O&M) for up to 20 years after the Programme’s implementation period. SREP will also contribute post-project funding of the ICT Officer position as well as associated fees including hosting of the data portal.

These measures will reduce the risk to Low.


## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Medium |

Description

There may be limited local expertise to fill some of the technical positions in the EEs and NMHSs, which could delay the implementation of the Programme.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Cook Islands, Nauru and RMI have existing national project management units or offices that will provide the basis for their National Coordinating Committees (NCCs) for this Programme. Technical training workshops and on-site visits will be provided by experts oversided within the country and the Programme will provide focused on-site training, short-term courses, mentoring and attachment training in-country and at regional technical agencies and institutions. The Programme has factored in targeted and regular recruitment of a range of professional mid-level to senior staff for the next five years in the full range of required skills, including maintenance of the new technology, workshop facilitation, effective communication with non-scientists and service beyond the programme’s lifetime.

These measures will reduce the risk to Low.


## Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

Description

Communities do not respond to interventions promoting behavioural change.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The paradigm shift to science-informed, science-based adaptation is a key measure of Programme success, both by government agencies and by island communities. The Programme includes a range of activities directed at changing community behaviour—careful, measured and gradual introduction of new technologies and practices, coupled with intensive discussions of traditional climate knowledge and its congruence with NMHSs’ forecasting methods. Forecast-based Financing and co-development of Early Action Protocols (EAPs), practice drills for emergency response, and other activities will help to build community capacity and improve the accessibility of NMHSs advice through use of local terms and clear language.
