**Page 164**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUND PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 188 OF 167

## Selected Risk Factor 5

### Category: Technical and operational
#### Probability: Medium
#### Impact: Medium

The Programme will be complex from both a technical and operational perspective, with involvement of multiple countries, regional and national stakeholders, which may cause difficulties during implementation.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

As outlined in Section B.4, at Programme level, a Programme Steering Committee (PSC) will be established to provide high-level oversight and guidance towards achieving Programme objectives. A Programme Management Unit (PMU) will also be established to manage the Programme on behalf of the GCF and oversee national programmes. The PMU will also contract, coordinate, manage, oversee and report on deliverables for programme implementation. The PMU will have a dedicated team of experts who will provide guidance and source expertise as needed on Programme management, financial management, procurement, etc., to ensure that the Programme is implemented in line with GCF policies. Quarterly progress report and quarterly financial reports to UNEP; enabling UNEP to report to the GCF in line with GCF requirements. If necessary, the PMU will draw upon the UNEP Task Manager for support.

At country level, a Country Coordination Committee (CCC) will be established to oversee the overall implementation of the Programme. It will coordinate national plans, budgeting and country-specific procurements, progress and expenditure reporting. It will facilitate cooperation among the implementing agencies, address issues passed up to it by national partners and stakeholders and manage any conflicts or overlaps.

These measures will reduce the risk to Low.


## 2. AMIL/FTF and Prohibited Practices compliance due diligence assessment

### Category: MTTF
#### Probability**: Low
#### Impact***: Low (45% of Project Value)

*MTTF*: Financial assistance provided by the United Nations, or other relevant sanctioning authorities (including the World Bank Debarred List).

**Prohibited Practices**: Abuse, Conflict of Interest, Corruption, Retaliation against Whistleblowers or Witnesses, as well as Fraudulent, Coercive, Collusive, and Dishonest Practices


---
**Page 165**
---

The risk of the proceeds being utilized towards money laundering or terrorist financing is Low.

The five Programme countries – Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, Marshall Islands and Tuvalu have all acceded and/or ratified the United Nations Convention Against Corruption (UNCAC).

### Cook Islands
- The Cook Islands has a reputation as a safe and well-regulated jurisdiction. It is economically dependent on tourism and fishing industries that do not contain attracting legitimate investors with funds and assets that are untainted by criminality.
- The Financial Transactions Reporting Act 2017 aligns with the FATF Recommendations, which require reporting institutions to report suspicious transactions.
- Reporting institutions must have in place compliance systems to assist with the prevention, detection and protection of financial misconduct. The Ministry of Finance and Economic Management (MF EM) is a key body that oversees the implementation of these requirements.

### Niue
- The Financial Transactions Reporting Act 2006 (FTR) sets out details on Hawke’s framework for compliance with the FATF Recommendations.
- The Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) of the Bank of Niue fulfills its role as the Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU).
- The following government agencies are also involved in AMLOFT operations: Customs, Inland Revenue Department, Reserve Bank of New Zealand, and the Ministry of Justice (including Tax and Customs); and the Niu Bank and Monetary Board.
- The Terrorism Suppression and Transnational Crimes Act 2006 implements the United Nations Resolution 1373 and Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora. Section 54 of this Act is part of the Proceeds of Crime Act 1998 and amends it to strengthen it; ensure that the Act is always up-to-date with international standards, and ensure that it does not duplicate the FTR 2006.

### Palau
- The Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) of the Republic of Palau fulfills its role as a financial intelligence unit under sections 151(3), 331(9c), 334(a)(i), 351(b)(a), 351(b)(b), 352(a)(ii), 352(c), 3220, and 332(6)(c) of the Money Laundering and Proceeds of Crime Act 2001 [Act (17 PNCA Chapter 38)] as amended.
- The Financial Intelligence Unit Act 2001 is part of the Proceeds of Crime Act 1998, which was amended in 2006. The Republic of Palau is authorised to promulgate regulations under sections 1019 and 10,133 of the Financial Institutions Act of 2001 [26 PNCA Chapter 10] as amended (Act). This Application applies also to Palau banks and other financial institutions.
- The Republic of Palau has enacted the Anti-Money Laundering Act 2014 in the Republic of Palau. The regulation addresses obligations placed on reporting institutions under the PALAU ANTI MONEY LAUNDERING ACT 2014, which is part of the Proceeds of Crime and financing of terrorism.
- The Palau Anti Money Laundering Act 2014 and the Financial Intelligence Unit Regulation on Anti Money Laundering Reporting Requirements are provided in the footnote link.


---
**Page 166**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 162 OF 167

## Marshall Islands - The Republic of the Marshall Island Banking Commission is the lead agency for AML/CFT in RMF in Majuro Atoll. The Banking Commission has the powers of a Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU), as a law enforcer over licensed banks, and as an AML/CFT supervisor of banks, financial institutions, and other entities subject to the Act.

The Commissioner of Banking is responsible for ensuring compliance with the Act under Title 17 Chapter 1 Banking Act 1987 to ensure the safety and soundness of the RMF banking sector and to promote public confidence in the banking system. The Commissioner of Banking reports annually to Section 104 of the Act, the Commissioner of Banking reports to the Minister of Finance, Banking and Postal Services. One of the key responsibilities of the Commissioner of Banking under Section 16 is to report suspicious transactions to the Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) in accordance with Section 23(5). Such reports are analysed and disseminated to law enforcement when there are reasonable grounds for money laundering or terrorist financing. The Republic of Marshall Islands Office of the Banking Commission issues an Annual Report.

The Financial Institution Supervision (FIS) division is responsible for the licensing and supervision of banks, credit unions, and other financial institutions subject to the Act under Section 102 of the Banking Act 1987 to ensure their safety and soundness and full compliance with AML statutes and regulations. The FIS division also supervises the RMF banking sector in accordance with the Act.

The Office of the Banking Commission has recently established in the formulation and revision of new operating policies and procedures, including new regulations and guidelines for licensed banks and other reporting entities. In addition, the Marshall Islands Financial Intelligence Unit (MI-FIU) is responsible for receiving suspicious transaction reports from the central point of contact for investigations involving RMF and the international police community.

## Tuvalu - Tuvalu acceded to the United Nations Convention Against Corruption on 4 September 2015 and deposited its instrument of accession with the Secretary-General of the United Nations. The Convention entered into force for Tuvalu on 4 October 2015. Several institutions are responsible for the fight against corruption in Tuvalu, including the Ministry of Justice, the Office of the Attorney General, Public Service Commission and Central Procurement Unit. Tuvalu's three financial institutions are National Bank of Tuvalu, Tuvalu Provincial National Fund and Development Bank of Tuvalu. The Government has established a National Anti-Corruption Committee to oversee the implementation by Tuvalu of the United Nations Convention against Corruption in 2017.

Furthermore, the Project Cooperation Agreement between UNEP and National Executing Entities will include warranties from each of the National Executing Entities to make all ensure compliance with the UN Fraud and Anti-Corruption Frameworks of the United Nations Secretariat, as well as the Green Climate Fund Policy on Prohibited Practices. There are no sanctions by the United Nations Security Council currently in force against Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, The Republic of the Marshall Islands or Tuvalu.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The purpose of the Environmental and Social Safeguards Screening (ESS) is to identify the risk category that the components of the proposed Programme will fall into. The scope and depth of the environmental and social safeguards will be commensurate to the level of risks and impacts determined in the screening as per ESS standards and GCF policies.

An initial screening by UNEP indicated that this Programme was likely to be categorised as Category C. This indicates that the proposed Programme is unlikely to have significant adverse environmental or social impacts. Any proposal rated as Category C does not require an Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) or any other form of ESIA. However, a summary of the Environmental and Social Performance Assessment (ESPA) has been included in Annex 1. Hence, this Programme is not expected to have significant environmental or social impacts.

The proposed Programme that potentially hold environmental or social risks involves improving meteorological and climate observation, monitoring, modelling and prediction capabilities across the five Pacific Island nations in question. These improvements aim to give better response capability and contribute to disaster risk reduction.

The improvements are centred around infrastructure-building: expansion of observation abilities and scope, and training and education. The latter, people-based activities carry no negative environmental or social impacts. However, the former is more complex as it involves construction interventions that may have very little physical footprint or impact. This leaves the infrastructure activities as the remaining potential risk.

All five countries will gain dual-polarisation X-band Doppler weather radar units that will greatly increase their capacity to monitor and predict severe weather events. The existing meteorological offices in each country will be upgraded or installed to strengthen the monitoring network towards compliance with the provisions of the World Meteorological Organization (WMO). This includes the installation of new weather stations, sensors, and measurement equipment in various locations; the upgrade of an existing meteorological office; and the construction of small meteorological offices on several islands.

Assessment of the proposed Programme is unlikely to have significant negative environmental and social impacts. The construction interventions are not of significant size and do not raise flags by nature or location, and land clearance and earthworks will not be extensive. No special mitigation will be required for these activities as they are in line with the existing practices in each country. There will be no government land, as formally indicated by the Directors of the five NMSs, and no persons will be displaced. No indigenous peoples will be affected.

Upon evaluation of the checklist from the initial screening done for UNEP in March and April of 2018, the only variation from the original assessment is in Safeguard Standard 7: Cultural Heritage. This variation specifies that there are no cultural heritage sites within the vicinity of the proposed new meteorological stations. This construction also relates to an additional safeguard noted for GCF funded projects, per the design and construction of new buildings. However, the size and location of these new buildings means they will not raise flags in terms of environmental or social impacts. The project team has confirmed that industry best practice is followed during their construction.

In order to protect local and indigenous peoples, a Grievance Redress Mechanism has been included in Annex 1. This mechanism will be implemented with submissions of any complaints or concerns around the execution of this proposed Programme. A disclosure statement has also been prepared per the GCF’s requirements for all projects. The statement is available on the Project page and on the Entity's website. The Programme will develop appropriate ESS, anti-corruption, anti-nepotism, child protection, human rights, etc., disclosure forms to be filed/signed by new staff, consultants and contractors before engagement in Programme activities.

### G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

This Funding Proposal includes a Gender Assessment and Action Plan in Annex 8, informed, analysed and complied with the following methodology:

- The content of this Annex draws from a country desk-review and ex-post gender analysis of Focus Country’s climate change policy. Country Action Plans through the action plan, and beneficiary identification (and enumeration), therefore are indicative.

The desk-review to collect data and information was conducted on existing literature drawn from: research studies; national and international development agencies; regional and global multilateral development programmes – UNDP South Pacific, United Nations Economic and Social Commission for Asia and the Pacific (UNESCAP); other international development banks (the World Bank and the Asian Development Bank [ADB]); and general development journals of Coastal Research, etc.]; and grey literature (government reports and policies).

These are considered as baseline information to be incorporated in the design and preparation of the Programme, to be further defined in the later stages of the Programmes.

### Mainstream Gender within Climate Information Services in Pacific Island Countries

Impacts of exposure to climate change and weather variations compound with other pre-existing vulnerabilities of Pacific Island countries. These are often disproportionately borne by women and the poorer and more vulnerable demographics – determined by the needs, opportunities and risks facing men and women. In this context, gender-responsive climate information services will play a unique and positive role in empowering women and girls to participate as active agents in decision-making processes; bolstering national meteorological institutions (NMIs) and climate data collection, analysis and interpretation.

### Gear Products towards End Users in Climate-Vulnerable Sectors

A World Bank report and a CGIAR report (see Annex 8 – Section 2.40) reiterate the importance of climate information services for the Pacific Island countries, and how these cannot simply be limited to technical capacity building or training. The need is for more user-friendly and gender-sensitive climate information products that are tailored to the needs of end users in vulnerable sectors such as agriculture, fishing and pearl farming.

### Utilise Community Information Channels and Local Knowledge

A complementary approach to creating user-friendly and gender-sensitive climate service products is to work with communities to ensure that they have the capacity to inform these outputs. Despite the vulnerability of Pacific islands to disaster and climate change variability, research shows that men and women in these countries are actively engaged in traditional practices and community-based learning environments to mitigate impacts and risks for generations. Through a variety of traditional practices and unique local knowledge systems, communities have developed strategies and methods which have managed climate and disaster loss burdens. These methods include both community-based practices: traditional systems of exchange/giving; management of natural resources; weather pattern interpretation and forecasting; and the use of indigenous knowledge systems.

Conducting mapping exercises, informed by participant observation and consultative approach, will be key to understanding the needs of women and men in these communities as well as their roles within decision-making groups, and investing in gender-relevant or women’s groups. These exercises will assist in empowering women in their roles as key actors in climate-vulnerable sectors; encourage active

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximate page 1)

The financial management and procurement within the Programme will be guided by UN Financial Regulations and Rules (UNFR) and UNEP’s own internal rules of UNEP, which follow International Public Sector Accounting Standards (IPSAS). are promulgated pursuant to the Financial Regulation Act. The financial management and procurement processes are managed in accordance with UN rules and procedures, including eligibility criteria, proposal evaluation process, quality assurance and control, project monitoring and supervision. UNEP is audited annually by the UN Board of Auditors and the GCF.

In line with UNEP reporting guidelines outlined in section E.7, reports to summarise the disbursement and planned demands for Programme funding will be prepared and submitted to a UNEP Programme Officer who will ensure that they are consistent with established standards and methodologies applied in past projects such as those implemented using GEF modalities.

UN financial regulations and rules require the segregation of duties and safeguards to ensure compliance with the UNFR. The UNEP’s own internal rules, which follow the UNFR, include specific regulations, rules and policies. UNEP’s modality for project implementation, in the case of a national project, requires that all EEs sign an agreement with UNEP and the GCF prior to the start of work. This will be subject to the conditions that are defined under the legal instruments (Project Cooperation Agreement(s); PCAs) to be signed between UNEP and the EEIs. The PCAs will prescribe each EE’s financial obligations related to the project, including payment terms.

The PCAs specifically require annual audits to be undertaken by a recognised firm of certified public accountants or, for governments, by a government auditor. This auditor should state whether the GCF programme is in compliance with the UNFR and UNEP’s own internal rules.

As all five EEs are national government authorities, they follow their government’s financial and procurement rules and standards, and aid coordination agencies standards, as described in section B.4. High level negotiations will be held between UNEP and the EEIs to determine the capacity of each EE to undertake the proposed Programme’s inception phase. UNEP will conduct more detailed assessments of the EEs’ capacity to undertake the programme’s inception phase and will agree with the EEIs on a procurement plan, which is part of the procurement monitoring plan which will be agreed between UNEP and the EEIs. The same will be done for the Regional Technical Partners supporting execution in the countries (SPRC, SREP, BoM, NiWRA, NOA, UNDP). This will ensure that all EEs are able to undertake their responsibilities as defined by their members or their governments’ financial and procurement policies and guidelines and are audited annually.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP147

## Proposal name: Enhancing Climate Information and Knowledge Services for resilience in 5 island countries of the Pacific Ocean

### Accredited entity: United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

### Country(s): Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, Republic of the Marshall Islands and Tuvalu

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| Capability upgrade from category 1 (basic) to category 2 (essential) will enable participating countries to better understand, manage and adapt to their increasing climate vulnerabilities. | Under-maintenance of existing climate observation infrastructure is an issue. Maintenance and provision of related funding is critical. |

Innovative: Compliance with emerging Global Basic Observing Network (GBOON) standards as a first in the Pacific.

| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| Innovative: Compliance with emerging Global Basic Observing Network (GBOON) standards as a first in the Pacific. | Staffing approach depends on the countries converting some key consultant positions to permanent staff, which may impact project lifespan. Whether or not these are converted will depend on the countries’ ability to hire. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XXIII titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

As argued in the funding proposal, the livelihoods and economies of the Cook Islands, Niue, the Republic of Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands (RMI) and Tuvalu depend heavily on the ocean and climate-sensitive sectors such as fisheries, tourism and agriculture. Rarotonga, the main island of the Cook Islands, is a low-lying atoll with a population of 150,000 people in these countries, and current forecasts are not sufficiently reliable and downscaled for use in disaster risk management or climate change adaptation planning.

The National Meteorological Services (NMS) provided by the countries’ respective National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS) is critical to the safety of their populations. However, the quality of the data collected and the dissemination of information is not reliably reaching the people who need it. These services are crucial to enhancing resilience and reducing the vulnerability of Pacific nations to climate-related hazards and the effects of climate change.


---
**Page 179**
---

### Project Proposal Aim

This project proposal aims to address several barriers across the project countries: the lack of climate and ocean observation data, the lack of clarity on roles and responsibilities among national agencies, and the lack of communication to people "at the last mile." The project's objective is to strengthen the delivery model for climate information services and Multi-Hazard Early Warning Services (MHEWS) covering oceans and islands. It aims to establish a National Framework for Climate Services in each country to coordinate, facilitate, and strengthen collaboration among national institutions for enhanced climate information services and products.

### Component-by-Component Analysis

#### Component 1: Strengthened Delivery Model for Climate Information Services and Multi-Hazard Early Warning Services (MHEWS) Covering Oceans and Islands
- **Total Cost:** USD 4.7 million, GCF cost: USD 3.5 million
- **Objective:** To assist each country to establish a National Framework for Climate Services.

#### Component 2: National Framework for Climate Services


---
**Page 180**
---

### National Climate Change Forum Process

10. In addition, the project will build on these frameworks each year during the National Climate Change Forum process to ensure that the climate information products are shared with the private sector, non-governmental organizations and community representatives. This National Climate Change Forum process appears valuable, but as an ongoing process, it is not clear how it would be linked to the project activities or what its role will be in terms of post-project impact.

11. Furthermore, this project will also be explored under this component: each country will conduct a climate services market assessment and develop a policy for sustainable financing and delivery of climate services. The usefulness of some of these activities will depend heavily on whether the countries have already established a climate change forum or similar process to engage and ultimately put the new data and services to productive use.

12. Component 3: Improved climate information, early warning systems, and prediction of climate impacts on socio-economic areas (total cost: USD 94 million; GCF cost: USD 57.1 million)

13. Currently, the five project countries' NMHS operate at the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) Essential Climate Variable (ECV) level, which is defined as the minimum number and climate services. An upgrade to "Essential" or "Category 2" means the NMHS possesses the capability to provide ECVs for a wide range of applications including early warning systems, improved forecasting, early warning systems (EWS), and climate-resilient preparedness and response actions. Component 2 makes this upgrade possible; Component 2 also represents the project's largest investment at USD 57 million or approximately 60% of the total project budget.

14. In terms of budget, this component seeks funding for the hardware, infrastructure and installation costs of these capabilities. This means automatic weather stations, radar systems, wave buoys, data management systems, among others. It also proposes funding for the training workshops to ensure that the NMHS staff are able to use the new capabilities effectively.

15. The transformative aspect of these investments is ultimately whether the assets can be adequately maintained for regular operation and whether they will be used in a manner consistent with their design during implementation.

16. Within the country context, this component brings two relatively innovative or transformative aspects to the project. First, the project would be GROW compliant. GROW design specifications state in quantitative terms which parameters to measure, how often, at what horizontal and vertical resolution, etc., and how to interpret the results of these measurements for use. GROW compliance will translate to better weather forecasts, early warnings and climate information products.

17. The second relatively transformative project aspect is end-to-end ocean services. With these capabilities in place, the respective NMHS can engage with their marine sector and other relevant stakeholders to provide a range of climate-related forecast and ocean data, which can then inform decision-making from other stakeholders.

18. Component 3 contains the "last mile" investments to make the upgraded climate data capabilities useful for people on the ground. Similar to component 1, this involves fairly

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

22. The entire population of the programme countries (Cook Islands, Marshall Islands, Nauru, Palau and Tuvalu) are at risk from climate-related hazards such as cyclones, storm surges, coastal flooding and sea-level rise. This intervention will reduce the vulnerability of these communities to climate change by enhancing their resilience against climate-related disasters through improved early warning systems (EWS). The EWS will provide timely information on climate events that could lead to loss of lives and economic assets due to the impact of climate hazards and extreme climate-related disasters. The EWS will also help in reducing the number of people affected, as well as enhancing disaster preparedness by providing a platform for better coordination among stakeholders.

23. The impact of this intervention is significant since this is the first time such innovative interventions are being deployed at this scale.

24. Since disaster response systems have been developed in some of the islands compared to other regions around the world, the proposal will benefit from a detailed analysis of beneficiaries based on demographics and disaggregated at the community level to enable a full understanding of the impact of climate change on these communities and their vulnerability by climate variability and change. This will be important for focusing early warning/action interventions at


---
**Page 182**
---

3.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium-High

The Pacific region lacks adequate meteorological and disaster management capacity to manage the scale of disasters presented by the increasing and intensifying climate extremes.

Most of the countries in the region are small island states (SIS). They have weak early warning/early action systems to drive uptake and investment in CDMs.

The proposal seeks to upgrade the level of the NMHS to category 2 by using innovative technologies that will meet or exceed international meteorological and hydrological standards, establish sub-seasonal and seasonal climate outlooks, climate monitoring, ocean services, and early warning as part of a national disaster risk reduction security sector service. It also seeks to establish a business model for quality management and capacity building in early warning/early action through impact-based forecast and forecast-based actions to drive performance.

The innovative technologies that the programme proposes includes radar for nowcasting of severe weather systems for aviation, marine, agriculture and water production sectors, as well as economic assets at risk disaster warnings. It also proposes to use cloud computing, the internet of things and data analytics technologies to improve the performance of NMHSs. The programme seeks to establish a regional hub for sub-national and national scale pooling together the scarce national resources into a regional pool that will enable a more efficient delivery of climate services. This will include the delivery of climate information and early warning services to the "last mile" at-risk communities.

The programme also proposes to adopt impact based forecasting and forecast-based actions (FBA) as part of the policy reforms in CDMs. The policy reforms that the proposal seeks to effect and its potential to drive uptake and investment in CDMS are outlined below:

Collectively, these instances demonstrate the paradigm shift needed to ramp up climate action in the Pacific.

The programme is aligned with the Paris Agreement, Sustainable Development Goal 13 and the four priorities of the Sendai Framework: (priority 1) understanding disaster risk in all its dimensions of vulnerability, capacity, exposure of persons and assets; hazard characteristics and trends; and (priority 2) strengthening disaster risk governance to manage disaster risk; (priority 3) investing in disaster risk reduction for resilience through public and private sector investment in the protection of people, communities, countries and their assets as well as the environment; and (priority 4) enhancing preparedness for effective response to disasters and "build Back Better" in recovery, rehabilitation and reconstruction.

This is an ambitious undertaking in strengthening local capacities by UNFCCC on its own, given the limited resources available. The proposal will therefore benefit from a long-term strategic partnership with core players such as the World Meteorological Organization, UN Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNISDR), Pacific Community (SPC) and other regional institutions already present in the region. It must detail how it will build local capacity to ensure that the investments made are sustainable over time and that they benefit the Pacific to ensure a critical mass of human capacity to sustain the investments. This can be done during project implementation.


---
**Page 183**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential

Scale: Medium-High

- Tropical cyclones are the major cause of deaths and financial losses in the Pacific. The 2015/2016 El Niño event caused severe damage to critical capital infrastructure - airports, water storage, sanitation, hospitals and clinics, schools, cyclone shelters, fishing boats, inter-island ferries and often food gardens along the coasts of the Pacific.
- Small island developing states (SIDS) have very high concentrations of risk. All five groupings of SIDS are at risk from tropical cyclones. The average annual loss to natural disasters with total replacement costs of over USD 7.6 billion, with buildings at risk contributing most to this figure (USD 4.5 billion or 59 per cent), followed by crops and livestock (40.4 per cent). The current average annual losses per capita in USD at 2018 equivalent rates are: Cook Islands (USD 3,169); Niue (USD 2,112; Nauru, USD 750; Palau (USD 1,364) and Tuvalu (USD 1,364).
- Through a combination of early warning services, disaster risk reduction measures, and building resilient infrastructure, the economic impact of natural disasters can be reduced by up to 80 per cent. The average annual loss per capita in USD at 2018 equivalent rates is USD 579.
- A study on the cost-benefit analysis for investment in early warning services in the Pacific region found that every dollar invested in early warning services would save USD 36 when compared to no investment, with a return of USD 4.5 per dollar invested.

3.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

- Small island developing States (SIDS) are among the most vulnerable countries to climate change. World Bank Index 2018 unequivocally states that disaster risk - the risk that an extreme natural event will lead to a disaster – is at its highest in Oceania.
- The proposal comprehensively articulates these needs and provides interventions that will lay the foundation for addressing them in a way that reflects their expression in the national


---
**Page 184**
---

## 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

The proposal is assessed to have a relatively strong strategic alignment with national and regional climate change and sustainable development policy objectives and disaster management.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

The Pacific Ocean provides some of the global weather phenomena that impact the day-to-day functioning of the world's weather systems. Despite this, the oceans of the Pacific are the least observed of our earth climate systems. The GRON system to be established will contribute to improve the quality and quantity of data available for all national forecasting systems of the programme countries.

The proposal, citing a comparison of Switzerland to the rest of the Pacific Island countries, shows that the cost per sensor is higher than funding models for developing country observations and particularly for SIDS with vast ocean territories. Switzerland (area 41,000 km²) has GDP of USD 528 billion and spends USD 37 million on meteorology (0.09% of its GDP), on its observing network. A similar share of GDP spending for observations for Cook Islands (area 1,836 km²) would amount to USD 524.2 million while the cost is USD 15,700; for Niue (area 269 km²) it would be USD 103.4 million and the cost of observations is USD 63,978; GDP: USD 284 million) would amount to USD 8520; for RMI (area 9,999.530 km²) it would be USD 1,000 billion while the cost of observations is USD 42,59 million. GDP: USD 42.59 million) would amount to USD 1,280. Therefore, even for the Cook Islands with the highest density of sensors (one sensor per km²), the cost of funding for observations would cover less than half the cost of sensors for a single Automatic Weather Station. This is not equivalent to expected climate change impacts and does not cover full costs of observations. It is also unrealistic to expect that the countries will be able to sustain the


---
**Page 185**
---

provision of observations over their territories or territorial waters on national and aid resources.

41. Given the public goods nature of the interventions (both national and global), the financial structure of the proposal is justified for highly vulnerable SIDS [climate risks, financial and economic vulnerability].

42. The proposal also provides a cost-benefit analysis, assuming a 10-year useful life of proposed interventions at a per cent discount rate, showing that all discounted net present value to the public sector exceeds zero. This demonstrates that the proposed investments are economically viable in each making each investment economically viable. This demonstrates significant potential for scaling up and replication of the proposed interventions through climate risk assessment and forecasting forecast-based actions and the other proposed interventions in mitigating against climate change.

43. The proposal will, however, benefit from further exploration of potential sources of national funds (e.g., private sector investments) that could be mobilized to sustain and scale up the investment. This could be done during project implementation.


### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

46. Based on the environmental, social and economic screening submitted by the AIE, the project is assessed to be low risk, or with no minimal adverse environmental and/or for social risks and/or other significant adverse impacts. The project will not be categorized as a high-risk category. The Secretariat confirms the categorization which is within the entity's accreditation level.

47. Most of the programme activities are concerned with climate information, institutional capacity enhancement, monitoring and disaster risk preparation measures and will therefore have no physical or economic displacement of people in communities. The programme is not expected to cause physical and/or economic displacement of people in the communities (either authorities or residents) where such installations are located.

Ecosystem disturbance from the construction activities will be minimized by appropriate site selection where least impacts will be observed. Some of the impacts relate to the potential for community health and safety hazards. Refurbishment of offices and installation of equipment (e.g., data communication systems, computers, servers, printers, photocopiers, fax machines, and batteries) may result in the production of electronic hardware waste which will need to be disposed of appropriately at the end of their useful life.

49. Construction activities will also present construction-related hazards to workers and the ensuing E&S should take into consideration providing appropriate labour and working conditions.

# Gender policy

## Assessment and analysis

The gender assessment and analysis provide a description of the enabling environment for addressing gender equality in disaster risk reduction that is at the focus of the proposal. The three countries of Cook Islands, the Marshall Islands and Tuvalu have ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) but are not party to the convention (through New Zealand) but has not yet ratified CEDAW and lacks robust legal policy or institutional framework for gender equality. The Cook Islands is a signatory to CEDAW, however it is not a signatory to CEDAW. In the case of Palau, the constitution guarantees women equal rights with men in all spheres of life including political participation but there are no laws that provide for this and the country has not ratified CEDAW.

The Cook Islands developed The Cook Islands National Policy on Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment (2013) which provides a framework to address gender equality. However, it does not have sufficient technical capacity to implement it lacking. The Marshall Islands does have a national gender policy and a Gender and Development Office to provide technical capacity and move the policy forward. Tuvalu has no specific gender policy but has included gender considerations in its commitments to gender equality and women’s empowerment. It also has a Gender Affairs Department which is responsible for promoting gender equality, women’s rights and strengthening the enabling environment for women’s empowerment throughout the five islands.

The Gender Assessment, which was conducted based on a desk review, found little information about the current status of gender equality in disaster risk reduction programming throughout the five countries in relation to the proposed programme. Some studies referenced in the assessment indicate that women are more vulnerable than men and that women in the region by Oxfam demonstrates that the bulk of post-disaster resource allocation decisions were made by men, with little or no input from women. The gender assessment found that receiving correct and timely information from men as forecast and disaster warnings were often not relayed to women (particularly those living outside urban areas who are more vulnerable in terms of disaster’s preparation); and women generally could not participate in decision-making councils or at community meetings. A further look at the realities of women in the Pacific region indicates that women face a number of challenges, including limited access to resources, priorities and capacities in preparation for response to disasters. It was observed that they prefer earlier evacuation than men as they are more aware of their vulnerability. Women also face additional difficulties in engaging and participating in decision-making processes. Regionally, women have traditionally been responsible for the care of children and elderly people and the transition to productive work outside the home, both of which are further exacerbated by high levels of male out migration for seasonal work. This leads to gendered time poverty in the Pacific


---
**Page 187**
---

Islands. These challenges therefore emphasize the need for and the importance of gender-responsive evaluation approaches, which would include targeted consultation with women and purposive qualitative data collection.

The assessment also highlighted two areas where women face the greatest challenges: employment in the informal economy and gender-based violence (GBV). Gender inequality in employment distribution and labour markets persists throughout the region and women are more likely to be employed in low-skilled, low-paying jobs. Women are also less likely than men to have access to education and training opportunities in general. The prevalence of women in the informal economy may indicate the shortage of formal sector jobs for women or that women are not able to enter into formal sector employment. Lifetime exposure to gender-based violence can be as high as 60 per cent and even 70 per cent on some islands, according to the World Health Organization (WHO) and the UN. The Asian Pacific Institute of Gender-Based Violence finds that cultural control of pregnancy decisions, domestic violence, shame stemming from experiencing violence, and lack of redress and grievance mechanisms as well as low levels of awareness about GBV are common in many Pacific Island countries. According to the latest GBV statistics, sourced from either national bodies or UN Women, also show that, in the five Pacific countries with available data, 40 per cent of women have experienced physical violence by an intimate partner violence in their lifetimes.

The accredited entity (AE), as part of the requirement of the GEF Gender Policy, has developed a gender action plan to guide its activities, indicate targets, timelines, budgets and gender experts to implement the gender action plan. The gender action plan indicates that at the start of programme implementation, a baseline assessment will be conducted in each country. Workshops will be conducted to ensure relevant information is gathered from stakeholders and communities on the status of women’s empowerment and gender equality. This will be done with the Social Development Programme (Gender, Culture & Youth) of the Pacific Community and will examine i) the existing gender equality policies and strategies; ii) the role of women in economic activities (including supply networks, etc.); iii) the roles of women in various sectors (including fishing, pearl farming, tourism, etc.); and iv) the opportunities, challenges and barriers for women-headed households. The baseline assessment will be used to inform the gender-responsive design of EWSs and organizational and decision-making processes. A mid-term review will also be conducted at the halfway point of implementation in order to gather a range of indicative suggestions and will contemplate and propose concrete and relevant actions, targets and indicators. At the end of programme implementation, an evaluation will be conducted that will be refined on the basis of the study and, as interventions progress, will include lessons learned in mainstreaming gender in project design.

The overall programme plan mirrors the local areas, results and activities of the overall programme and has included measures to strengthen gender responsiveness of institutions and organizations involved in climate change adaptation. The programme plan includes a monitoring assessment that will focus on areas where women’s role is important, including subsistence agriculture and food security leverage points. A group-based approach will be used for training of Trainers in climate risk awareness and adaptation and resilience measures in various sectors. The programme will also include the development of gender-specific information products, and gender mainstreaming in data and information dissemination activities. The programme plan includes the use of a range of tools to provide gender-specific climate information products, and gender mainstreaming in data and information dissemination activities. ### 4.3 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)
59. GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 47.4 million to develop integrated climate and ocean information services and multi-hazard early warning services (MHEWS) in the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, and Tuvalu. The Government of the Cook Islands will contribute USD 10 million; the AIF will provide a total of USD 2.3 million and USD 150,000 in-kind contributions, respectively.
60. The GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 47.4 million to develop a full grant from GCF. As small island developing States, there is limited scope for the cost recovery mechanism due to their public debt and the GCF investment does not generate direct revenue. While the amount of co-financing provided by the AIF will be less than one-third of total financing, the Governments will provide GCF beyond the implementation period.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)
61. The AIF UN Environment Programme has a track record in climate change information management and early warning systems. It has a regional presence and operation in the Pacific region.
62. The AIF will serve as an executing entity (EE) for certain subactivities under Component 3 and Component 4. In addition, a national EE is designated by the Government in each country, namely the Ministry of Environment in the Cook Islands; the Department of Environment and Climate Officer in Niue, the Ministry of Finance in Palau and in Tuvalu, and the Ministry of Environment in RMI. The AIF has reviewed the track record and procurement capacity of the national EEs and the risk assessment for each EE will be built into the agreements. The EE capacity assessments by the AIF are pending.

#### 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)
63. Implementation arrangements in remote coordination: the project activities will mostly be executed remotely, with the exception of the training component which will exceed 50 per cent of the project cost. Currently, the funding proposal package does not lay out clear responsibilities for the respective AIF and national EE to ensure that the project is implemented by the appropriate subactivities in the funded activity agreements. While the AIF will be executing project components with a maximum of three per cent of the project cost, in the event of any light, the AIF will act remediately.
64. The programme coordinates on different sites of international date line/time zones with suitable time differences and it is expected that the remote coordination will be more effective than face-to-face meetings. However, the high cost of travel, logistics and transportation may limit the frequency of such meetings. In addition, there are risks associated with training and trainings cost. Effectively the training is critical for steady implementation and operation.
65. O&M risk: the funding proposal identified O&M related constraints as risks with high probabilities. The countries may face difficulties in maintaining the new and upgraded equipment and systems over time. As the project activities will be executed remotely, it will not be generated from the programme, it is expected to develop information products with potential commercial value. However, the revenue generated by these products is not included in the economic analysis. The costs for O&M and necessary training have been integrated into the programme budget, and revenues are expected to be generated over time once the required system lifetime utilization capacity is built. The countries have committed to ensuring O&M for up to 20 years and a partner agency (SPREP) has also

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

#### 4.3.1 Compliance risk (medium risk)
The AE (UNEP) has informed that the project and its activities will not engage with any entity or individual who may be subject to, or on whom are, United Nations Security Council (UNSC) sanctions. The project will also not engage with any entity in the five beneficiary countries—namely Cook Islands, the Marshall Islands, Niue, Palau or Tuvalu.

The AE has advised that GCF grant funds shall not be used for the purchase of land, cash, vouchers, commodities, or other items of value among beneficiaries, either directly or indirectly. It has also advised that GCF grant funds will be utilized towards strengthening the climate monitoring and observation systems in the five beneficiary countries. The project will provide technical assistance and training. Financial management of the programme will be in line with UNEP’s financial policies.

UNEP has informed that project cooperation agreements with national executing entities (EEIs) will require the EEIs to put in place localized grievance redress mechanisms, including a complaints procedure for beneficiaries and other stakeholders. The programme website will also allow for reporting allegations of complaints of wrongdoing in programmes or projects. UNEP will ensure that EEIs are aware of their legal obligations and/or the UNEP Environmental and Social Sustainability Framework.

UNEP has advised that a risk assessment was conducted to identify potential risks for money laundering, terrorist financing, and prohibited practices among counterparties and beneficiaries of activities within the project. UNEP has estimated a low level of risk.

UNEP has confirmed that the five programme countries—Cook Islands, the Marshall Islands, Niue, Palau and Tuvalu—have all acceded to and/or ratified the United Nations Convention against Corruption (UNCAC). The AE has advised that there is no difference between UNEP and the EEIs will include warranties and covenants by the EEIs, inter alia, ensure compliance with the UNCAC. The AE has also advised that the UNEP Executive Director, Business Secretary, as well as GCF’s Policy on Prohibited Practices.

UNEP has advised that capacity assessments are being undertaken for each of the EEIs prior to project implementation. These will identify financial and fiduciary risks including risks related to prohibited practices, money laundering and misuse of funds. Appropriate risk mitigation strategies will be developed in accordance with UNEP’s policies. Increased reporting requirements will be incorporated in the Project Cooperation Agreements to be signed with EEIs.

#### 4.5.3 Recommendation: Condition
Considering that UNEP has yet to advise the results of their capacity assessments for each of the EEIs on the risk of money laundering, terrorist financing, or prohibited practices, the Office of Risk Management and Compliance (ORMC) team recommends that payments are not disbursed until such time as these assessments have been completed. Appropriate risk mitigation strategies are reviewed by ORM/C/Compliance and determined as acceptable.

### Fiduciary

77. There are six Executing Entities (EE) in this project. They are the Ministry of Finance and Economic Management (MFEM), Development Cooperation Division of the Cook Islands; Project Management Group (PMG) in the Central Agency for Finance and Planning within the Ministry of Finance of Niue; Ministry of Finance and Economic Development within the Ministry of Finance of the Republic of the Marshall Islands; the Ministry of Finance of Tuvalu, and the Ministry of Finance and Economic Development within the Ministry of Finance of Palau.

78. UNEP will undertake the dual role of Accredited Entity (AE) and EE in the project. As AE, UNEP will be responsible for overseeing the implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting, and other related matters associated with the project. As EE, UNEP will ensure the execution of the project and ensure the proper management and application of GCF grant proceeds to achieve the intended results within the agreed time frame in accordance with the terms of the Funded Activity Agreement and the Accreditation Master Agreement.

79. The national EE designated by the Government in each country will be respectively accountable for ensuring that the project is implemented effectively, efficiently, and with efficient use of resources. Each EE is an existing established national agency and shall enter into a Project Cooperation Agreement (PCA) with UNEP for the execution of the Programme. The PCAs will


---
**Page 191**
---

establishes roles and responsibilities of the respective parties for the delivery of the proposed activities, and the schedule and conditions for instalments, the determination of the prevailing fiduciary standards and the terms and conditions for arbitrations and termination of contract. The EEs will be required to comply with UNDP rules, policies and procedures on financial management, personnel administration and reporting, as well as on arbitration and liability terms. The EEs will be required to comply with UNDP rules, policies and procedures on procurement.

Each national EE will provide technical and implementation guidance and will facilitate cooperation between the GCF and the National Programme Management Unit (PMU) and the National Coordination Committee on Programme progress and will submit regular progress reports to the GCF. The PMU will be responsible for managing the EEs through its Sub-regional Office for the Pacific, in the context of project Result 4 focused on regional knowledge management and cooperation. UNDP will execute the joint line in with programme partners, including the Pacific Community (SPC), Regional Technical Assistance and Training Centres, consultants and Technical Partners to undertake relevant activities as appropriate.

A Programme Steering Committee (PSC) will be established to provide high-level oversight of the Programme, achieve its strategic objectives and monitor progress. The PSC will review and approve the annual workplan and budget and approve the Programme's annual financial statements. A Project Management Unit (PMU) and a Programme Secretariat will be established in each of the five countries whose role will be to review and approve the national project proposals, manage the implementation of the projects and provide support to the PMU and PSC. The project will also receive technical expertise and support from Regional Technical Assistance Centres.

UNEP will provide to the GCF the following reports: Annual Performance Reports (APR), Semi-annual Financial Information, a Mid-Term Evaluation Report, a Final APR at project completion and an End-of-Project Review. All financial transactions and payments within the Programme will be guided by UN financial regulations, rules and practices, as well as UNDP's procurement policies.

The capacity assessment for each of the EE's has not been provided and it is recommended that this is placed in a condition in the project.

## Results monitoring and reporting

The theory of change (TOC) clearly defines the ultimate project goal and explicitly states the causal logic that informs the project design. The TOC's stated outcomes are defined in a manner that ensures the achievement of meeting the ultimate project goal. The "If, then, because" logic is clearly shown in the diagram as provided in the funding proposal.

### Logical framework

At the core indicator level, the logical framework has been designed with relevant details, including reporting on the appropriate core indicators for adaptation, as well as on the respective national and regional outcomes. The indicators have been selected to get the GCF results management framework and performance measurement frameworks.

The appropriate indicators have been selected for outcomes at GCF level. However, we note that these indicators are not sufficient in themselves to provide a baseline against which to define the baseline following careful analysis of the country-specific circumstances. It is good practice for formulation teams to use the indicators as a starting point when defining the type of incentives and mechanisms, integration of the mechanisms in the planning process and measurement of effectiveness.

---

### Implementation timetable

The implementation timetable for the funding proposal has been completed appropriately and includes key milestones associated with each phase of the project and these are consistent with the logical framework.

#### Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

The arrangements for monitoring, evaluation, and reporting (section E.7 of the funding proposal) are adequate.

### Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 December 2016, and it came into effect on 20 February 2017.

The Secretariat has received a letter from the Government confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Cook Islands, Niue, the Republic of the Marshall Islands (RMI), and Palau. The Agreement provides for the protection of privileges and immunities with the Cook Islands. With respect to Niue, the Republic of the Marshall Islands, and Palau, the Secretariat has not yet received a letter confirming privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or exposure to claims for compensation where risks need to be further assessed. The Secretariat has engaged with these countries as follows:

With respect to Niue, the Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement for consideration by the Government on 4 April 2016. Negotiations of the agreement have not advanced since June 2018.


---
**Page 193**
---

(b) With respect to the Republic of Palau, the Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of the Republic of Palau on 7 April 2016. The agreement is currently under the Government's review.

(c) With respect to the Republic of the Marshall Islands, the Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of the Republic of the Marshall Islands on 7 April 2016. So far, no response has been received from the Government.

(d) With respect to Tuvalu, the Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of Tuvalu on 7 April 2016. The negotiations with regard to this agreement have not advanced since June 2019.

(e) The Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. In such cases, the IRM and IIU will not be able to ensure that payments by GCF are made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expatriation of assets, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

(f) In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approved by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(g) Signature of the fund activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(h) Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP147

## Proposal name:
Enhancing Climate Information and Knowledge Services for resilience in 5 island countries of the Pacific Ocean

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

## Country/ies:
Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, Republic of the Marshall Islands, Tuvalu

## Project/programme size:
Small

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel1

#### 1.1 Impact potential
Scale: N/A

##### 1.1.1 Adaptation impact

The proposed programme is designed to reduce loss of life and economic assets due to the impact of extreme weather events and other effects of climate change through the development of improved early warning services (EWS) in five small island developing States (SIDS) in the Pacific: Cook Islands, Marshall Islands, Niue, Palau and Tuvalu. Climate resilience would be enhanced through the implementation of a national framework for climate information services, including the assessment and development of the climate services market (e.g., insurance, carbon markets), strengthening institutional frameworks and policies, including the assessment and development of the climate services market (e.g., insurance, carbon markets), strengthening institutional frameworks and policies, including the assessment and development of the climate services market (e.g., insurance, carbon markets), strengthening institutional frameworks and policies for modelling, forecasting and managing climate data and information; (3) improvement of communication strategies to increase awareness among communities on the use of early warnings; and (4) enhancement of regional knowledge management and cooperation for climate services and Mitigation.

The programme is expected are expected to directly benefit the entire population of the participating countries, estimated at approximately 110,000 people, who are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. The programme will also have a positive impact on national budgets through increased access to international climate funds. The combination of an improved climate and ocean information system with sufficient capacity building for early warning services would reduce extreme events and deliver early warnings, better exposure capabilities and strengthened institutional structures to increase the population’s exposure to climate-related risks.

Capacity development is essential in strengthening institutional capacities and national policies related to climate services. This would be achieved through the establishment of national platforms to facilitate coordination and communication among climate sector agencies, a market assessment of climate information services for the five countries, the creation of a Pacific Climate Information Forum to mainstream climate risk knowledge into the government, private and commercial sectors, and the development of enabling policies and mechanisms for sustainable financial management of climate services.

Increasing the geographical scope and scale of climate services is the core objective of the programme. The focus in phase 2 is specifically on expanding and extending the coverage


---
**Page 195**
---

of climate and ocean observation networks in compliance with the World Meteorological Organization Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) standards. This would be achieved through a mix of surface-based automatic weather stations, automated weather observing systems, and shipborne observations. The project will also include the development of an integrated upper air observations. It would also involve training and support to improve capabilities for observation and data management in accordance with GBON standards, including the establishment of quality management systems (ISO9001:2015). Marine observations would be conducted using a combination of shipborne and buoy-based platforms to cover a range of physical and environmental oceanic variables; (b) multi-parameter water quality and pressure sensors for systematic ocean monitoring; (c) installation of a high frequency radar to provide real-time wave height, period, direction, and significant wave height data; (d) deployment of a near-breaker mean current profiler and wave directional system; (e) an automated weather buoy to collect meteorological and environmental data at sea; (f) the establishment of a marine observation network in support of our inter-island shipping vessels; and (g) routine deployment of an unmanned aerial vehicle to quantify coastal erosion. The project will also include capacity building for remote sensing-derived coastal observations such as water quality mapping, marine biodiversity monitoring, and coastal erosion assessment. The technical and operational aspects of the proposed interventions are described with sufficient detail in the feasibility study and the operations and maintenance (O&M) plan.

- Capacity building: This includes training on early warning dissemination and communication systems, the enhancement of preparedness and response capabilities, and the establishment of a national disaster risk management system. Very few projects in a similar area where pre-agreed funds are available to implement pre-disaster action programs before the occurrence of a climate event, based on climate forecasts.

- In accordance with the recommendations of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), the accredited entity (AE) submitted a description of the pre-identified potential sources of funding for PdH:

(a) The forecast-based action by the Disaster Relief Emergency Fund of the International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies is fully operational and can contribute up to USD 10 million per year, with an average contribution of USD 384,000 per country to national Red Cross societies based on a forecasted disaster.

(b) The United Nations Central Emergency Response Fund has set aside USD 140 million to support the implementation of PdH in the Caribbean region for the period 2020-2021, with the expectation that this would assist in the design of a sustainable PdH mechanism. This fund is also available on an annual basis and can be allocated to United Nations agencies, which can create partnerships with local governments and civil society for delivery of funding.

(c) The Caribbean Community (CARICOM) has established a regional mechanism for non-governmental organizations to prepare for forecasted crises and respond early to mitigate their impact. This fund is available on an annual basis.

(d) Component 4 is focused on establishing an interactive information and communication technology platform and a regional data centre for the management and organization of climate data, information, and knowledge sharing in order to disseminate knowledge from the five participating countries in standardized formats aimed at end users.

- In order to mainstream the consideration of climate-related hazards and climate change knowledge into disaster risk reduction (DRR) planning and implementation, this project includes the development of a National Data and Information Strategic Action Plan for each country to improve data management systems. This will include the establishment of a national assessment activity (2.3), the establishment of an annual National Climate Outlook Forum to provide a platform for dialogue between climate service providers, disaster risk management actors

all relevant stakeholders (sub-activity 1.1.4), and the design and implementation of FbP (activity 3.3).

### Sub-activity 3.2.3 involves conducting public awareness and education campaigns to enhance community knowledge and understanding of climate hazards and the potential impacts on lives, livelihoods, and assets. The campaign would be tailored to meet the specific needs of vulnerable groups and would include the delivery of school and community-based activities that would raise awareness about climate change, its associated extreme weather events, climate hazards, disaster risk reduction and the use of technology to access early warning information, community resilience building, health promotion and disease surveillance programmes, and the establishment of two climate information centres.

One of the fundamental objectives of the project is to help communities adapt new climate-resilient livelihood practices. This will be achieved by raising awareness about climate knowledge. The mechanism through which climate information, including forecasts and early warning systems, would be disseminated was not clearly indicated in the funding proposal. However, the original funding proposal reviewed by independent TAP does not clearly indicate how this would be done. Questioned about this issue, the AE explained that the Climate Information Services Sector Action and Communications Plans for key climate-sensitive sectors, such as agriculture, forestry, fisheries, water resources, and disaster risk reduction, would outline sector-specific actions for disaster risk reduction, management and climate-resilient livelihood practices. These plans will be developed in consultation with representatives of sectors and the livelihoods of those that work within them. For example, if a wetter-than-usual season is forecasted by the National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS), it would ensure that particular high value crops are likely to flourish, or that cleared land should not be left uncovered in order to prevent soil erosion.

The AE also explained that the NMHS would work with communities to remove vetching sites in advance and issue warnings about egucators; conservation departments might take advantage of a quiet cyclone season to support community-based forest management activities. The AE further clarified that the AE would ensure that information from National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS) is translated into concrete climate-resilient livelihood practices, such as those described in the Action and Communications Plans for key climate-sensitive sectors.

The AE also clarified that under activity 3.2, the programme would deliver concrete climate-resilient livelihood practices through several means of verification including surveys and benefit assessments in the five countries, community consultation reports and national programme implementation progress reports.


## Paradigm shift potential

### Innovation

The combination of impact-based forecasting and FbP would represent an innovative mechanism that could improve disaster preparedness and response, and enable more efficient management of government budgets.

A technical aspect of the project is the installation of a polarization X-band Doppler radar system in the five countries, which would increase weather forecasting capacity and real-time monitoring of weather conditions.

13. However, most of the project focuses on expanding and renewing the existing observing networks and improving existing systems.

### 2.2 Potential for knowledge and learning

The platform and the regional data centre to be developed would serve as fundamental tools for knowledge sharing across the participating countries. This would involve the addition of a peer-to-peer exchange component, the development of a regional climate change portal, and engagement of national climate data contacts, support to prepare national reporting for climate change agreements, the provision of data products in regional forums to enhance the use of data by stakeholders, the establishment of electronic access to meteorological data sources and data backup in the regional portal, and support for all information and communication technology (ICT) needs.

The programme would also include training, mentoring and advisory services for staff in SMEs on topics related to climate information services, observations, monitoring, modelling and prediction. The project will also provide technical assistance to countries in the development of a community resilience against climate risks, and national stakeholder workshops and a regional workshop to facilitate knowledge transfer on improved coral bleaching model forecasting and protocols.

Provisions for monitoring, reporting and evaluation are adequately described in section E of this document.

### 2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

Each NMHS is committed to conduct sound O&M of the equipment procured under the project as well as sustain the functions of the local consultations as they relate to positions established by the project. The commitment letter also states that the NMHS will continue building community resilience against climate risks, and national stakeholder workshops and a regional workshop to facilitate knowledge transfer on improved coral bleaching model forecasting and protocols.

The government would seek co-funding aid from the Specialized Operations Financing Facility (SOFF), which is being developed by the World Meteorological Organization as a new facility for providing financial support to developing countries through grant-based financing of basic observations and the value of these observations as a global public good. It would provide funding for the procurement of equipment, including radars, primarily Africa, Asia and least developed countries, to achieve and maintain GBNR compliance and data-sharing at the global level.

The NMHS TAF found that post-project O&M costs could be unaffordable for the participating countries. For example, the total annual budget allocation for "meteorological services" in 2019/2020 is USD 76,000; this is planned to increase to USD 78,000 for 2020/2021 and USD 78,000 for 2021/2022 and USD 130,000 for 2022/2023; while the O&M of the equipment procured by the programme is estimated to be USD 65,000 per year. The NMHS TAF also found that consumables are unaffordable for upper air observations. The O&M plan states that upper air observations will be conducted using a mobile radar, which would require ongoing funding to fund the supply of consumables. Another example is in the case of Nauru, which, with 120 km² and a population of approximately 13,500 people, would need to buy five countries would install an X-band Doppler radar, which would each cost USD 45,000 per year.

### Outputs related to post-project sustainability

* Outputs pertaining to post-project sustainability include the assessment of the climate information services to be used as a basis to promote its expansion, the development of value-added climate products and services, the engagement of the private sector, the development of new business models, the promotion of public-private partnerships in general, and most importantly, the inclusion of training and capacity-building as cross-cutting activities in all programme components.

#### 1.2.3 Governance of the regulatory framework and policies

* Through sub-activity 1.2.1, the programme would support the development of national policies aimed at enabling the sustainable financial management of climate services, thus helping ensure that each NMHS has the means to cover O&M costs after programme lifespan.
* The second component under sub-activity 1.2.2 would include the identification of policy incentives to foster private sector investments in climate information and early warning services.

#### 1.2.5 Scalability and replicability

* Scalability is limited, given that the project would already cover the entire territory of the participating countries. On the other hand, there is a high potential for replicability in other SIDS, backed by a robust knowledge management component.
