#### 1.4.1 Vulnerability of the country, vulnerable groups and gender aspects

31. Due to their geographical context and the dependence of their economies and livelihoods on climate-sensitive sectors such as subsistence farming, fisheries and tourism, these SIDS are particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change including flooding and drought.

32. Climate data presented in the feasibility study shows that temperatures have risen over the last 65 years, with a significant increase in the number of hot days (>30°C) and hot nights (>24°C). All trends in annual extreme temperature indices indicate a warming climate. Ocean temperatures are also increasing, with sea surface temperatures rising by up to 1.7°C since 1980, with the area of water with temperatures exceeding 29.5°C having increased by 600–800 per cent.

33. Regarding precipitation, the proposal states that the only statistically significant trends were observed at Majuro, Marshall Islands and involve a decrease in the number of consecutive dry days (1974–2008) and an increase in the number of wet days (1956–2008). The latter was also associated with an increase in total wet day rainfall when daily rainfall > 95th percentile), and a decrease in total wet day rainfall. However, in the north later (Tropical Pacific region), there were no significant trends observed.

34. A seasonal negative trend in September–November total precipitation was associated with increased evaporation rates. The proposal also describes statistically significant trends to drought-specific indices were reported for the regions covering Cook Islands, Niue and Tuvalu.10


---
**Page 200**
---

18. Questioned by independent TAP about drought predictions, the AI responded that seasonal variations were also found in the Southern Cook Islands and Tuvalu, and that more intense droughts... have implications for sectors such as agriculture and water, even if annual rainfall is not affected.

19. The AI also noted that there are significant correlations between ENSO and total precipitation and between ENSO and the threshold TETCCDI indices (Griffiths et al., 2013). These correlations are also found in other studies (e.g., Cat et al., 2014; Doherty, 2015). As such we infer that the increase in frequency of extreme El Niño events (Cat et al. 2014: DOI: 10.1038/nature13769) will also lead to an increase in extreme tropical cyclone-induced rainfall. This is corroborated by a recent journal paper by Dr Savin Chaudhuri (Federation University Australia) [currently under review], which reports that tropical cyclones contribute significantly more precipitation during El Niño years than La Niña conditions and that there are indications of increasing intensities of tropical cyclone-induced extreme rainfall events.

20. A study cited by the funding proposal found that the average rate of relative sea-level rise in the region was 2.8 millimeters/year between 1950 and 2003. As per the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) AR4, this rate is expected to continue to rise in the 21st century and will likely be in the range of 0.26–0.82 meters by 2100, depending on emissions scenarios.

21. The proposal states that climate studies show that predicted higher ocean temperatures would lead to more intense tropical cyclones, with increased wind speed and rainfall, as a result of changes in atmospheric circulation patterns (e.g., the Walker Circulation). However, at the same time recognizing that there are no significant trends in the overall number of tropical cyclones observed over the past 50 years, the AI responded that “there is no ‘trend’ about this inconsistency,” and the AI responded that “a shift in tropical cyclone intensity with increased wind speed and rainfall does not necessarily mean an increase in frequency.” Pacific (CSIRO, Australian RM and SPPR, 2015). At the regional level, the PACASAP findings on climate change impacts for the Pacific region indicate that there is a high likelihood of fewer but more intense tropical cyclones in the Pacific basin by the end of the 21st century: tropical cyclones will become less frequent in the Pacific region, but more intense.

22. The IPCC AR5 report also projects that the Pacific islands will experience “extreme precipitation events with an increasing frequency and intensity” (p. 30). A substantial rate of –5%–20% is projected by the late 21st century (Chand et al., 2020). A Review of South Pacific Tropical Cyclones: Impacts, Trends, Climate Variability and Climate Change?

### Absence of alternative sources of financing

As explained in the proposal, financing for specific climate change-related actions is highly constrained by budget limitations. Niue and Palau have no national budget allocations for climate change adaptation or mitigation activities. The Cook Islands has allocated a small budget for the financial period 2016/2017-2019/2020 for climate change-related actions, of which less than half is available to be spent on climate change adaptation and mitigation projects with development partners and loans. The Marshall Islands and Tuvalu are developing contingency funds, but these dependents on foreign aid.

### Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

The need for institutional strengthening is recognized and addressed throughout the programme via support for the revision and development of policies related to the climate services market and financing mechanisms, fostering of coordination and collaboration between agencies, and building of capacity in key areas. The Niue Ministry of Health (MoH) and the National Meteorological Service (NMS) are currently able to provide basic weather and climate services to their countries, but they lack capacity for advanced weather forecasting or oceanographic systems, based on forecast models, seasonal forecasts, and informed preparedness and response actions. The feasibility study includes a comprehensive description of the status and needs of the existing climate information services and early warning systems.

### Financial viability

The cost-benefit analysis was conducted separately for each country. Components 4 costs and project implementation costs, being common to the five countries, were divided into equal parts for each country. The analysis shows that, assuming a 10-year useful life of proposed interventions, the net present values (NPV) are positive and the economic internal rates of return exceed the discount rates (see Table 2).

Questioned by independent TAP, the AE confirmed that the assumed avoided losses used for benefit estimation are based on the damage reduction potential for households, livestock and infrastructure, and country's net replacement cost and early retirement age figures.

Economic damages and losses are estimated to be reduced by 1 per cent for Niue, Papua and Tuvalu, as a few investments in climate change and early warning services have taken place in these countries and therefore the full savings are anticipated. For Cook Islands and PNG, which have not yet implemented any such measures, an assumed average reduction of 20 per cent were assumed.

The sensitivity analysis considered a case with a decrease in benefits of 15 per cent, another one with a decrease in costs of 15 per cent, and another with both a 10 per cent decrease in benefits and a 10 per cent increase in costs, resulting in all three cases in a decrease of economic internal rates of return.

The cost-benefit analysis submitted failed to include O&M costs of equipment procured by the programme for years 6 to 10, assuming zero NPV costs for this period. Questioned by independent TAP, the AE confirmed that these costs are included in the sensitivity analysis including O&M for 10 years. The results from this revised version are satisfactory.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP147)

## Proposal name:
Enhancing Climate Information and Knowledge Services for resilience in 5 island countries of the Pacific Ocean

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

## Country(ies):
Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, Republic of the Marshall Islands, Tuvalu

## Project/programme size:
Small

### Impact potential
UNEP welcomes the ITAP’s positive assessment of the impact potential of the Programme, which accurately reflects how its implementation will achieve the core objective of increasing the generation and use of timely, accurate and actionable climate and ocean information for enhancing resilience in small island developing states (SIDS) to climate change impacts.

### Paradigm shift potential
UNEP would like to highlight that the Programme will not only strengthen the existing observing networks but will also significantly expand them to new locations to ensure that every inhabited island has an observation point – thereby significantly improving the spatial resolution of the global ocean observing system. This will allow for better use of the Global Ocean Observing Network (GOON) to feed into Numerical Weather Prediction (NWP) as a global public good.

Furthermore, UNEP agrees that the Programme has a high replication potential in other SIDS but also highlights the potential for in-country scalability of community-based activities.

### Sustainable development potential
UNEP considers the long-term environmental co-benefits of the Programme to be substantial. Actionable climate information can significantly improve natural resources management – from fisheries and tourism, through to coastal protection and disaster risk reduction. Ocean information services support the responsible management of marine ecosystems and inform sustainable fisheries and tourism practices that minimise environmental impacts.

Impact assessments show that detailed climate information is critical for identifying and managing environmental risks for safeguarding nature and biodiversity. Furthermore, detailed climate information can help identify areas at high risk from sea-level rise and can inform energy management decisions to improve efficiency and reduce GHG emissions.


---
**Page 205**
---

# Needs of the recipient

UNEP notes the ITAP’s assessment of this criterion and affirms the high vulnerability of the five Pacific Island countries to extremes and related hazards. UNEP further notes and agrees that the Programme addresses the urgent need for institutional strengthening and capacity building at all levels, including national decision-making, impact-based forecasting, early warning systems and evidence-based preparedness and response actions.

## Country ownership

UNEP agrees that the Programme is well aligned with international, regional and national priorities in terms of climate information, early warning and DRR (e.g. priorities identified in the region’s first Framework for Resilient Development in the Pacific 2017-2030) and appreciates ITAP’s recommendation to include this as a criterion.

Extensive consultations were undertaken with stakeholders at different levels. National counterparts engaged additional stakeholders such as women’s organisations – e.g. People’s Empowerment Network (PEN) and Women’s Environment and Development Organization (WEDO). A depth gender study will be conducted in partnership with SPC to further facilitate engagement of women’s groups and communities.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

UNEP appreciates and agrees with ITAP's recognition that the Programme will support the unlocking of barriers to private sector investment in climate services. Employing a value chain approach, UNEP anticipates the Programme to encourage private sector engagement and catalyse new partnerships for climate resilience.

At the local level, UNEP agrees that engagement with traditional knowledge (TK) holders is highly effective in producing reliable and accurate seasonal forecasts. Accordingly, UNEP also endorses the value of integrating TK into climate-related decision-making processes and institutional response capacity, as highlighted in the IPCC Global Warming of 1.5°C report.

# Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

UNEP appreciates the ITAP’s overall positive review of the proposal and notes with thanks the recommendation that the Board approves the Programme.

Further to this, UNEP also recognises the considerable social and economic value of increasing the generation and use of climate information to support climate resilience and sustainable development in five highly vulnerable Pacific SIDS. We would like to specifically highlight the considerable environmental and cultural values associated with these countries’ strong connections to their natural environments on which populations reciprocally depend. The strong emphasis on integrated approaches to climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (DRR) acknowledges the importance of integrating marine health and the need for sustainable development in all sectors and contexts – as recognised by the UN Decade of Ocean Science for Sustainable Development.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## 1. INTRODUCTION: Gender mainstreaming for the GCF Pacific UNEP Programme

This Gender Assessment is a formative research input for a Green Climate Fund (GCF) funding proposal being developed by UN Environment Programme (UNEP), titled "Enhancing Climate Information Services in Small Island Developing States (SIDS)".

### The Problem Statement
The Pacific region, including countries such as Small Island Developing SIDS (SIDS), these countries have been identified as 'climate hotspots' (or sub-regions of climate hotspots) due to their vulnerability to the impacts of climate change, such as sea-level rise and saline intrusion, temperature variability among others) compounded by the following factors: remoteness and deprivation from the benefits of scale, small domestic markets and heavy dependence on natural resources for livelihoods, economic growth, fragile natural environments, and socioeconomic as well as gendered vulnerabilities.

### Objectives
With four Results focused on strengthened climate information, climate modelling and predictions, and improved access to early warning services, this proposed Programme can strategically contribute towards greater gender equality in the five countries by:

- Adopting gender-responsive approaches for the design of climate information services;
- Promoting the development and implementation of gender-sensitive capacity-building activities of hydro-meteorological networks, equipment, and systems;
- Establishing gender-aware policy frameworks to inform collaboration between climate service and national/regional hydro-meteorological stakeholders; and
- Promoting the integration of gender perspectives in climate change adaptation, climate information, geospatial, hydro-meteorological and early warning services in the Pacific Ocean region.

### The Context
A kaleidoscope of overlapping cultural, economic, social and political roles form gender relations in the Pacific region. These are shaped by the diversity within each country of the region and the unique markers of the different island societies, these roles have multiple facets, thereby reflecting the:

- Geographical and geopolitical specifics of the region;
- Traditional norms within intragenerational societies of the five countries; and
- Gender gaps in economic roles and political representation, which also show variation between countries.

The Programme recognizes the lack of a gender-responsive approach to address the above baseline (see Section 3 for details), particularly stemming from:

- Stereotypical views of climate services being gender-neutral;
- Lack of consideration of gender needs or ethnic vulnerabilities in adaptation opportunities, resilience capacities and mitigation services; and
- Gendered access to resources and decision-making power.

• **Faculty of financial means**, gender budgeting and dedicated resources towards mainstreaming gender action.

These factors can limit the potential, inclusiveness and success of adaptive actions in the Pacific countries. Benefits of increased support and access to climate services, as well as awareness raising and capacity building for adaptation, are not fully realised because of these constraints. It is important to capitalise on new opportunities and respond better to changes ushered in by the Programme.

A gender lens, thus, is both necessary and relevant for the project to maximise its outcomes. This is particularly true when considering climate change impacts, which are complex and dynamic, for predicting and tackling climate change impacts, natural hazards and weather variation that cannot be avoided. It is also important to consider how these changes will impact women and men in their socioeconomic frameworks to mobilise medium- and long-term climate change adaptation action as well as coping capacity is also necessary.

To elucidate, employing a mainstreaming perspective will highlight and amend persistent inequalities and unequal access, which often result in specific and entrenched vulnerabilities in the region. This approach can help communities to identify how they are vulnerable as a social group to cope with external shocks, to respond effectively to them, and to adapt to a situation of climate change. These vulnerabilities (such as economic status and political access; performance of gender roles; age, ethnicity, religion, etc.) are often experienced by different social groups (men, women, children, youth, older people) in different ways. They also vary across networks, skills, among others) need addressed through climate services, as they act as impediments to adaptation and resilience. The same applies for the other types of vulnerabilities, when compounded or individually, can reverse the progress achieved in securing household economic stability (with spill-over effects on food, energy and water security) and managing environmental risks and hazards, which are key elements towards achieving sustainable developmental goals and social change.

On the other hand, gender mainstreaming through the Gender and Development (GAD) approach? (as adopted by the Pacific Climate Change Program) will also recognize the role marginalised and vulnerable groups can play, and not simply stylise them as priori victims. With an inclusive project design, they are often able to contribute traditional and ad-hoc knowledge of coping and improving strategies, which can either be strengthened and mainstreamed as well as used as a basis for further capacity development.

existing burdens on women among such groups. See Nelson V., Meadows, K., Common T., Morton J., & Martin A. (2013). Uncertain predictions: inviable impacts and the need to mainstream gender in climate change adaptation? In Gender and Development Journal Article 22(4), pp. 758-769. The authors argue that women are often excluded from decision-making processes, which can lead to policies and programmes that do not address their needs or interests. This exclusion can also result in the perpetuation of gender inequalities and the reinforcement of existing power imbalances between men and women. Gender mainstreaming is a strategy for ensuring that gender equality is considered throughout all stages of policy development and implementation. It involves integrating gender perspectives into decision-making processes, policies, and programmes to ensure that they are inclusive and equitable for both men and women. This approach aims to address the root causes of gender inequality by challenging existing power structures and promoting greater participation and representation of women in public life.

The authors also argue that gender mainstreaming is essential for achieving sustainable development goals because it helps to create a more equal society where both men and women have access to opportunities, resources, and decision-making power. By addressing the root causes of gender inequality, gender mainstreaming can help to reduce poverty, improve health outcomes, and promote social cohesion. It also has the potential to increase economic growth by tapping into the full potential of all individuals in a society.

2. Methodology: Identification of Gender Priorities for GCF Pacific

2.1 Appraisal of Gender Mainstreaming Priorities of GCF and UN Environment

The analysis prerequisites of this Gender Assessment and Action Plan are informed by both GCF's and UNEP's work.

The GCF adopted a revised version of its 2014 Gender Policy and Action Plan on June 2018 in Korea. The revised policy addresses pertinent issues on gender and climate change: the expansion of gender equality, women's empowerment, and the inclusion of women in decision-making processes; synergies with the in-house Indigenous People (IP) policy as well as the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). The GCF's Gender Policy and Action Plan is designed to address the unique needs of a heterogeneous, culturally diverse context of gender equality to better address and account for the links between gender equality and climate change – a perspective that this five-country Programme mainstreaming assessment seeks to explore.

UNEP recognises the role of gender equity as a 'driver of sustainable environmental development'; not only to assuage the stresses on natural resources, impending climate risks and adopting measures to mitigate these risks but also to ensure that women and other vulnerable and social groups in sustainable development and climate resilient policy- and decision-making. The GCF has been actively engaged with UNEP in gender mainstreaming efforts through its Gender Equality and the Environment" policy, Global Gender and Environment Outlook research study, and inclusion of gender-responsive elements in previous and upcoming projects.

2.2 Literature Review, Secondary Data and Gender Baseline

The Gender Assessment and Action Plan is informed by the following methodological steps:

- The content of this annex draws from a comprehensive desk review and ex-post gender analysis of Feed-in-Tariff (FIT) policy and climate change Actions suggested through the action plan, and beneficiary identification (and enumeration), therefore are indicative only. All other programme implementation, an in-depth study of gender stakeholders will be conducted.

- The desk-review of existing literature was based on early warning systems (EWS), disaster risk reduction and management (DRRSM), and hydro-meteorological relations in relation to gender and the context of the Pacific 2015. Literature was drawn from research works from international organisations, such as the United Nations Development Programme – UNDP South Pacific, United Nations Economic and Social Commission for Asia and the Pacific (UNESCAP), World Bank, Asian development banks (the World Bank and the Asian Development Bank – ADB), journals (Gender and Development, Journal of Coastal Research, etc.), and grey literature (government reports and policies).


---
**Page 210**
---

* The literature review revealed useful data and research, which were informed by varied methodologies and perspectives. Thus, a derivative baseline (Section 3) from these readings was inferred to identify the gender barriers and dynamics in the five nations, and the policy gaps that exist.
* The next section (Section 4) presents gender-responsive elements to be incorporated within Programme outcomes to reduce climate vulnerability, particularly of at-risk households and communities. This section also includes a list of potential stakeholders; and presents normative information to gear the overall proposal towards better socioeconomic, gender and environmental co-benefits.
* Subsequently, Section 5 provides an overview of gender equality development in the Programme countries, and presents a potential list of gender collaborators, relevant for activities and stakeholder consultations during Programme implementation.

3. Socioeconomic and Gender Baseline in the Pacific Countries

### 3.1 Composite Indices

Implementing gender-responsive adaptation action requires situating the project's results framework on a thorough and context-driven baseline. A primary step is understanding the baseline is collation of socioeconomic data, including poverty rates, income distribution, education levels, health indicators being employed, as indicative (and not conclusive) measures of current levels of development, gender equality, and poverty and labour force participation.

As Box 3.1 shows, composite indices are widely used in development practice both challenges and advantages. For example, numerous fallacies have been identified in the methodologies employed in composite indices. These indices are many-quantitative, and present empirical and aggregate measures of complex social phenomena. They are often criticized for their lack of transparency and interpretative nuances. Yet, these also remain invaluable as useful supplements to income-based development indicators and can be used to provide a more holistic view of development progress through complex measurement constructs as well as providing access to non-technical audiences.

This baseline, firstly, has compiled scores of three different UNDP composite indices: Human Development Index (HDI), Gender Inequality Index (GII) and Oxford Poverty and Human Development Initiative (OPHI)'s Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI), where data for the project countries are available. At the outset, this revealed a data gap, as figures for Cook Islands, Nue and Tuvalu are unavailable for the HDI. Further, there are no figures for the GDI and MPI, as the tables below reveal. Secondly, the existing indices have different methodologies and definitions of poverty and inequality. The World Economic Forum (WEF)'s Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) and Oxford Poverty and Human Development Initiative (OPHI)'s Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI), where data for the project countries are available.

## II. GII
GENDER INEQUALITY INDEX (UNDP (2018) - out of 199 countries)

This index, showing inequality in achievement between men and women in education, reproductive health, empowerment and labour market participation, is measured in terms of health equity, economic capital and political empowerment.

It provides a primary understanding of the differences in gender-related development indicators between men and women. The GII does not use the sex ratio quintile hypothesis, which could then be an advantage or disadvantage depending on how it is used. It should be treated as indicators, not Permitting any conclusions about the relative performance of women vis-à-vis men, using with plausible economic social indicators.

### NOTE:
GII rankings for all the project countries are not available in the latest edition, revealing the existing data gap for the region (East Asia and Pacific (0.312 - high development) and SIDS (0.447 - low development)).

## III. GDI
GENDER DEVELOPMENT INDEX (UNDP 2018) - grouped in 5 sub-indexes

The GDI (UNDP index shows the ratio of female to male HDI values. GDI expresses values in terms of a scale from zero to one, with an understanding: GDI grouped categories have lower scores than their corresponding HDI values. This further reinforces the notion that the GDI is not a simple ratio of HDI values.

Geske Dijkstra and Hammer find that although gender-related development indicators have improved over time in many countries, the most useful data and indicators are required to monitor progress towards achieving contemporary trends in gendered provision, particularly with the conceptualization "women" as

### IV. GGGI
GENDER GAPS GAP INDEX
WEF (2018) – out of 144 countries

The GGGI (WEF) benchmarks 144 countries on their progress in closing gender gaps across four thematic dimensions - economic participation and opportunity, health, education, and survival, and political empowerment. The Index provides a composite score for each dimension, with scores ranging from 0 to 100.

The GGGI also provides country rankings that allow for effective comparisons across regions and income groups.

### V. MPI
MULTIDIMENSIONAL POVERTY INDEX (2019) – out of 101 countries

Calculated by the Oxford Poverty and Human Development Initiative (OPHI), the global Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI), measures acute poverty in developing countries (covering 76% of the world’s population). The MPI identifies those living on less than $2.15 a day, who suffer from at least three deprivations with regard to different indicators of health, education and standard of living.

The index not only identifies those living in multidimensional poverty but also measures the intensity of their poverty. The MPI can help the affected individuals and governments identify and provide the targeting of those with the greatest need for interventions that reduce vulnerability and increase impacts of policy interventions.

### 3.2 National Aggregate Statistics and Data

Adding nuance to the baseline established by the composite indices is the next step of understanding the gender inequality context in which the project is expected to function. The national-level data points (divided into three categories - poverty and hardship, labour market, and social vulnerability) are not available for all 101 countries across, given different methodologies of each statistical bureau or office. These broadly speak to issues identified by the GGGI and MPI such as: gender-disaggregated poverty statistics; gender-disaggregated informal/formal labour markets; gender-disaggregated education and literacy indicators; gender-disaggregated health, mortality and morbidity statistics, etc.) to map and mainstream existing vulnerabilities.

## NOTE

NATIONAL/INTERNATIONAL POVERTY LINES: HARDSHIP/VULNERABILITY. It is important to differentiate from the national poverty lines in the case of the five Pacific project countries, national poverty lines are defined according to each country's own criteria and are not internationally comparable. International poverty lines refer to the lowest income quintile in richer and higher-income countries. International poverty lines attempt to hold the real value of the poverty lines consistent across countries.

According to a World Bank report<sup>3</sup>, hardship and vulnerability are related, but distinct: hardship is about access to resources, while vulnerability is about the ability to cope with shocks or stressors. Hardship is said to experience hardship based on many different measures such as lacking access to services or living in low-quality housing. Vulnerability is defined by the inability of a household to meet its basic needs and/or to meet the basic needs of all members as measured by consumption. On the other hand, vulnerability is based on expectations about future shocks or stressors that could affect households' ability to meet their basic needs.

COOK ISLANDS: In 2008, 35% of female-headed households (FHH) had just one person, which increased to 26% in 2011, as evidenced from trends in Rarotonga. It is important to note because one person households are considered more vulnerable than two or three-person households.

In Rarotonga, women headed 37% of FHHs and men headed 45%. In the main islands, women headed 27% of FHHs, but such households accounted for 32% of the households in the main islands. In the outer islands, women headed 60% of FHHs and such households accounted for 81% of all FHHs in Rarotonga, and only 4% of FHH were headed by a woman in the Southern Group. A similar analysis was conducted on men-headed households (MHH) in Rarotonga. In the main islands, MHHs accounted for 32% of all households but women headed 75% of such households.

NIUE: In 2014, 68% of female-headed households had just one person and 9% were headed by a woman in the Southern Group. A similar analysis was conducted on men-headed households (MHH) in Rarotonga. In the main islands, MHHs accounted for 32% of all households but women headed 75% of such households.

PALAU: In 2014, 68% of female-headed households had just one person and 9% were headed by a woman in the Southern Group. A similar analysis was conducted on men-headed households (MHH) in Rarotonga. In the main islands, MHHs accounted for 32% of all households but women headed 75% of such households.

RMI: In 2014, 68% of female-headed households had just one person and 9% were headed by a woman in the Southern Group. A similar analysis was conducted on men-headed households (MHH) in Rarotonga. In the main islands, MHHs accounted for 32% of all households but women headed 75% of such households.

TUVALU: In 2014, 68% of female-headed households had just one person and 9% were headed by a woman in the Southern Group. A similar analysis was conducted on men-headed households (MHH) in Rarotonga. In the main islands, MHHs accounted for 32% of all households but women headed 75% of such households.

LABOUR FORCE PARTICIPATION: The labour force participation rate is a measure of the proportion of a country's working-age population that is employed or actively seeking employment. It provides an indication of the size of the supply of labour available to engage in the production of goods and services, relative to the potential demand for those goods and services.

The data presented here are based on the International Labour Organization (ILO) definition of the labour force. The age group given is a general age range that covers all ages from 15 years old and above. This can be an indication of the size of the supply of labour available to engage in the production of goods and services, relative to the potential demand for those goods and services.

**MATERNAL MORTALITY RATIO & UNDER-FIVE MORTALITY RATE**

The World Health Organization (WHO) identifies maternal mortality ratio or complications during pregnancy and childbirth as a leading cause of death and disability among women of reproductive age in low-income countries. The under-five mortality rate is the number of deaths per 1,000 live births for children aged less than five years old. It was an MDG as well as an SDG indicator. Similarly, the under-five mortality rates take stock of child health and development. In 2017, the maternal mortality ratio in low-income countries was 489 deaths per 100,000 live births, while the under-five mortality rate for children aged less than five years old in these countries remained large, according to the WHO [1]. In 2017, the under-five mortality rate in low-income countries was 69 deaths per 1,000 live births. The SDG targets are ambitious and will require a concerted effort by all stakeholders. Countries’ roles by ending preventable child deaths are important priorities, particularly under the post-2015 SDG agenda.

**LIFETIME EXPOSURE TO GBV**

According to the World Bank, around 1 in every 3 women in the world face violence during their lifetime. In low-income countries, the statistics are telling – lifetime exposure from 40 to 70% in certain areas, making GBV incidence rates the highest globally. The consequences of GBV are devastating and include physical injury, psychological trauma, HIV/AIDS transmission, preserving family honour, shame stemming from experiencing violence, and lack of redress and grievance mechanisms. In addition, GBV is a major contributor to maternal mortality or GBV-related deaths. Globally, an average of 1 out of 5 women experience intimate partner violence in their lifetimes in the selected Pacific countries for the project.

# Gender Mainstreaming, Sectoral Issues & Climate Information Services

Using the above gender baseline as the point of departure, sectoral issues in relation to gender mainstreaming for climate information services have been identified for the Programme design and planning.

## 1. Gender Mainstreamed within Climate Information Services in Pacific Islands Countries

The Pacific Island countries identified by the Programme are uniquely vulnerable to aggregate shocks from both economic and environmental stressors due to their countries' predilection of small size, isolation, and other geographic, economic and socio-political features. Despite several Household Economic Studies (HES) that have been conducted in these countries, the full range of macroeconomic impacts of negative shocks having been well studied, the impacts on household well-being and resilience are not fully understood.

Given this lacuna in data and research, impacts of climate-related risks, hazards and natural disasters in the backdrop of limited climate information services, cannot be empirically quantified or can be poorly estimated. The gendered nature of these vulnerabilities is further compounded by the exposure compound with current prevalence of hardship, gender inequality and lack of access to social safety networks to disproportionately affect the poorer and more vulnerable demographics – determined by age, sex, disability, ethnicity, etc.

A study conducted by Practical Action1 in Nepal and Peru, for example, finds that gender inequality and social marginalization increases vulnerability to disasters. This is primarily because pre-determined roles within households determine who has access to resources and information on DRM decision-making. Women also tend to care-givers, in the Pacific societies, and hence have different needs from men when it comes to disaster preparedness and response. Women are more likely to be displaced by natural disasters than men and are less likely to evacuate early, for example, when disaster and early warnings are announced.

At the start of Programme implementation, an in-depth engagement with these stakeholders will be conducted, which will include exercises to understand the youth and women’s roles within their communities and households, to deliver gender-responsive climate information products.

Gender-responsive climate information services will play a unique role in positive rise in income adaptation strategies, increasing awareness on disaster risks and hazards, bolstering national meteorological and hydrological services (NMHS) and climate data collection, analysis and interpretation of climate information for the Pacific region.

At the outset, mainstreaming gender in climate information services through the Programme will involve the consideration of the following factors2 in activity design: division of labour (activity/roles); visibility of women in decision-making processes; access to resources (e.g. land, water, finance, nutrition pattern, literacy levels, health, recourse to legal protection [interdependence/risk]; survival skills, etc.) and influence over decision-making processes.

The Programme will further analyse and address the impacts of gender inequalities in the implementation of relevant sub-activities (sub-activity 3.1.2: Climate Information Services; sub-activity 3.1.3: Early Warning Systems [EWS] organisational and decision-making processes (sub-activity 3.1.1); and atmospheric monitoring and forecasting services (sub-activity 3.1.4) in the Pacific region.

The Programme will also address gender inequalities in the development of communication channels for women, youth and the elderly in the development of localised communication systems (sub-activity 3.1.2).

A study by Oxfam3 in the Pacific region demonstrates the importance of gender-responsive adaptation approaches: through women’s focus group discussions and purposive qualitative data


1 World Bank (2015): GdI

2 Brown, et al., 2014; Gender Transformative EWS: Experiences from Nepal and Peru. Report. Practical Action UK.

3 Oxfam (2016): Women’s Voices on Climate Change: A Study of Women’s Perceptions of Climate Change in East Asia and the Pacific.


---
**Page 220**
---

collection, it was discovered that — bulk of post-disaster resource allocation decisions were made by men at the household and community level: women were concerned about receiving correct and timely information from men as forecast and disaster warnings were not often relayed from the public to the private sector. Women also felt that they lacked access to decision-making processes in general, and, women were generally segregated from decision-making councils, community meetings etc.

The Programme will minimise the risk of increasing gender-based violence (GBV) through adopting a gender-sensitive and responsive approach to EWS. Based on awareness and understanding of the specific needs and vulnerabilities of different groups of women, men, boys and girls, and the roles of the EWS on group genders, the Programme will ensure that disaster preparedness, response and contingency planning are tailored to meet the diverse needs and capacities of different gender groups. To this end, the Programme will follow Good Practices as defined by the EU-funded Global Crisis Response Support Programme. For the four key elements of EWS:

* **Risk Knowledge:** risk assessments will include core questions on what gender-related factors influence specific groups of women, men, girls and boys at greater risk during different types of crises.
    * Monitoring and Warning Service: The programme will assess which specific groups of women, men, boys and girls have access to different types of observational information and how each group can be leveraged in the information to forecast or detect early warning signs of impending crises.
    * Dissemination and Communication: The Programme will assess gender and age differences related to communication of warnings and develop a strategy that takes these into account.
    * Response Capability: The Programme will consider women and children as potential change agents, not just as vulnerable groups. Planning will take this factor into account and also ask women at the community level what factors need to be addressed to ensure that women are also taken care of during different types of crisis and in the post-crisis recovery period.

### 4.2 Gear Products towards End Users in Climate-Vulnerable Sectors

A World Bank report** finds that of the 20 countries with the highest average annual disaster losses stated (in US$ billion) between 1985-2013, 17 are located in the Pacific region, including blue-ranked (4th), the Marshall Islands (ranked 14th), and the Cook Islands (ranked 16th). Palau, in this analysis, is ranked a close 22nd. The extent of exposure, another CGIAR report** highlights, to these impacts and increasing climate variability has led to increased demand for information on weather patterns and information available to a community, frequency and intensity of shocks/impacts that determine the vulnerability of communities, and the ability of communities to adapt over time. The reports reiterate the importance of climate information services for the Pacific island countries where climate change can be expected to have significant impacts. This is because expected and unpredictable variations in climatic patterns, climate-induced disasters and hazards, and unpredicted climate change risks will create different exposure patterns among communities. These findings are consistent with the results from a recent study that sought to disentangle the relations of power and patronage in five Pacific SIDS. This will build on the stakeholder consultation and mapping exercises conducted in each island, which revealed that


---
**Page 221**
---

communities are aware of, and have some auto-adaptation methods for coping, towards climate events.

In Cook Islands, outer island consultations highlighted the necessity to expand forecasting capabilities (Pa Erutu), with cross-checking capacities from local observations. In this context, Au Vaine can be used as a pilot project to demonstrate how forecast information can be disseminated using funds and disperses tropical cyclone warnings to communities. With Programme support, Au Vaine can be expanded to other outer islands. The programme will also provide training for key stakeholders who play a similar role in community engagement and will be supported to ensure community pre-occupation with climate change issues. In Palau, the programme will work closely with existing island disaster preparation and recovery committees, the Palau Red Cross Society, and Center for Women's Empowerment Belau: are key stakeholders to both deeper identify nuances of what communities need in terms of forecast information and how best to disseminate it. The Bureau of Meteorology (BOM) and SPREP will also coordinate and collaborate with the Palau Weather Service to include traditional weather forecasting practices into their climate services. In Tuvalu, the programme will work with Marshall Islands Red Cross Society and Women United Together Marshall Islands will perform similar roles as in Palau. BOM and SPREP are also expected to instigate work on collecting and capitalizing on existing forecast information systems. The programme will also support the ongoing collaboration of the RMI Weather Service Office. In Tuvalu, the Red Cross has been identified (as in Palau) as a key stakeholder for climate services.

In order to address differential exposure patterns, climate service products, such as impact-based forecasting (analysing and disseminating implications of the forecast and specific uses of the forecast information), are being developed. These include, but not limited to, early warning systems (based remote sensing systems, radars, wind profilers) etc., have to geared towards end users, particularly those with low exposure in sectors such as agriculture, fishing, pastoralism.

In the Programme’s second phase, consultations will be conducted to further consolidate insights into end user-relevant climate information services. In Cook Islands (where the economy is driven by tourism), the focus of these consultations will be on the development of a decision support system for tailored forecasting and information (such as agro-climate bulletin) for end users in each sector. Similarly, in Niue, consultations focused on the requirement of demand-driven climate products, interpretation of forecast information, and dissemination mechanisms. In Palau, the focus was on RMIs consolidated adaptation portal with a user-friendly interface that collates relevant climate information services. In Tuvalu, the programme will work to identify key stakeholders and sectors as requested. Lastly, in Palau and Tuvalu, funding requests prioritized national dashboards to allow for downscaled forecast data and analysis in line with user requirements, and disseminate oceanic climate content development at the local level.

At the start of Programme implementation, a deep dive into these identified community contexts in collaboration with key stakeholders will be conducted, which will improve understanding of the current state of climate services. This will also allow for the identification of gaps between responsive climate information products and early warning services for greater end-to-end value and efficiency of climate services. The programme will work to ensure that these gaps are addressed.

These processes, in the context of the Pacific island countries, have to specifically target women because they are often excluded from decision-making spheres, and lack access to information and communication technologies. This is particularly important as women play a pivotal role in the climate content development at the local level.

The case of agriculture in the Pacific Islands, for example, demonstrates how women play pivotal roles in the implementation of the Development of Sustainable Agriculture in the Pacific (DSPAP), a EU-funded SPC project. It highlights how using a gender-responsive participatory consultative approach for project design recognized women’s participation in the agricultural sector in seventeen


---
**Page 222**
---

Pacific islands. Further, the successes of gender engagement of the project reveal how women are able to adopt long-term adaptation strategies (through active participation and inclusion in priority-setting and decision-making) in the project's intervention sites through capacity building and strategic extension services.

DSAP’s methodology recognized and capitalized on the fact that women are generally more active in subsistence agriculture in Pacific societies because: they are the food counters often predominate cash crop production; they are responsible for most of the household chores, including training or, migrate to urban/urban areas of the islands/islands as they are culturally to attain the breadwinner role; and they are the primary caregivers within the household. Women also have a greater understanding of the surrounding environment and can make decisions about how to use resources within the household and have to shoulder important farm management decisions and actual labour – making them key users of agro-climate information services, impact forecasting and hydro-meteorological data. The project’s Gender Action Plan (GAP) recognizes this.

DSAP project in recognition of the need to leverage the capacities of women as key actors in food security and climate change adaptation, and given that the methodologies presented above will be conducted in a dedicated Gender Sector within the climate sector action for climate adaptation plan (CSACP) workshops.


### 4.3 Utilize Community Information Channels and Local Knowledge

A complementary approach to creating user-friendly and gender-responsive climate service products is sourcing local knowledge, artisanal practices and traditional know-how to inform these outputs.

Despite the vulnerability of Pacific islands to disaster and climate change variability, research shows that many communities have developed a range of strategies for adapting to changing conditions surrounding environments to mitigate impacts and risks for generations. Through a variety of traditional systems, communities in the Pacific Islands have managed climate and disaster risks over generations. Pacific communities have managed climate and disaster loss burdens. These methods include both community-based practices: traditional systems of exchange/warning, management of natural resources, water harvesting, food preservation, housing construction, etc.

Conducting mapping exercises, informed by participant observation and consultation approach, will be key to identifying the most appropriate channels for delivering climate services work, identifying community engagement and decision-making groups; and, investing in gender-relevant or women’s groups. These groups can provide valuable information on how to best deliver climate services to different sectors; encourage active participation and involve them as information producers; and, draw out valuable inputs for the design of climate information products.

Youth are also a key group that is often overlooked when considering adaptation in the Pacific islands. Women are often the primary caregivers within this household structure; therefore, this assessment prioritises gender stakeholders. However, the programme includes specific interventions targeted at young people. Young women and youth are considered as key change agents (see Box 3.2 on youth engagement with community elders to integrate traditional knowledge in EWS (sub-activity 3.1.3). The programme also seeks to increase awareness of climate change, increasing participation, and expanding the roles played by these specific vulnerable groups.

A CGIAR report finds that inclusion of traditional methods and gender-responsive design in communication strategies is critical for successful adaptation efforts. Women are key actors in community-based disaster risk reduction (CBDRR) initiatives, including in raising public awareness or accessibility, as these utilise strong social networks that have a historical continuity within the community and provide a familiar means of communication. Adaptation to technological changes is less linear; hence, these traditional methods can be easily hybridized with modern


---
**Page 223**
---

technology such as (where available) radio, television and short messaging service (SMS) through telecom networks to ensure impact forecasting products and other relevant climate content are disseminated effectively. Feedback loops, by which community members, specifically women in vulnerable communities, can provide feedback on how the information is received and used within their particular context, are also necessary. By introducing new technologies into traditional networks of communication, such as mobile phones or radios, such as field workers from met services, may be able to extend the reach of their climate information services and help more vulnerable communities to adapt to environmental changes.

### 4.4 Create Opportunities for Women to Overcome Gendered Barriers

Section 3.2 presents different statistics related to poverty and hardship, labor education, and health in the Pacific Region. This section provides a summary of the findings from the baseline survey of this project. To further the analyses, it is important to consider the intra-household dynamics that are at play within the household. In the context of the Pacific Small Island Developing States (SIDS) Regionally, it is commonplace for women to participate in the bulk of domestic care and reproductive work, which often results in a lack of time available for other activities such as education or work for seasonal work. Thus, it can be summed that gendered time poverty in the Pacific Islands is high.

Bandari and Wodon define an individual as “time poor” if he/she is working long hours while being simultaneously unable to meet his/her basic needs (e.g., food, shelter, clothing). The term refers to working hours below a given time poverty line. Thus, time poverty results from the combination of two conditions: firstly, the individual does not have enough time for rest and leisure once all working hours are accounted for; secondly, the individual cannot reduce his/her working time without losing income or consumption (e.g., by reducing the number of children in school or leading her/himself to fall into monetary poverty due to the loss in income or consumption associated with the reduction in working time if the household is not originally poor).

An additional factor that must be considered when analyzing women’s access to and use of the current time usage pattern within the household to explore other economic activities, or as in the case of this project, for autonomous or technical adaptation options towards climate resilience. For the project, it will be important to consider the following: (1) the importance of gender equality; (2) an important factor to be considered in designing pilot activities, technical or maintenance capacity building, and other interventions is the time poverty experienced by women.

In addition to facing the time poverty-related risks, GBV is pervasive as a serious epidemic in the Pacific region. Available figures suggest that between 40 – 70% of women are subjected to violence from intimate partners, and 15-20% experience sexual violence during their lifetime. Studies show that about 37% of the women in the five beneficiary countries face GBV during their lifetime. Despite long-standing aid programming to target and community policing to address GBV rates remain high. The reasons for this are complex but include: lack of awareness, low levels of patriarchy, high incidence of alcoholism among men in certain areas; and, lack of redressal and grievance mechanisms (or lack of expression) to report on norms infringing dignity, stigma and shame).

The International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies (IFRC) has conducted in-depth research into its disaster regions and national societies. It found that GBV is a constant theme in post-disaster contexts. Given the negative shocks, loss of family members,

livelihoods and homes, domestic conflict levels tend to rise leading to greater incidence of GBV,

Research19 undertaken by the International Union for the Conservation of Nature (IUCN) with USAID has revealed a significant number of linkages between environmental and climate-related stresses on increased GBV, which is also detrimental to the well-being of survivors as well as an impediment for
climate change adaptation and resilience.


19 Research connecting environmental and climate-related stressors to GBV is recent. AGENT - Advancing Gender in the Environment (USAID-IUCN partnership) is at the forefront of cataloging, analyzing and identifying these stressors in different contexts

### Cook Islands

CEDAW,
United Nations General Assembly (UNGA)
2006 - Cook Islands ratified CEDAW.

The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women is an international human rights treaty adopted by the United Nations in 1979. It sets out to ensure women's enjoyment of all civil, cultural, economic, political and social rights. The convention has been ratified by 189 states.

In ratifying CEDAW, the Cook Islands committed to undertake all appropriate measures, without delay, with a view to achieving the full advancement of women. Since its adoption in 1979 by the United Nations General Assembly (UNGA), it has been ratified by 189 states.

National Gender Policy
2016 - The Cook Islands National Policy on Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment and Strategic Plan of Action (drafted by the Ministry of Finance and Economic Management), recognises that women and men are equal partners in development. It also recognises that gender equality is at the heart of economic and social progress, giving equal value to the roles and responsibilities of both sexes.

It also recognises that in order to redress gender it is necessary to create the conditions for women's empowerment while women and men work together to achieve this goal.

Governmental Mechanisms
1979 - The Government of Cook Islands established the Women's Desk (the Women in Development Division, since renamed GADDD) within the Ministry of Finance and Economic Management. This was the first national-level gender equality mechanism.
2011 – 2016 The Cook Islands National Policy on Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment includes a strategic action plan with clear policy direction, prioritisation and commitment to allocate resources for gender equality, but with

## II. Niue

CEDAW, UNDCP

New Zealand ratified CEDAW in 1985 and through this process New Zealand has accepted the convention; however, Niue has not yet ratified CEDAW on its own right.

In 2001, it was recommended that Niue undergo a CEDAW compliance assessment. The report noted that Niue had made significant progress towards achieving a focus on legislative compliance and non-discrimination. The report was not completed but the draft highlighted shortcomings in existing legislation.

However, the Government of New Zealand, in collaboration with Niue, has increased its efforts to ensure that Niue is able to implement CEDAW protocols and articles, such as for the Armed Forces and law enforcement.

### Gender Policies

One of the strategies under the implementation of the 2009-2013 plan is to encourage increased participation of women in the development of Niue. The Government of New Zealand has provided funding through the NOW (Niue Council of Women) to promote the increased participation of women and support Niue to meet its obligations under CEDAW.

### Governmental Mechanisms

The Niue Council of Women (NOW) has historically been the civil society organization responsible for promoting gender equality in Niue. The NOW is a non-governmental organization that operates at a local level. Until 2010, the NOW was the sole implementing agency for government grant funds for women. These grant funds are the only government mechanism specifically targeted towards women.

A stock take of the Government's gender mainstreaming capacity undertaken by the SPC (2015) found that while the government had a draft national policy on gender equality and a draft national action plan, there were limited resources as well as limited resources and technical capacity to address the same.


## III. Palau

CEDAW, UNDCP

Palau is one of only seven countries in world that is not signatory to CEDAW.

### National Policies

The Palau Constitution and Title I of the Palauan National Code (PCN) guarantees women equality under the law.

### Republic of Marshall Islands (RMI)

#### CEDAW
CEDAW was adopted by the General Assembly in 1979 and has been ratified by RMI.

#### National Gender Mainstreaming Policy Action Plan

The purpose of the National Gender Mainstreaming Policy is to guide the implementation of the policy, taking into account the needs, priorities and aspirations of all women and men, working towards effectively eliminating all forms of discrimination and inequality.


---
**Page 228**
---

It concerns private and public sectors, local governments, and communities. It requires the mainstreaming of a gender perspective across all government policies, strategies, programs and services.

### Governmental Mechanisms

The Ministry of Internal Affairs is the official gender focal point of the government. The Gender and Development Division within this ministry provides technical assistance to the various levels of government to ensure that gender inequality and women's human rights issues are properly addressed.

The Gender Development Unit coordinates the government’s efforts in addressing these issues. The Office supports the monitoring and reporting process under the National Gender Mainstreaming Policy and other regional initiatives, including promoting gender equality, and identifies areas needing further action.


## V. Tuvalu

CEDAW United Nations General Assembly (UNGA) 1999 – Tuvalu ratified the CEDAW and is working towards embedding CEDAW articles into the constitution, legislative and statutory reforms, and policy development in line with the National Gender Policy. The CEDAW review report also being prepared for presentation to the UNGA.

National Gender Policy 2014 - the Tuvalu National Gender Policy enacted this year aims to “achieve the equality of men and women by addressing gender inequalities across all aspects of decision making and to ensure progressive realisation of women’s human rights.”

The Strategic Plan of Action (2014 – 2016) outlined a series of outputs and key actions in order to achieve the key outcomes of the Policy. The National Gender Policy aims to provide the guiding framework to operationalise Government commitments towards gender equality and women's empowerment in Tuvalu. The Policy outlines the specific areas that the government will focus on within its development agenda, including partnerships with civil society groups and partnerships towards the overarching goal of gender equality and the empowerment of women.

### Governmental Mechanisms

Tuvalu’s main gender institution is the Department of Women Affairs, which has been renamed to the Gender Affairs Department, to reflect an increased emphasis and a broader focus on gender equality and women's empowerment.

# Conclusion: Regional Potential & Way Forward

This Gender Assessment has identified and illustrated both explicit and implicit gender and socioeconomic issues that need to be addressed within the Programme Results. The findings from the Assessment also form the basis for the Gender Action Plan (Section 7), which will specify this proposal's gender strategy, objectives, indicators, timelines, responsible parties, and budget allocations, through the results framework.

This assessment is based on gender analysis of literature and data collected through a comprehensive review of existing information as well as an inventory of key stakeholders, which will be the basis for a deep dive into gender aspects relevant for the Programme at the start of the next phase. The Gender Action Plan will be developed in close collaboration with key stakeholders who will flesh out the indicative suggestions made by this Annex and will contextualise the Results areas for greater gender impact and results. The Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) framework will also be designed to ensure that the programme is able to capture differences between gender groups, including gender differences and gaps in coping mechanisms, resources and information availability to design appropriate interventions.

As gender equality gains priority in the GCF's, SPC's, UNEP's and other agencies' portfolios, this Programme partakes in the international conversation on gender mainstreaming and gender-responsive programming. The Programme will also contribute to the development of gender information services. If implemented effectively, this Programme has the potential to become a good practice model for the GCF and guide for future interventions in the Cook Islands, Tuvalu, Palau Republic of Marshall Islands, and Tuvalu (nationally), other Pacific Ocean island nations (regionally), as well as amongst other SIDS.


---
**Page 230**
---

7. **Gender Action Plan**

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) forms the basis for operationalizing the results and recommendations of the Gender Assessment presented in the above sections. It contains specific gender-responsive elements to be considered in the Programme design and during the implementation of Programme activities, in order to maximize the development impact and co-benefits of the GCF investment.

The GAP is closely aligned to the outputs of the Programme logical framework and proposed activities. The GAP complements the Environment and Social Safeguards Framework (ESF), which identifies the Programme to be categorized as Risk Category C. This does present certain gender-related risks and the Programme has initiated mitigation of these by conducting the Gender Assessment and developing the Action Plan.

### In-country deep dive study

At the start of Programme implementation, an in-depth study on gender and community stakeholders will be conducted in partnership with the Social Development Programme (Gender, Culture & Youth) of the Pacific Community (SPC). This will include: i) an assessment / analysis of women’s access and control over resources; ii) a review of existing laws, policies, and institutional frameworks that impact women’s livelihoods and other socio-economic factors (including fishing, palm farming, tourism, etc.); and iii) an analysis of the opportunities, challenges and barriers for women-headed households, indigenous groups, youth, the elderly and other disadvantaged groups.

The objectives of the study are outlined below:

- Identify opportunities to strengthen the enabling environment for gender mainstreaming in government implementing partner agencies (institutions);
- Explore the existing gaps in terms of women’s access to community sites in each of the five Pacific countries, their access to and control over resources, including gendered opportunities, barriers, and challenges;
- Identify existing women's groups and community networks that could support the active engagement of women and other vulnerable groups in community decision-making processes; and
- Recommend concrete actions to support meaningful participation of women in Programme activities;

Study and document traditional knowledge of early warning systems and climate adaptation;

Using a gender analysis framework, explore how the overall Programme can contribute to the poverty reduction in the community, empowerment, increased awareness of rights and social justice, meaningful participation in governance mechanisms and education;

Through participatory approaches, identify and implement measures to ensure gender-responsive measures are incorporated throughout the Programme, including within the Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) framework.

The study will inform the gender-responsive design of Early Warning Systems (EWS) organisational and decision-making processes – to be delivered under sub-activity 3.1.1


---
**Page 231**
---

# Provision of gender expertise within the Programme

Throughout the Programme, gender expertise will be provided in cooperation with UNEP’s Gender Unit and relevant Regional Technical Partners. In particular, the Programme will partner with the Pacific Community (SPC) and its Social Development Programme, which has a strong track record in the region on gender equality and women's empowerment, including in policy development, capacity building, advocacy, and provision. The Programme Steering Committee will engage gender expertise and include specific discussions on the gender dimension of all activities of the Programme. The National Coordination Committees in the five countries will also source and engage local gender expertise, in collaboration with SPC and others.

## Monitoring, evaluation and reporting

The Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Plan (Annex 11) will be refined based on the findings from a gender phase study to design gender-specific indicators and collect associated sex-disaggregated data that will monitor the gender-responsiveness of Programme implementation – including in achieving the GAP and Environmental Sustainability Goals. The M&E Plan will also include a Gender Monitoring Framework (GMF), which will track progress towards gender equality, as well as lessons learned from gender mainstreaming activities undertaken by the Programme. The Project Steering Committee (PSC) will include representatives from women’s organisations to facilitate that gender issues are reflected in Programme decision-making.

## Grievance Redress Mechanism

The Programme will set up a grievance redress mechanism (GRM), as outlined in Annex 6b. The GRM has been designed to be able to:

- Record, categorise and organise grievances.
- Resolve disputes through consultation with the complainant and other stakeholders;
- Inform the aggrieved parties about the solutions; and
- Forward any unresolved cases to higher authorities for resolution.

The GRM will address concerns and complaints from affected parties promptly and transparently through this process, with responsibilities and required activities outlined in Annex 6b. The GRM will be gender-responsive and readily accessible to all affected parties at no cost.

Furthermore, the Project Cooperation Agreements (PCAs) with national Executing Entities (EEs) will require that EEs put in place localised GRMs, including stakeholder consultations and national procedures relating to grievance redress. GRMs should be gender-and age-inclusive and responsive, and address potential barriers to access for women and other vulnerable groups. The GRM will also maintain a flexible approach with respect to receiving grievances in light of identified local constraints to communications and access to resources for some stakeholders. To facilitate communication between the GRM and potential claimants, the GRM will seek support from local government, civil

# RESULT 1: STRENGTHENED DELIVERY MODEL FOR CLIMATE INFORMATION SERVICES AND MHWS COVERING OCEANS AND ISLANDS

Programme Result 1 will assist each country to establish a coherent National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen collaboration among national institutions for enhanced climate information services and products, supported by effective coordination mechanisms. The NFCS will be developed in close consultation with key stakeholders including government agencies, civil society organisations and women's groups. All individuals and communities participating in the Programme activities will be made aware of the GRM and the means to access it.

## ACTIVITY: 1.1: Strengthen institutional and policy frameworks and delivery models for climate services

### SUB-ACTIVITIES

The Programme will include gender-relevant inputs from Consultation Workshops for the NFCS through the analysis of cross-cutting gender impacts from key sectors in a dedicated Gender Session. The NFCS will also include gender-responsive policy elements and legislative frameworks, in alignment with WMO guidelines. The NFCS will also include gender-sensitive performance standards (e.g., employment of women).

### Gender-responsive protocols and service delivery practices, and human resource management practices

#### 1.1.2. Conduct market assessment to explore viable opportunities for providing climate information services in sectors and business segments
Market assessments will be used by the Programme to understand the sectoral needs of different climate-vulnerable segments. Particular focus will be given to subsistence agriculture and other sectors where women are key players, and where gender equality is a critical issue related to food security and nutrition security. Women's organisations will be leveraged in this context to ensure that marginalised voices are heard and provisioned for.

#### 1.1.3. Mainstream climate change knowledge into sectors
The Programme will analyse cross-cutting gender impacts in key sectors in a dedicated Gender Session within the climate sector action and communication plan (CSAP). Consultation workshops, CSACPs will be drafted with gender-disaggregated indicators and gender indicators for monitoring and evaluation.

#### 1.1.4. Develop national policies for financing climate services
The Programme will provide guidance for gender-responsive policy elements aligned with WMO guidelines to incorporate gender considerations into the financial policies of the five countries. This will include a focus on gender equality in financing, training, and capacity building for gender mainstreaming into climate services.

### RESULT 2: STRENGTHENED OBSERVATIONS, MONITORING, MODELLING AND PREDICTION OF CLIMATE AND ITS IMPACTS ON OCEAN AREAS AND ISLANDS
Programme Result 2 will enable the five countries to expand their observation networks with robust and well-supported equipment, collecting data from critical sites such as distant inhabited outer islands and from ocean areas. Enhancing observation and monitoring network will build the foundation for


---
**Page 234**
---

full compliance of the five countries with the requirements of the VAMO Global Basic Observing Network (GBON), which "represents a new approach in which basic science-based observations are designed, defined and monitored at the global level" and emphasises the need for access to 24/7 global observations as a global public good. Installation and capacity building for weather radar observations will further enhance extreme weather monitoring and early warning systems. The GBON is not only a network of observing stations but also a system of services. This Programme will not only benefit the host countries but also the entire global forecasting capability. One of the most significant activities in this Programme will be to establish end-to-end ocean services. This is a major priority for Pacific NMHSs, in response to user demand for ocean climate information, particularly in relation to fisheries and aquaculture. The GBON will provide a framework for the development of ocean observing systems in small isolated islands, but the small economies of the Programme countries render most modern equipment and consumables infeasible to obtain.

This Result will support the implementation of the Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS) helping to address deficiencies in the Observation and Monitoring pillar. The GBON is one of several activities that will be implemented under the Pacific Roadmap for Strengthened Climate Services 2017-2026, which proposes several regional and national actions within the GFCS pillars.

RESULT 3: IMPROVED COMMUNITY PREPAREDNESS, RESPONSE CAPABILITIES AND RESILIENCE TO CLIMATE RISKS

Programme Result 3 contributes to the attainment of the overall Goal of the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction. "The substantial reduction of disaster risk and losses in lives, livelihoods and health and in the economic, physical, social, cultural and environmental assets of persons, businesses, communities and countries". In particular, the Programme will contribute to the Framework's Global Target (g) "Substantially increase the availability of and access to early warning systems and information for all".

The Programme will also contribute to the outcomes of the 2019 UN Climate Action Summit, in particular targets 3 and 4 of the Risk-informed Early Action Partnership (REAP) on investment in 'early warning system infrastructure and institutions' to target early action in "last first mile" communities and "more resilient communities".

Furthermore, this Result will introduce Forecast-based Financing (FbF) in the five countries as an innovative mechanism whereby early actions at community and government level are pre-planned based on credible forecasts, and are funded and implemented before a climate shock to minimise losses and damages caused by climate-related hazards and reduce the need for humanitarian assistance in their aftermath.

RESULT 4: ENHANCED REGIONAL KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT AND COOPERATION FOR CLIMATE SERVICES AND MHWS
Programme Result 4 will optimise synergies among the Cook Islands, Niue, Palau, the Republic of the Marshall Islands and Tuvalu. Benefits of the multi-country approach include:
i) facilitation of strategic partnerships; stronger relationship and collaboration;
ii) cost efficiency; ease of planning and coordination across the region;
iii) enhanced capacity building for climate services in the Pacific region;
iv) improved access to climate information products, tools and services across the region;
v) South-South cooperation modalities enhancing the multiplier effects of the Programme. To this end, the Programme will organise joint learning events and training; promote equipment, software and tools common to all five countries; and foster networking and mentoring among the five MHWS.
The Pacific Climate Change Centre (PCCC), established in 2015 by the World Meteorological Organisation (WMO) with support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), will provide technical assistance for climate services, including Instrumentation and Observation and Climate Services – reported by Pacific NHMSs to WMO and in a study conducted by the Disaster Resilience for Pacific Climate Change Centre.
This regional approach will ensure optimal knowledge management by sharing lessons learned and information on best practices. Programme Result 4 will complement and build on existing mechanisms for the entire Pacific region such as the newly established Pacific Climate Change Centre (PCCC), the Pacific Meteorological Council (PMC), the Regional Climate Outlook Forum, WMO regional meteorological centres and training and regional learning institutions such as the University of the South Pacific (USP) and its Pacific Centre for Environment and Sustainable Development (PACE-SD).
